Overall winner: This lecture examined nested and subqueries in SQL, focusing on their structure, usage across different clauses, and their relationship to query monotonicity and optimization. Building on earlier SQL operations—projections, selections, joins, grouping, and aggregation—it introduced subqueries as queries embedded within other queries. These can appear in the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses and may return either scalar values or full relations. Although powerful, nested queries are typically less efficient; thus, subquery unnesting, or rewriting them into equivalent join queries, is encouraged when possible.

Subqueries in the **SELECT** clause were illustrated with examples involving movies and actors: retrieving the genre of each actor’s movie, computing the number of actors per movie, or calculating the average salary of actors in highly rated films. These correlated subqueries depend on outer query variables and can often be replaced with equivalent join and GROUP BY queries, improving performance. However, the slides also showed that apparent alternatives are not always equivalent—particularly when outer joins (LEFT OUTER JOIN) are introduced—highlighting the need for care in comparing differently structured queries.

Subqueries in the **FROM** clause act as temporary tables or inline views. Using constructs such as `WITH` (common table expressions), one can refactor queries to improve readability while maintaining equivalent logic. The lecture demonstrated converting a subquery that filters movies by rating range into an unnested form with a standard WHERE condition, establishing that these two are equivalent.

Subqueries in the **WHERE** clause expressed logical quantification. **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) were represented using `EXISTS` and `IN`, enabling tests for presence or membership, such as finding actors who appeared in Sci‑Fi films. These queries can be rewritten as joins. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) were addressed next and presented greater difficulty, as SQL lacks built‑in universal operators. Examples included finding actors who only acted in action movies and movies whose actors all earned above $100K. Slides showed two equivalent formulations for the latter—using `$100K < ALL (...)` and `NOT EXISTS (...)`—and noted that the first is not supported in SQLite.

The lecture concluded by introducing **monotone and non‑monotone queries**. Monotone queries (those without negation, aggregation, or universal quantifiers) do not lose results when data is added and can usually be unnested. In contrast, non‑monotone queries—those involving negation, universals, or aggregates—cannot be safely unnested, linking logical expressiveness with practical optimization limits.

*(Approx. 398 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined how race and culture are socially constructed ideas that shape law, identity, and schooling. It began with reflection on labeling—how teachers or institutions may categorize students in ways that influence motivation and self-perception. The slides stressed that silence or denial around race or cultural difference are common but inadequate; instead, the answer is courage—the courage to confront how social conditioning and upbringing shape our perspectives on difference.

Race, the lecture emphasized, has no biological basis. Across history, political struggles and economic interests have redefined racial categories, making “race” appear as common sense despite being a social invention. The American Anthropological Association’s *RACE: Are We So Different?* project underscores how scientific evidence dispels myths about racial difference. Legal scholar Ian Haney López’s work illustrated that the law does not merely reflect racial realities—it constructs them. Through “law as coercion,” legal rules have historically controlled marriage, immigration, and citizenship, thereby shaping who could access rights and resources identified as “white.” Cases involving Armenians, Japanese, and Mexicans revealed the instability and prejudice embedded in such classifications. The 1967 Supreme Court ruling against bans on interracial marriage marked a turning point against explicitly racialized legal practices. López also distinguished “law as ideology,” showing that laws and institutions normalize racial hierarchies by making them appear natural and race-neutral, a process echoed in criminal justice and education systems. Together, these mechanisms demonstrate how race operates as both enforced policy and internalized belief. James Baldwin’s observation that “no one was white before coming to America” captured how whiteness emerged through coercive privilege, a process further reinforced by shifting U.S. Census categories and evolving racial language.

The second half focused on culture, drawing on Sonia Nieto’s definition of it as a complex, dynamic human creation encompassing values, traditions, relationships, and worldviews. Culture is learned, continually reshaped, and multifaceted—formed through family, peers, class, religion, geography, and media. Everyone participates in multiple, hybrid cultures. Schools themselves embody distinct cultural systems in routines, hierarchies, and beliefs about intelligence and ability. These assumptions—such as viewing ability as fixed or ranking skills in set hierarchies—deeply influence students’ engagement, sense of belonging, and future expectations. Overall, the lecture linked the social construction of race and culture to how educational structures both reflect and reproduce broader societal meanings.

(≈395 words)Overall winner: This lecture explored **nested and subqueries in SQL**, focusing on how they occur, how they can be logically rewritten through *unnesting*, and how monotonicity determines whether such transformations are possible. Building on prior SQL features—projections, selections, joins, aggregation, and grouping—it introduced **subqueries** as queries embedded within others. They may appear in a **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clause and can return either a single value or a relation. Subqueries are essential when a condition depends on data that must itself be computed.

The lecture first examined **subqueries in the SELECT clause**, such as retrieving each actor’s movie genre or computing the average salary or number of actors per movie. These examples showed that correlated subqueries can often be rewritten as *joins* with grouping or aggregation, a process called **subquery unnesting**. The slides emphasized conceptual *equivalence*—verifying that alternative rewrites return identical results—rather than just performance concerns. Some queries even required **outer joins** to preserve tuples from one side, illustrating that logical equivalence depends on query semantics, not simply syntax.

Next, **subqueries in the FROM clause** were discussed. These can define intermediate results or *derived tables*, allowing a sequence of filtering steps such as selecting movies with ratings between 8 and 9. Equivalent statements can be written using common table expressions (CTEs) for readability. **Subqueries in the WHERE clause** introduced the use of **existential quantifiers**, expressed through `EXISTS` or `IN`, which test whether related tuples exist. These conditions were shown to be interchangeable with join formulations that achieve the same logical result.

The lecture then introduced **universal quantifiers** (“for all”), representing conditions such as actors who only act in Action movies or movies where all actors earn above \$100K. Because SQL lacks a direct universal operator, these are implemented through negation—using `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS`—making them more complex and logically distinct. The rewriting of `$100K < ALL (...)` as a `NOT EXISTS` query illustrated this transformation.

Finally, the concept of **monotone and non-monotone queries** explained why some queries can be unnested while others cannot. A **monotone query** never loses results when more tuples are added—typical for positive SELECT-FROM-WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates—while queries containing **negation**, **universal quantifiers**, or **aggregations** are **non-monotone**. Slide examples demonstrated that adding tuples to a non-monotone query (e.g., those using “NOT IN”) may cause previously valid results to disappear. The lecture concluded that only monotone queries can be safely transformed or unnested without changing their meaning.  

*(395 words)*Overall winner: This lecture covered **nested and subqueries in SQL**, emphasizing how they appear in different clauses, when they can be rewritten through **unnesting**, and how **monotonicity** determines whether such rewrites preserve meaning. Building on prior SQL topics—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and data modification—the lecture defined a **subquery** as a query embedded within another. Subqueries may occur in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and are used when a query condition depends on computed results.

The lecture first examined **subqueries in the SELECT clause**, illustrated by finding each actor’s movie genre or computing average salaries and actor counts per movie. These examples introduced **correlated subqueries**, whose results depend on the outer query. The slides cautioned that nested subqueries can be inefficient—“whenever possible, don’t use nested queries”—and showed how many of them can be transformed into equivalent **join** or **group-by** queries through **subquery unnesting**. This rewriting was presented as both an optimization and a way to test **logical equivalence**, including cases requiring **outer joins** to preserve unmatched records.

Next, **subqueries in the FROM clause** were shown to produce *derived tables* or temporary views that can simplify multi‑step filtering. The example retrieving movies rated between 8 and 9 demonstrated how a subquery in the FROM clause—or an equivalent **common table expression (CTE)**—supports modular query construction.  

**Subqueries in the WHERE clause** introduced **existential quantifiers**, expressed through `EXISTS` and `IN`, meaning that some related tuples satisfy a condition. Examples compared these forms and their **join‑based equivalents**, demonstrating their interchangeability. The lecture then extended these ideas to **universal quantifiers** (“for all”), such as finding actors who only acted in Action movies or movies whose actors all earn more than \$100K. Universal quantification was implemented using **negation**, e.g., with `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS`, and the slides presented a two‑step method:  
(1) identify entities violating the “for all” condition (e.g., actors in non‑Action films), and  
(2) return all others that do not appear in that violating set.  

Finally, the discussion of **monotone versus non‑monotone queries** explained why some queries can be unnested while others cannot. A **monotone query** (a positive SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE without subqueries or aggregates) cannot lose results when data is added, so it can be safely unnested. Queries with **negation**, **universal quantifiers**, or **aggregates** are **non‑monotone**, meaning additional tuples can remove previous results. Only monotone queries allow equivalent unnesting without affecting correctness.

*(398 words)*Overall winner: This lecture focused on **nested and subqueries in SQL**, explaining where they appear, how they can sometimes be rewritten through **unnesting**, and how **monotonicity** determines whether such rewriting preserves results. Building on earlier SQL concepts—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and modifications—the lecture defined a **subquery** (or nested query) as a query embedded within another. Subqueries may occur in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and are useful when a condition depends on computed or intermediate results.

The first section examined **subqueries in the SELECT clause**, such as finding each actor’s movie genre or calculating the average salary or number of actors per movie. These examples introduced **correlated subqueries**, whose inner query depends on the outer one. The instruction emphasized that nested queries should be avoided when possible—though sometimes required—and illustrated how many can be replaced by equivalent **join** or **group‑by** statements through **subquery unnesting**. Several slides used the equality symbol (“=”) to show when a nested and unnested query produce identical results, including cases where **outer joins** are needed to preserve unmatched tuples when combining data across tables.

Next, **subqueries in the FROM clause** were shown to produce **derived tables** or temporary relations, facilitating step‑by‑step filtering. The example that selected movies with ratings between 8 and 9 showed both a subquery in FROM and an equivalent **common table expression (CTE)** using `WITH`, illustrating **subquery refactoring** and simplifying query modularization.

In **subqueries within WHERE**, the lecture introduced **existential quantifiers**, implemented using `EXISTS` or `IN`, meaning a related tuple exists that satisfies a condition. Equivalent **join forms** were shown to demonstrate their logical interchangeability. The topic then extended to **universal quantifiers** (“for all”), used to identify actors who only acted in Action movies or movies where all actors earned more than $100K. These queries relied on negation, using `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS`, in a two‑step pattern: first find entities violating the requirement and then exclude them. Slides also noted that the `$100K < ALL` syntax for universal checks is **not supported in SQLite**, showing practical system constraints.

A query is **monotone** if adding data never removes results—typical of positive SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates—allowing safe unnesting. Queries with **negation**, **universal quantifiers**, or **aggregates** are **non‑monotone**; their results can shrink when data grows, meaning they cannot generally be unnested.

*(390 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined nested SQL queries—known as subqueries—their types, roles in query construction, and how their logical properties affect query optimization. Building on earlier SQL fundamentals such as projection, selection, joins, grouping, and aggregation, it introduced subqueries as queries embedded within others. Subqueries can appear in the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses, returning either single values or entire relations. They are powerful for expressing conditions that depend on computed tables, but the slides emphasized a critical rule of thumb: avoid nested queries when possible. When the same result can be expressed through standard joins or aggregations, “subquery unnesting” is preferred for clarity and performance.

Subqueries in the SELECT clause were shown through examples computing related attributes, such as the genre of each actor’s movie, the number of actors in a film, or the average salary of actors in highly rated movies. Equivalent join-based queries illustrated how unnesting often yields the same results, though the lecture noted that equivalence depends on whether outer joins or grouping are used. Subqueries in the FROM clause function as derived tables or common table expressions (CTEs), enabling intermediate filtering steps like “movies with rating >8 and <9.” This illustrates subquery refactoring—building clarity without correlation.

When used in the WHERE clause, subqueries express logical quantifiers. The slides introduced *existential quantifiers*—checked via **EXISTS** or **IN**—to capture “there exists” or “for some” conditions, such as finding actors who appeared in Sci‑Fi films. These can typically be replaced by equivalent join queries, demonstrating that existential logic aligns with join semantics. In contrast, *universal quantifiers* (e.g., “for all”) are much harder to express. Examples showed multi-step methods for retrieving actors who acted only in Action films or finding all movies whose actors earned more than $100K, using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS** to simulate the “for every” logic.

Finally, the lecture introduced the theory of *monotone* versus *non‑monotone* queries. A query is monotone if adding tuples to input relations never removes results. Simple SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone and can safely be unnested. However, queries involving universal quantifiers, negation, or aggregation are **non‑monotone**, meaning they can lose outputs as data grows. This distinction determines whether nested subqueries can be transformed into equivalent unnested forms.

Building on earlier SQL features—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and grouping—it defined a **subquery** as a query embedded inside another. *(≈392 words)*Overall winner: This lecture expanded on **nested (sub)queries in SQL**, showing how they appear in different clauses, how to rewrite them effectively, and how their structure relates to **query monotonicity**. Building on prior topics—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and grouping—it defined a **subquery** as a query embedded within another and explained that it can return single values or entire relations. Slide 10 highlighted that unnesting can change results if null‑producing tuples are lost.

For instance, selecting movies with ratings > 8 and then filtering those < 9 illustrated how multi‑step filtering can be structured clearly and later unnested into a single condition.

A query is **monotone** if adding tuples to inputs never removes results—true for basic SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates. Slide 33’s theorem used **nested‑loop semantics** to prove this property: inserting a tuple into any input cannot eliminate previous outputs. Thus, monotone queries can be safely unnested. Conversely, those involving **universal quantifiers**, **negation**, or **aggregates** are **non‑monotone**, meaning unnesting may alter their results and must be applied with care.

*(≈388 words)*Overall winner: The first week of *Principles of Accounting* introduces financial accounting as “the language of business,” explaining how it records and communicates a company’s financial story through data about performance, risks, and opportunities. Accounting serves two central functions: **recordkeeping**, which involves identifying, measuring, and recording economic events in value terms, and **communication**, which provides summarized information to external decision makers through financial reports.

Users of financial information include investors evaluating profitability and condition, creditors assessing repayment ability, suppliers setting credit terms, management and boards monitoring performance and strategy, and others such as employees, customers, analysts, regulators, and competitors who use this data to make informed decisions.  

Financial accounting focuses on three categories of business activities: **financing** (transactions with investors and creditors, such as loans or stock issuance), **investing** (acquiring or disposing of assets like buildings, equipment, or inventory), and **operating** (day‑to‑day activities of producing and selling goods or services). The outcomes of these activities are reported through four core statements: the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows**.  

Financial reporting follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)**, which establish the rules for what, when, and how events are reported. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** oversees public reporting and delegates standard setting to the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**. Internationally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues **International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS)**.  

Public companies must file periodic reports with the SEC—**10‑K** (annual, audited), **10‑Q** (quarterly, unaudited), and **8‑K** (to disclose major current events). Independent **auditors** assess whether the statements comply with GAAP and are free of material misstatements, issuing one of four opinions: unqualified, unqualified with explanatory note, qualified, or adverse. Their review adds credibility for investors and regulators.

The lecture concludes by emphasizing the **balance sheet**, which reports a firm’s financial position at a specific point in time using the **accounting equation**:  
**Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity.**  
Assets represent what a company owns and expects to benefit from (e.g., cash, equipment, land, or supplies). Liabilities are what it owes (e.g., notes or accounts payable), and stockholders’ equity reflects owners’ residual interest—what remains after debts are paid. Through examples of starting a business, borrowing, buying equipment, and paying rent, the lecture illustrates how every transaction affects this equation yet keeps it in balance.

It performs two main functions: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records a firm’s transactions in value terms, and **communication**, which provides summarized information to **external decision makers** through financial reports.

Financial information serves a broad range of users. **Investors** assess management performance and financial health; **creditors** evaluate repayment capacity and loan terms; **suppliers** determine credit arrangements; **management** and **boards of directors** review operations, set strategy, and monitor performance. **Employees**, **customers**, **financial analysts**, **regulators**, and other stakeholders also rely on this information to gauge a company’s stability and reliability.

Financial reporting follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)** to ensure consistent, comparable information. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** requires publicly traded firms to file periodic financial statements and delegates standard‑setting authority to the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**. Internationally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues **International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS)**. Public companies submit an audited **10‑K** annually, unaudited **10‑Q** quarterly, and **8‑K** reports to disclose major current events.

*(Word count: 374)*Overall winner: Week 1 of *Principles of Accounting* introduces the purpose and framework of financial accounting—how information about business activities is recorded, summarized, and communicated to support decision‑making. Accounting is described as the **language of business**, a means of telling a company’s financial story—its results, risks, and resources—so users can make informed choices. It has two core functions: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records economic events in monetary terms, and **communication**, which conveys this information to external stakeholders through standardized financial statements.

The lecture explains that financial information is used by **a broad range of decision‑makers**: investors assessing management performance and financial condition, creditors evaluating repayment ability, suppliers determining credit terms, and internal users such as management and the board of directors who monitor operations and strategy. It also matters to employees considering job stability, customers evaluating reliability, and other groups such as regulators, analysts, competitors, and consumer advocates.

Students are introduced to the **financial accounting framework**, which measures three central business activities: **financing** (obtaining funds from investors and lenders), **investing** (acquiring and disposing of productive resources), and **operating** (conducting the company’s primary business). These activities generate information summarized in four **financial statements**: the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows. Together they allow users to assess both financial position and performance.

The lecture outlines the **financial reporting process and standards**. Reports are prepared according to **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)** to ensure consistent, comparable data. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** requires public companies to issue periodic filings (10‑K annual, 10‑Q quarterly, and 8‑K current reports) and delegates most standard‑setting authority to the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**, while the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues global **IFRS** standards. **Independent auditors** review the statements to determine GAAP compliance and issue one of four opinions: **unqualified** (financials fairly presented), **unqualified with explanatory paragraph** (additional context noted), **qualified** (specific limitation or deviation), or **adverse** (statements are misstated). These opinions enhance the credibility of reports for users.

Finally, the **balance sheet** is examined in depth. It presents a company’s resources (**assets**), obligations (**liabilities**), and owners’ residual interest (**stockholders’ equity**) at a specific point in time, reflecting the fundamental **accounting equation: Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**. Through the *Klein, Inc.* example—showing transactions such as stock issuance, borrowing, purchasing equipment, prepaying rent, and buying supplies—the lecture demonstrates that every transaction affects at least two accounts but always keeps this equation balanced, forming the foundation for accurate and ethical financial reporting.  

*(Word count: 398)*Overall winner: This lecture deepens understanding of the accounting framework by connecting financial statements to the double-entry recording system. It begins by emphasizing that economic activity is complex and accounting requires judgment to translate that activity into meaningful financial information. The four fundamental financial statements—the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—serve as the core communication tools of financial accounting. The balance sheet shows a company’s financial position at a specific point in time (the beginning and end of the year), while the other statements describe activity over a fiscal period. The income statement reports revenues and expenses to determine net income, which increases stockholders’ equity, and net loss, which decreases it. The statement of stockholders’ equity explains changes in equity during the period, and the cash flow statement details cash movements between two balance sheet dates.

The lecture revisits the accounting equation—Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity—and expands it to show equity as contributed capital plus retained earnings. Retained earnings evolve according to: beginning retained earnings + net income (revenues – expenses) – dividends. This expanded form highlights how income-producing activities and dividend distributions alter the equity portion of the equation.

Building on this foundation, the lecture introduces the double-entry system: every economic event has two sides—a give and a take—and must be recorded in at least two accounts so that debits equal credits. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities and equity; credits have the opposite effects. The “D E A L O R” memory aid helps classify accounts that normally carry debit (Dividends, Expenses, Assets) or credit (Liabilities, Owners’ equity, Revenue) balances. Several transaction examples illustrate this symmetry—borrowing from a bank, purchasing equipment or inventory, repaying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing shares—showing how journal entries maintain balance and feed into T‑accounts. Ending balances from T‑accounts become beginning balances in the next period, ensuring continuity across reporting cycles.

Finally, students are reminded that transactions move through a disciplined three‑step process: **analyze** the event, **record** debits and credits as a journal entry (listing debits first and indenting credits), and **post** those entries to the ledger. These procedures uphold the central rule—**Debits = Credits**—which guarantees that the accounting equation remains balanced and financial statements remain accurate.  

*(~385 words)*Overall winner: This lecture deepens understanding of how financial reporting and the double‑entry system work together to represent economic activity accurately. It opens by stressing that accounting requires judgment to capture complex transactions and communicate them through four interconnected financial statements: the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows**. The balance sheet reports a company’s position at specific points in time (beginning and end of the fiscal year), while the other statements cover activity over the period. The income statement shows revenues and expenses that determine net income or net loss, which respectively increase or decrease stockholders’ equity. The statement of stockholders’ equity tracks changes in equity, and the cash flow statement explains movements of cash between the two balance sheet dates.  

The lecture reviews the **accounting equation**—**Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**—and expands it to show **equity = contributed capital + retained earnings**. Retained earnings are updated as: beginning retained earnings + net income (revenues – expenses) – dividends. This form clarifies how company performance and distributions affect equity.  

Building on this, the lecture introduces **double‑entry accounting**, where every economic event has two sides—a give and a take—and must be recorded so that total debits equal total credits. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities and equity; credits do the opposite. No negative numbers are used; changes are recorded only through debits (Dr) and credits (Cr). The “**D E A L O R**” mnemonic identifies which accounts normally carry debit (Dividends, Expenses, Assets) or credit (Liabilities, Owners’ equity, Revenue) balances.  

Through examples—borrowing funds, purchasing equipment or inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing stock—the slides show how every transaction affects at least two accounts and how the accounting equation stays balanced. Ending balances from **T‑accounts** become the beginning balances of the next period, maintaining continuity between reporting cycles.  

Finally, the lecture introduces the formal **recording process**:  
1. **Analyze** each transaction.  
2. **Record** its effects in a **journal entry**, listing debits first and indenting credit accounts.  
3. **Post** the entries to the ledger (**T‑accounts**) to determine ending balances.  

These procedural steps uphold the foundational rule—**Debits = Credits**—ensuring that the accounting equation remains balanced and financial statements remain internally consistent.  

*(~395 words)*Overall winner: This lecture introduces the conceptual basis and recording mechanics of financial accounting through two connected parts: how financial statements communicate activity and how double‑entry procedures ensure accuracy.  

It begins by emphasizing that accounting translates complex economic events into structured reports using judgment and reasoning. The four main **financial statements**—**balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows**—are presented as an integrated communication system. The **balance sheet** shows assets, liabilities, and equity at the beginning and end of a period; the other statements explain changes between those dates. The **income statement** reports revenues and expenses over the fiscal year, producing **net income** or **net loss** that increases or decreases stockholders’ equity. The **statement of stockholders’ equity** summarizes all equity changes, and the **statement of cash flows** details movements in cash. Together they connect performance over time with position at each balance‑sheet date.  

The lecture restates the **accounting equation**—**Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**—and expands it to show **equity = contributed capital + retained earnings**. Retained earnings evolve as: beginning retained earnings + net income (revenues – expenses) – dividends. This structure makes clear how operations and distributions influence equity and maintain balance across periods.  

Next, the lecture introduces **double‑entry accounting**, the framework ensuring the equation holds. Every economic event has two sides, a give and a take, recorded in at least two accounts so that **total debits always equal total credits**. Debits (Dr) increase assets and decrease liabilities and equity; credits (Cr) have the opposite effect. All amounts are positive, expressed solely through Dr/Cr postings. The mnemonic **D E A L O R** identifies normal balances—Dividends, Expenses, and Assets are debit; Liabilities, Owners’ equity, and Revenue are credit.  

Through transaction examples—borrowing from a bank, purchasing equipment or inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing stock—the slides show how T‑accounts record increases and decreases, determine **ending balances**, and prepare the next period’s **beginning balances**.  

Finally, the lecture outlines the procedural sequence forming the accounting process:  
1. **Analyze** each transaction.  
2. **Record** debits and credits in a **journal entry**, listing debits first and indenting credits.  
3. **Post** entries to the **ledger (T‑accounts)** to aggregate balances.  

This cycle implements the fundamental rule—**Debits = Credits**—preserving the balance sheet equation and the internal consistency of all financial statements.  

*(~382 words)*Overall winner: This lecture deepens the foundation of financial accounting by connecting the structure of financial statements with the mechanics of double-entry recordkeeping. It begins by emphasizing that economic activity is inherently complex and that accounting requires both judgment and critical thinking to interpret and communicate this activity accurately. Net income or loss flows through retained earnings, linking the income statement to shareholders’ equity. Revenues raise, and expenses reduce, equity, illustrating how all statements are interdependent.  

Building on this linkage, the accounting equation—Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity—is expanded to show that equity consists of contributed capital and retained earnings, which adjust for revenues, expenses, and dividends. This evolving equation underpins both financial statement relationships and the mechanics of transaction recording.  

The lecture’s core section focuses on double-entry accounting: every economic event involves a “give” and a “take,” recorded through equal debits and credits that keep the accounting equation in balance. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity, while credits do the opposite. The “DEALOR” mnemonic (Dividends, Expenses, Assets, Liabilities, Owners’ equity, Revenue) helps identify normal debit or credit balances. The “super T-account” visualizes this balance by showing how different account types interact. Step-by-step transaction examples—borrowing from a bank, purchasing equipment or inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing stock—illustrate the dual-entry process.  

As transactions accumulate, T-accounts are used to compute ending balances, which become beginning balances in the next period’s records. This rolling structure demonstrates how periods connect through the balance sheet. Finally, the lecture summarizes the accounting process: analyze transactions, record journal entries, post to the ledger, and ensure that total debits always equal total credits. This logical sequence ensures the accuracy, consistency, and continuity of financial reporting.  

**Word count: 398**Overall winner: This lecture builds on the foundations of financial accounting by connecting the structure of financial statements with the mechanics of double‑entry recordkeeping. It begins by emphasizing that economic activity is complex, requiring judgment and critical thinking to measure and communicate financial information accurately.  

The session first revisits the financial accounting framework introduced in Week 1 and reviews the four key financial statements: the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows. These statements reveal different aspects of the same system—the balance sheet reports a company’s position at a point in time, while the income statement, statement of equity, and cash flow statement describe changes over a fiscal period. Net income increases stockholders’ equity and net loss decreases it; revenues raise, and expenses lower, retained earnings. Together these linkages show that financial statements do not exist in isolation.  

Building on this connection, the lecture expands the accounting equation from **Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity** to show equity as contributed capital plus retained earnings, which further depend on revenues, expenses, and dividends. This formulation illustrates how income generation and distributions shape equity and ensures that every transaction keeps the equation balanced.  

The lecture’s main focus is the double‑entry system. Each economic event has two sides—a give and a take—and is recorded with equal debits and credits. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity; credits have the opposite effects. Because every transaction affects at least two accounts, total debits must always equal total credits. The “DEALOR” mnemonic (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debits; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenues = Credits) and the “super T‑account” diagram help visualize these relationships across account types.  

Multiple transaction examples—borrowing cash, purchasing equipment and inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing stock—illustrate how entries maintain the accounting equation. Each entry is first analyzed, then recorded in journal form, and finally posted to T‑accounts to determine ending balances. Slides close with the explicit three‑step process: **analyze the transaction → record debits and credits (journal entries) → post to ledger (T‑accounts)**. Ending balances on the balance sheet become the starting balances for the next period, ensuring continuity across reporting periods.  

By linking financial statement relationships, the expanded accounting equation, and the journal‑to‑ledger procedure, the lecture demonstrates how double‑entry accounting provides an integrated and self‑balancing record of business activity.  

**Word count: 362**Overall winner: This lecture deepens the foundations of financial accounting by connecting the structure of financial statements with the mechanics of double‑entry recording. The session begins with a brief refresher on the financial accounting framework and revisits the four financial statements: the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows. These reports communicate different views of a company’s performance — the balance sheet captures financial position at a point in time (beginning and end of year), while the other statements reflect changes across the fiscal period. Net income increases stockholders’ equity, and net loss decreases it; revenues raise, and expenses reduce, retained earnings. The statements are interlinked so that each explains changes observed between balance sheet dates.  

The lecture then expands the accounting equation from **Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity** to show that equity consists of contributed capital and retained earnings. Retained earnings evolve through **beginning balance + revenues – expenses – dividends**, linking operational results to owners’ equity and ensuring the equation always balances.  

The core topic, double‑entry accounting, is introduced through the principle that every economic event has two sides—a give and a take. Each transaction affects at least two accounts and is recorded through equal debits and credits, keeping the accounting equation in balance. Debits increase assets while decreasing liabilities and equity; credits produce the opposite effects. The mnemonic “DEALOR” (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debits; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenues = Credits) and the “super T‑account” layout illustrate how account types respond to each side of a transaction.  

Worked examples demonstrate the mechanics numerically:  
– Borrowing **$100,000** from a bank increases both cash and notes payable.  
– Buying equipment for **$60,000** cash decreases cash and increases equipment.  
– Purchasing inventory for **$15,000** on account raises both assets and payables; paying **$8,000** of that reduces each.  
– Paying **$600** for a one‑year insurance policy creates prepaid insurance while reducing cash.  
– Issuing **10,000 shares for $100,000** increases both cash and common stock.  

The lecture concludes with the accounting process steps: **analyze the transaction → record debits and credits as journal entries → post to T‑accounts (ledger)**. Ending balances populate the balance sheet and become beginning balances for the next period, illustrating the continuity and balance that define the accounting system.

The session begins with a refresher on the financial accounting framework, reviewing how the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows** communicate a company’s financial position and performance. The balance sheet reports assets, liabilities, and equity at specific points in time (beginning and end of year), while the other statements describe changes across the fiscal period. Net income increases stockholders’ equity, and a net loss decreases it; revenues raise and expenses lower retained earnings. Each statement connects to the others—explaining movements between two balance sheet dates—to form an integrated reporting system.  

Next, the lecture expands the accounting equation from **Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity** to show equity as the sum of **contributed capital** and **retained earnings**. Retained earnings evolve as **beginning balance + revenues – expenses – dividends**, directly linking operations to changes in owners’ equity. This ensures the equation always balances and ties to the double‑entry framework.  

Double‑entry accounting is then introduced through the principle that every economic event has two sides—a give and a take—that must be recorded through equal **debits and credits**. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities and equity; credits do the opposite. Every transaction requires at least one debit and one credit, and **no negative numbers are allowed**—the equality of debits and credits maintains the balance sheet equation. The **“DEALOR”** mnemonic (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debits; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenues = Credits) and the “super T‑account” summarize how accounts respond.  

Worked examples reinforce these rules: borrowing **$100,000** increases both cash and notes payable; purchasing **$60,000** of equipment reduces cash but increases assets; buying **$15,000** of inventory on account raises both assets and payables; paying **$8,000** reduces each; paying **$600** for insurance creates prepaid insurance; issuing **10,000 shares for $100,000** increases cash and common stock. Ending balances are calculated in T‑accounts, then shown on the balance sheet. Slides emphasize continuity—each **12/31 ending balance becomes the 1/1 beginning balance** of the next period.  

The lecture concludes with the accounting process: **analyze transactions → record debits and credits as journal entries → post to T‑accounts (ledger)**. This cycle preserves accuracy and balance across reporting periods.

The session begins with a refresher on the **financial accounting framework**, focusing on how the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows** work together to communicate performance and position. The balance sheet reflects assets, liabilities, and equity at specific points in time (the beginning and end of the fiscal year), while the other statements describe changes over that period. Slides highlight how net income increases stockholders’ equity and losses decrease it, because **revenues raise and expenses reduce retained earnings**. Collectively, these statements explain the changes between two balance sheet dates, forming a fully linked reporting system.  

Next, the **accounting equation** is expanded from **Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity** to show the internal composition of equity: **Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + Retained Earnings**. Retained earnings evolve through **beginning balance + net income – dividends**, and net income itself derives from **revenues – expenses**. The slides then rearrange this form to present revenues, expenses, and dividends explicitly—clarifying how operations feed into equity and preserve the fundamental equation throughout each reporting period.  

The lecture then introduces **double‑entry accounting**, where every economic event has two reciprocal sides that must balance: one debit and one credit. Debits increase assets (and decrease liabilities and equity), while credits have the opposite effect. All transactions must include at least one debit and one credit, and **no negative numbers** are recorded—maintaining equality in the accounting equation. The **“DEALOR” mnemonic** (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debits; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenues = Credits) and the **super T‑account** summarize these relationships.  

Worked examples apply these rules: borrowing **$100,000** increases both cash and notes payable; purchasing **$60,000** of equipment decreases cash but raises assets; buying **$15,000** of inventory on account increases assets and payables; paying **$8,000** reduces each; **$600** for insurance creates prepaid insurance; issuing **10,000 shares for $100,000** increases both cash and common stock. Ending balances are computed in T‑accounts, shown on the balance sheet, and then carried forward—**each ending balance becomes the beginning balance of the next period**.  

The lecture concludes with the **accounting process cycle**: **analyze transactions → record debits and credits (journal entries) → post to T‑accounts (ledger)**, reinforcing that balanced recording underpins the accuracy and continuity of all financial statements.

The session begins with a **refresher on the financial accounting framework**, explaining how the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows** together communicate a company’s position and performance. The balance sheet presents assets, liabilities, and equity at specific points in time, while the other statements describe changes during the fiscal year. Slides highlight that **net income raises stockholders’ equity, and losses reduce it**, since **revenues increase and expenses decrease retained earnings**. Collectively, these statements explain the movement between two balance sheet dates and must be interpreted as a linked reporting system.  

The **accounting equation** is expanded from **Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity** to **Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + Retained Earnings**, showing equity’s internal structure. Retained earnings evolve through **beginning balance + net income – dividends**, where net income equals **revenues – expenses**. By rearranging this form, the slides make revenues, expenses, and dividends explicit, clarifying how operations maintain the equation’s balance throughout each period.  

Next, the lecture introduces **double‑entry recordkeeping**, where every economic event has two sides — a give and a take — that must balance. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity; credits have the opposite effect. Each transaction requires at least one debit and one credit, and **no negative numbers** are used. Critically, **“Debits = credits maintains the balance sheet equation (A = L + SE)”**, emphasizing the conceptual link between recording rules and financial statement integrity. The **super T‑account** and **DEALOR mnemonic** (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debits; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenues = Credits) summarize these relationships.  

Worked examples show how loans, purchases, payments, insurance, and issuance of shares are recorded and balanced. Ending balances computed in T‑accounts feed the balance sheet and become next period’s beginning balances.  

Finally, the **accounting process** is presented stepwise: **analyze transactions → record debits and credits (journal entries) → post to T‑accounts (ledger)**. This cycle illustrates how consistent double‑entry recording maintains both the equality of the accounting equation and the continuity of financial statements.  

**Word count: 395**Overall winner: This week’s lecture examined Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **coloniality of power** and its continuing influence on modernity, capitalism, and national identity in Latin America. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist—developed his ideas through several intellectual phases: *dependency theory* (focused on political economy), *cultural and historiographic approaches* (concerned with modernity, culture, and identity), and *decolonial theory* (examining epistemology, or knowledge and power). His work is part of a broader **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD) project** that includes other Latin American scholars such as Walter Mignolo, Arturo Escobar, and Catherine Walsh, highlighting a collaborative rather than singular movement. Together, these thinkers question Western assumptions of universal modernity and propose counter-narratives that center non‑Western experience and knowledge.

The lecture outlined two historical processes constituting the **coloniality of power**: first, the invention of racial distinctions separating conquerors (“the West”) from the conquered (“the Rest”); and second, the creation of new global systems of labor control in the Americas. Forms of coerced labor such as slavery, the **encomienda**, and the **mita** were organized for export economies and embedded racial hierarchy into capitalist development. These racialized divisions of labor shaped access to privilege, space, and authority. Over time, **Eurocentrism**—the worldview positioning Europe as the universal model of progress—naturalized this unequal order. Quijano links these patterns to the co‑constitution of coloniality and modernity: Western “progress” depended on colonial domination even as it masked that dependency through narratives of civilization.

The lecture then connected this analysis to **nation‑building and cultural identity**. Drawing on historian Rebecca Earle’s discussion of *“Indianesque nationalism,”* it examined how early independence movements idealized Indigenous pasts as symbols of pride while continuing to exclude living Indigenous peoples. Later, the ideology of **mestizaje**—celebrating racial mixing—was promoted as a unifying national myth yet often reaffirmed whiteness and erased Indigenous and Afro‑descendant identities. The class was invited to consider Quijano’s question: *How might the coloniality of power explain these shifts?*—prompting reflection on how racial hierarchies and Eurocentric epistemologies persist beneath apparently inclusive narratives.

Across these themes—including examples like Alfonso Cuarón’s *Roma*, which exposes enduring class, gender, and racial inequalities—the lecture emphasized Quijano’s closing call for **decoloniality**: to release ourselves from the “Eurocentric mirror” that distorts our self‑understanding and to recover plural, heterogenous ways of knowing and being beyond inherited colonial frameworks.  

(≈400 words)Overall winner: This week’s lecture examined Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **coloniality of power** and its continuing influence on modernity, capitalism, and national identity in Latin America. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist—developed key ideas through phases of dependency theory (focused on political economy), cultural and historiographic analysis (modernity, identity, culture), and decolonial theory (knowledge and power). His work contributes to the broader **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD) project**, alongside scholars such as Arturo Escobar, Walter Mignolo, and Catherine Walsh, who collectively question Europe’s claim to universal modernity and assert the importance of non‑Western epistemologies.

The **coloniality of power**, Quijano argues, originated through two interconnected historical processes: the creation of racial hierarchies that divided conquerors (“the West”) from the conquered (“the Rest”), and the establishment of new structures for controlling labor and resources in the Americas. This reorganization of global production relied on coerced systems such as slavery, the **encomienda**, and the **mita**, embedding racialized labor into emerging capitalism. The result was a lasting global order where access to privilege, space, and authority remained tied to race.

Building on this, the lecture turned to **Eurocentrism**—the naturalized worldview that locates Europe as the center of history and knowledge. Slides emphasized that Eurocentrism rests on **linear and dualistic models of progress** (“pre‑capital” → “industrial,” “Third World” → “First World”), which portray Western development as the universal path and render other histories “backward.” Quijano exposes how such evolutionist thinking sustains global inequality while erasing the colonial origins of modern capitalism.

Later sections linked these ideas to Latin American **nation‑building** and identity. Drawing on Rebecca Earle’s study of *“Indianesque nationalism,”* the lecture showed how independence movements romanticized Indigenous imagery to distinguish new nations from Spain, even as they marginalized living Indigenous peoples. The ideology of **mestizaje**, presented as harmonious “racial mixing,” further reinforced Eurocentric standards of beauty and belonging while excluding Afro‑descendant and Indigenous groups. Quijano’s framework helps explain these continuities as expressions of coloniality within post‑colonial forms.

Finally, the lecture introduced Quijano’s and others’ call for **decoloniality**, aligning with the MCD project’s move beyond linear temporality toward **heterogeneity and discontinuity** in historical experience—echoing Aymara notions of *pachakuti* as cyclical world renewal. Decolonial thinking thus seeks to free knowledge from the “Eurocentric mirror,” recover plural modernities, and recognize multiple, situated ways of being and knowing beyond inherited colonial frameworks.

(≈396 words)Overall winner: This week’s lecture expanded on Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **coloniality of power**, a framework for analyzing how colonial structures continue to shape Latin American societies and global hierarchies. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist whose work spans dependency theory, cultural analysis, and decolonial thought—forms part of a broader group of scholars (including Arturo Escobar, Walter Mignolo, and Catherine Walsh) often associated with the **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD)** perspective. His scholarship situates colonialism not as an isolated past event but as an enduring set of epistemic and material relationships linking knowledge, race, labor, and power.  

According to Quijano, the **coloniality of power** emerged through two foundational processes: (1) the creation of racial difference separating conquerors (the “West”) from the conquered (the “Rest”), and (2) new systems for organizing labor and resources in the Americas. Colonial authorities established racialized hierarchies within developing capitalist economies—through slavery, the **encomienda**, and the **mita**—that structured who held access to land, wealth, and authority. These racialized divisions became the basis for a global order that persists under capitalism and modern national systems.

Eurocentric narratives of “progress” rely on dualisms such as “pre‑capital/industrial” and “Third World/First World,” which conceal the colonial origins of global capitalism and normalize European dominance. By coupling racial hierarchies with capitalist development, Eurocentrism defined whose perspectives counted as knowledge and whose labor sustained the modern world.  

Applications illustrated how these logics continue in cultural and national imaginaries. The film *Roma* was discussed as an example of how domestic labor and racial hierarchy in contemporary Mexico reflect colonial divisions. Drawing on historian Rebecca Earle’s research, slides on **“Indianesque nationalism”** showed that after independence, Latin American elites celebrated Indigenous symbols to mark separation from Spain while excluding living Indigenous and Afro‑descendant peoples. Questioning linear, homogenizing histories opens space for recognizing heterogeneous and historically specific processes of change. In this sense, decolonial thinking re‑centers marginalized epistemologies and reimagines modernity through plural, locally grounded worldviews.  

(≈381 words)Overall winner: This lecture examines Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the *coloniality of power*—a framework explaining how colonial-era patterns of domination continue to organize knowledge, labor, and identity in Latin America. Quijano, a Peruvian sociologist, developed this idea after earlier work in dependency theory and regional cultural analysis. His thinking became part of the wider Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD) project, which interrogates the links among modernity, coloniality, and efforts toward decoloniality.  

Modernity, in this framework, is understood not as a Western gift but as a historical condition co‑constituted with colonial expansion. Coloniality exposes how domination persisted after formal colonialism through Eurocentric categories of knowledge that privileged Western perspectives while marginalizing Indigenous, African, and other epistemologies. Decoloniality, then, does not simply oppose coloniality but seeks to open intellectual and cultural spaces for multiple, non‑Western worlds of knowing and being.

At the core of Quijano’s argument are two intertwined historical processes: the invention of racial distinctions separating *conquerors* (“West”) from the *conquered* (“Rest”) and the formation of new systems of labor control in the Americas. Enslavement, the encomienda, and the mita institutionalized a global model of racialized labor that tied whiteness to power and non‑European peoples to coerced or unpaid work. This fusion of race and economy produced a “systemic racial division of labor,” shaping access to resources, status, and authority even beyond colonial rule. Eurocentrism emerged from and sustained this structure, naturalizing Europe’s dominance and portraying its trajectory as universal progress.

Slides also link Eurocentrism to ideas of nation, development, and democracy after independence. Linear narratives of “progress” and “underdevelopment” reproduced colonial hierarchies inside new nation‑states. Rebecca Earle’s discussion of “Indianesque nationalism” shows how independence‑era leaders romanticized Indigenous ancestry as national symbolism while excluding living Indigenous communities from citizenship. Later *mestizaje* ideologies invoked racial mixing as harmony but continued privileging whiteness, evident across cultural forms from family portrayals to artworks such as *A Redenção de Cam.*  

The lecture situates cultural examples like Alfonso Cuarón’s *Roma* within this matrix, illustrating how racialized and gendered hierarchies persist in everyday life. Quijano’s closing challenge—to “free ourselves from the Eurocentric mirror” that distorts identity—frames decoloniality as an ongoing process of unlearning inherited dualisms, recognizing historical heterogeneity, and envisioning plural modernities beyond the confines of Eurocentric thought.

**(399 words)**Overall winner: This lecture explained how natural selection and genetic inheritance together account for evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time. Darwin’s three postulates state that organisms struggle for existence, vary in traits affecting survival and reproduction, and pass these traits to offspring. Although Darwin recognized variation as essential, he could not explain how it arose or persisted. Artificial selection in domesticated plants and animals showed that selective breeding can rapidly produce significant changes, offering a model for natural processes. Rates of evolutionary change vary widely: traits such as a placenta-like structure in minnows can evolve quickly, while features like the eye accumulate through many small steps over long timescales.

The lecture then traced how genetic understanding resolved Darwin’s questions about variation. Gregor Mendel’s pea plant experiments demonstrated that traits are inherited as discrete “particles” (genes) rather than blended, maintaining variation. His rediscovered work at the turn of the 20th century spurred advances in cytogenetics. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, while Walter Sutton and Theodor Boveri showed that genes reside on chromosomes, establishing the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance.

Cell division occurs by mitosis, producing identical diploid cells, or by meiosis, which forms haploid gametes. Variation is introduced through mutation and recombination during meiosis. Mendel distinguished between genotype (genetic makeup) and phenotype (observable traits) and showed that dominant and recessive alleles interact predictably. Tools such as Punnett squares illustrate how heterozygote crosses yield consistent ratios. Linkage and recombination further explain how genes are inherited together or separately, with recombination rates measuring the frequency of chromosome crossing-over.

The discovery of DNA’s structure in 1953 by James Watson and Francis Crick, based on Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray evidence, revealed the molecular basis of heredity. DNA consists of a sugar‑phosphate backbone and paired bases—A with T and C with G—that enable replication and protein coding. Genes are functional DNA sequences, while mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA), inherited only from mothers, provides insight into human ancestry. Comparisons of mtDNA established the concept of “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans, living roughly 100,000–200,000 years ago in Africa.  

(Approximately 395 words)Overall winner: This lecture connected natural selection with the genetic mechanisms that make evolution possible. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time. Darwin’s postulates described a struggle for existence, heritable variation in traits relevant to survival and reproduction, and the passing on of that variation. While Darwin recognized that variation drives natural selection, he could not account for how it was maintained. Artificial selection in domesticated species such as dogs, pigeons, and crop plants showed that targeted breeding can rapidly create distinct forms, serving as a model for evolutionary change. Rates of change differ greatly: minnows demonstrate rapid evolution of placenta-like structures, whereas complex features such as the eye evolve gradually through roughly 1,800 incremental steps over about a million years.

Genetic discoveries resolved Darwin’s problem of variation. His rediscovered work led to cytological breakthroughs: Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, while Walter Sutton and Theodor Boveri linked genes to chromosomes, establishing the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Mitosis produces identical diploid cells, whereas meiosis forms haploid gametes, halving chromosome number so the total remains constant after fertilization. Variation arises through mutation and recombination during meiosis. Mendel’s concepts of genotype and phenotype, and dominant and recessive alleles, predict offspring ratios illustrated by Punnett squares. Comparisons of mtDNA trace human ancestry to “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor living about 100,000–200,000 years ago in Africa. DNA comparison at neutral sites functions as a molecular clock, allowing scientists to estimate when species diverged.

(≈392 words)Overall winner: This lecture connected Darwin’s theory of natural selection with the genetic and molecular mechanisms that make evolution possible. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time. Darwin proposed that individuals struggle to survive, vary in traits related to survival and reproduction, and pass on these traits to offspring. Although he recognized variation as essential, he could not explain how it was inherited or maintained. Artificial selection in domesticated species, such as dogs and pigeons, demonstrated that strong selection could produce major changes in few generations, mirroring natural selection. Evolutionary rates vary, from rapid adaptations like placenta-like structures in minnows to the gradual evolution of complex organs such as the eye through many tiny, incremental steps.

The genetic foundation of inheritance clarified how variation persists. Gregor Mendel’s pea plant experiments showed that traits are transmitted as discrete “particles” from each parent, preserving variation rather than blending it. The early 1900s rediscovery of his work led to studies on chromosomes. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, and later, Walter Sutton and Theodor Boveri demonstrated that heredity resides in chromosomes—the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Humans have 46 chromosomes arranged in 23 homologous pairs, one of which determines sex.

Understanding cell division was essential to understanding inheritance. Mitosis produces identical diploid cells for growth, while meiosis generates haploid gametes, maintaining consistent chromosome numbers across generations. Variation arises through mutation and recombination during meiosis. Mendel’s principles distinguished genotype (genetic code) from phenotype (physical expression), with dominant alleles masking recessive ones. Linkage and recombination studies further refined inheritance models: genes on different chromosomes assort independently, but those located close together may be inherited as linked traits. Crossing-over during meiosis can break such linkages, producing new gene combinations. The recombination rate measures how often a crossing-over event occurs between two genes during meiosis.

Molecular discoveries later pinpointed DNA as the hereditary material. Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray images enabled Watson and Crick to describe the double‑helix structure in 1953, establishing how structure explains replication, protein coding, and how DNA comparisons reveal evolutionary timelines. DNA has a sugar‑phosphate backbone with base pairs (A‑T, C‑G). Mitochondrial DNA, maternally inherited without recombination, has been used to identify “Mitochondrial Eve”—the most recent common matrilineal ancestor of all humans about 100–200 thousand years ago. Comparisons of neutral DNA sites serve as a molecular clock for estimating species divergence.  

(≈398 words)Overall winner: This lecture examined how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population across generations, supported by Darwin’s postulates that populations face a struggle for existence, individuals vary in traits tied to survival and reproduction, and this variation is heritable. Darwin’s key challenge was explaining how variation arises and persists, which later discoveries in genetics resolved. Artificial selection in domesticated animals and plants illustrated how targeted breeding can produce marked changes within a few generations. Examples such as rapid placental evolution in minnows and incremental eye evolution in small, cumulative steps showed that evolutionary rates can vary, from swift shifts to gradual transformations over millions of years.  

Mendel’s experiments with pea plants established the particulate nature of inheritance—traits are determined by discrete “particles” (genes) passed from each parent, maintaining variation generation after generation. His work resurfaced in the early 1900s and was connected to cellular structures. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, and Sutton and Boveri proposed the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance, identifying chromosomes as carriers of genetic material.  

Understanding cell division clarified how inheritance operates. Mitosis produces identical diploid somatic cells, while meiosis creates haploid gametes in which variation can arise through mutation, recombination, and independent assortment. Mendel’s laws distinguished genotypes (genetic makeup) from phenotypes (observable traits) and showed dominant and recessive allele interactions. When heterozygotes are crossed, predictable offspring ratios emerge, demonstrated through Punnett squares. Linkage and crossing over explained how nearby genes may be inherited together or reshuffled, introducing new genetic combinations measurable by recombination rate.  

The discovery of DNA’s double-helix structure by James Watson and Francis Crick, based on Rosalind Franklin’s x-ray data, revealed the molecular basis of heredity. DNA’s sugar-phosphate backbone holds paired bases—adenine with thymine and cytosine with guanine. Genes are functional DNA sequences that can vary as alleles. Mitochondrial DNA, inherited only from mothers and lacking recombination, is shorter and more conserved than nuclear DNA. Comparisons of mtDNA enable estimation of common ancestry, identifying “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans, dated roughly 100–200 thousand years ago in East Africa—an example of how molecular data illuminate evolutionary history.  

(398 words)Overall winner: The opening lecture of *Principles of Accounting* introduces students to the role of accounting in recording and communicating a company’s financial story. Accounting is presented as the language of business—capturing a firm’s successes, challenges, and opportunities to help users make informed decisions. Its two main functions are recordkeeping, which identifies, measures, and records economic events in value terms, and communication, which conveys this information to external and internal decision makers through financial statements. The course objectives emphasize developing the ability to read, understand, and use these statements, and to apply accounting information for sound business decision‑making across disciplines.

Users of accounting information include investors evaluating company performance, creditors and suppliers assessing repayment ability and reliability, management and boards monitoring operations and strategy, and others such as employees, customers, analysts, and regulators who rely on credible financial data. Accounting measures three core business activities: financing transactions with investors and creditors, investing in productive resources, and operating activities tied to production and sales. The results are summarized in four key financial statements—the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—that together describe the company’s financial position and performance.

Financial reporting follows Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP), which provide consistent rules for measurement and disclosure. The SEC oversees application of these standards for public firms and delegates standard‑setting to the independent Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB); international reporting operates under the IASB’s International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS). Independent auditors review statements to determine whether they comply with GAAP and are free of material misstatements, issuing opinions that enhance confidence in reported results.

The lecture focuses on the balance sheet as a snapshot of financial position at a specific date, summarizing the company’s resources (assets) and the corresponding claims—creditor obligations (liabilities) and owners’ residual interest (stockholders’ equity). The accounting equation, Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity, must always remain in balance, with every transaction producing equal dual effects. Illustrative examples show how issuing stock, borrowing funds, acquiring equipment, prepaying rent, and purchasing supplies affect assets, liabilities, and equity while maintaining equilibrium. This framework establishes how accounting captures business activity and communicates reliable information essential for decision‑making.

Illustrative examples show how issuing stock, borrowing funds, acquiring equipment, prepaying rent, and purchasing supplies maintain this equilibrium, reinforcing how accounting translates business activities into a coherent and reliable financial picture.

Examples included shifts in the legal status of Armenians, Japanese, and Mexicans, and the long-standing bans on interracial marriage removed only in 1967. Baldwin’s observation that whiteness in America was created “through generations and coercion” underscored how privileges of citizenship and property were tied to racial identity. Everyone possesses culture, and people actively select and adapt its elements. The lecture concluded by analyzing **school culture**—the norms, hierarchies, and beliefs about intelligence embedded in schooling practices—which influence students’ participation, achievement, and sense of possibility. In bringing together race, law, language, and education, the session emphasized awareness, reflection, and courage as central to understanding social construction in everyday life.

(≈393 words)Overall winner: The Week 1 lecture of *Principles of Accounting* introduces students to the purpose and framework of financial accounting and establishes the foundational goal of the course: understanding how financial statements are created so that students can read, interpret, and use them to make business decisions. Accounting is presented as the language of business—telling a company’s financial story about its successes, risks, and opportunities. It performs two main functions: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records a firm’s economic events in value terms, and **communication**, which provides this information to external decision makers through financial statements.

Users of accounting information include investors evaluating management performance and financial condition, creditors and suppliers judging repayment ability or credit terms, management and boards assessing operations and strategy, and other groups such as employees, customers, analysts, regulators, and competitors. All rely on accounting data to support informed economic choices.

Financial accounting measures three types of business activities. **Financing activities** involve transactions with investors and creditors that fund business operations. **Investing activities** relate to acquiring and disposing of the resources used to produce and sell products or services. **Operating activities** encompass day‑to‑day transactions tied to production, marketing, and administration. The results of these activities are communicated through four primary **financial statements**: the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows.

Preparation of these reports follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)**, developed by the Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) under the oversight of the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC). The **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues corresponding international standards, IFRS. Independent **auditors** evaluate whether financial statements comply with GAAP and are free of material misstatement, issuing opinions that add credibility for decision makers.

The lecture highlights the **balance sheet** as a central component of the financial accounting framework. It reports a company’s financial position at a specific point in time and must satisfy the **accounting equation**: *Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity*. Assets represent resources providing future benefits, liabilities reflect obligations to creditors, and stockholders’ equity shows owners’ residual claims. Step‑by‑step examples—such as issuing stock, borrowing funds, purchasing equipment, paying rent in advance, or buying supplies on credit—demonstrate that each transaction affects at least two accounts but always preserves balance. This concept lays the groundwork for analyzing how business activities are recorded and communicated through financial statements.
