≈400 wordsOverall winner: The Week 1 lecture of *Principles of Accounting* introduces students to financial accounting as the study of how businesses record and communicate economic activity through standardized financial statements. The course objective is to help students understand how these statements are created, read, and used to make informed business decisions.  

Accounting is framed as the **language of business**, telling an organization’s financial story—its achievements, risks, and challenges. It serves two core functions. **Recordkeeping** involves identifying, measuring, and recording economic events in value terms, while **communication** involves organizing that information into financial statements for external users.  

Those users include investors evaluating company performance, creditors and suppliers assessing repayment ability, management and boards reviewing operations and strategy, and others such as employees, customers, regulators, and analysts. Each group relies on accounting data to guide decisions about employment, lending, purchasing, and oversight.  

The lecture outlines three business activity categories measured by financial accounting. **Financing activities** capture transactions with investors and creditors that supply resources to the firm. **Investing activities** involve acquiring and disposing of productive assets such as equipment or inventory. **Operating activities** encompass the daily actions—production, marketing, administration—needed to run the business. These activities culminate in four key **financial statements**: the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows.  

Preparation of these reports follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)**, issued by the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)** under oversight from the **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)**. Public companies must provide periodic reports—annual (10‑K), quarterly (10‑Q), and current event (8‑K) filings. Internationally, the **IASB** sets parallel **IFRS** standards. Independent **auditors** evaluate compliance with GAAP and ensure statements are free from material misstatement, adding credibility through their professional opinions.  

The lecture then introduces the **balance sheet** as a snapshot of a company’s financial position at a specific date. It portrays the accounting equation — **Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity** — showing that every transaction affects at least two accounts but keeps the equation balanced. Assets represent resources providing future benefit; liabilities are obligations to outsiders; equity is the owners’ residual interest. Step‑by‑step illustrations, such as issuing stock, borrowing funds, purchasing equipment, prepaying rent, or buying supplies on credit, demonstrate how financing, investing, and operating decisions collectively maintain this balance and form the basis for understanding all financial reporting.

Accounting tells a firm’s financial story—its performance, risks, and opportunities—so that users can make informed judgments.  

The lecture presents two central **functions** of accounting.  
1. **Recordkeeping** identifies, measures, and documents business events in value terms, forming a systematic record of a company’s operations.  
2. **Communication** transforms this information into standardized financial statements used by external and internal stakeholders.  

The primary **users of financial information** include investors assessing management performance and firm value; creditors evaluating repayment ability; suppliers setting credit terms; management and boards overseeing operations and strategy; employees gauging job stability; and customers, regulators, and analysts judging reliability and overall financial condition.  

Financial accounting captures three categories of activity: **financing** (transactions with investors and lenders that provide capital), **investing** (acquiring or selling productive resources such as equipment or land), and **operating** (core activities of producing and selling goods or services). Their results appear in four fundamental **financial statements**—the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—each providing a different view of company performance and position.  

Reporting follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)**, which establish consistent rules for recognizing and presenting transactions. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** oversees accounting and reporting for publicly traded firms and delegates standard‑setting to the independent **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**. Globally, comparable guidance is issued by the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** under **IFRS**. Public firms file periodic reports: annual audited 10‑K statements, unaudited quarterly 10‑Q reports, and 8‑K filings for significant events.  

Independent **auditors**, required by the SEC for public companies, evaluate whether financial statements conform to GAAP and are free of material misstatements. They issue one of four opinions—unqualified, unqualified with explanatory paragraph, qualified, or adverse—adding credibility for decision‑makers.

*(Word count: 394)*Overall winner: This lecture examined nested and subqueries in SQL, explaining their forms, placement in different clauses, and their relationship to the theoretical idea of monotonicity in query evaluation. Building on prior SQL topics such as projection, selection, joins, aggregation, and grouping, the lecture defined a subquery as a query that is part of another—possibly itself nested—and noted that subqueries can appear in SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses. The guiding rule was to avoid nested queries when possible, since equivalent logic can often be expressed more efficiently through joins or grouping operations (“subquery unnesting”).

Subqueries in the SELECT clause were introduced through examples that retrieve related attributes, such as a movie’s genre or an actor’s average salary. By comparing these to equivalent join-based queries, the lecture showed that unnesting typically simplifies computation and preserves results, though differences may arise when outer joins are involved. Subqueries in the FROM clause function as temporary derived tables, either written inline or defined using common table expressions (CTEs). These refactor complex filters into clearly named intermediate results while keeping the final query concise.

Subqueries in the WHERE clause handle logical quantifiers. The lecture first discussed existential quantifiers—conditions testing whether some related row exists—using the EXISTS or IN operators. Such queries check for “for some” conditions and can often be rewritten as joins. Universal quantifiers (“for all”), however, are more challenging, as SQL lacks direct support. Slides illustrated a two-step approach: (1) identify all actors who have worked on some non-action movie, and then (2) retrieve all remaining actors who did not appear in that set—those who acted only in action movies. Similar logic was applied to examples such as finding movies where all actors earn more than a specific salary threshold, using NOT IN or NOT EXISTS constructs to approximate universal checks.

The lecture concluded with an exploration of monotone and non-monotone queries. A monotone query is one whose output never loses tuples when data are added. SELECT–FROM–WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone under nested loop semantics. The final slides established that if a query is monotone, it can be unnested; if it is non-monotone—as in cases involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates—it cannot. This relationship links the logical properties of queries to their rewrite and optimization behavior in SQL. It serves two main functions: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records economic events in value terms, and **communication**, which provides useful information to external decision makers through financial statements.

Users of accounting information include a wide range of stakeholders. **Investors** use it to assess management performance and financial condition. **Creditors** and **suppliers** evaluate a company’s ability to meet obligations and sustain business relationships. **Management** uses it to track operations and compare performance, while the **Board of Directors** oversees management on behalf of shareholders. Other users include employees considering job security, customers judging reliability of suppliers, analysts, regulators, and consumer groups.

Publicly traded companies prepare these reports periodically—annually (10‑K), quarterly (10‑Q), or for major events (8‑K).

Illustrative examples—such as issuing stock, borrowing from a bank, purchasing equipment, prepaying rent, and buying supplies—demonstrate that every transaction has a dual effect but keeps the equation balanced, forming the foundation for all financial reporting and analysis.

*(Word count: 401)*Overall winner: This lecture focused on nested SQL queries, explaining how subqueries function within different clauses and how their logical properties relate to query optimization and monotonicity. Building on prior topics such as projection, selection, joins, aggregation, and grouping, it defined a subquery as a query that is part of another and may itself be nested. Subqueries can occur in SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses, and although they are sometimes necessary, the rule of thumb is to avoid nesting when equivalent results can be expressed through joins—a process called subquery unnesting.

In the SELECT clause, subqueries can return a single value or relation to compute context-dependent attributes, such as finding a movie’s genre per actor or the average salary for actors in highly rated movies. Equivalent join-based queries often simplify computation, though care must be taken when outer joins change result semantics. In the FROM clause, subqueries act as derived tables or temporary relations, forming a clear intermediate step that can also be expressed with common table expressions (CTEs). These help refactor complex filtering logic into smaller, named subqueries without correlation.

Subqueries in the WHERE clause were discussed through logical quantifiers. **Existential quantifiers** test whether some related row exists, using *EXISTS* or *IN*, expressing “there exists” or “for some.” Such queries can usually be converted into equivalent joins. **Universal quantifiers** express that all related rows meet a condition—something SQL cannot directly represent. The slides illustrated a procedural, two-step approach for expressing “only acted in action movies”: first, find all actors who have appeared in any non‑action movie; then, in a separate query, return actors not in that set. The same reasoning applied when checking if every actor in a movie earns more than $100K, using *NOT IN* or *NOT EXISTS* constructs to handle the universal logic.

The lecture concluded with the concept of **monotone queries**, which never lose output tuples when input data grow. Using nested loop semantics, a plain SELECT–FROM–WHERE query without subqueries, aggregates, or negation is monotone. Monotonicity determines unnesting feasibility: a monotone query can be unnested, whereas queries involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates are non‑monotone and thus cannot. This connects the logical structure of queries to their practical optimization behavior. All reporting must comply with **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)**, which ensure comparability and consistency among U.S. firms. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** enforces these requirements, delegating standard‑setting authority to the independent **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)** but retaining the power to mandate changes. Globally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues **International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS)**. **Independent auditors**, required for public companies, evaluate whether statements follow GAAP and are free from material misstatement, adding credibility for decision makers.

The lecture concludes with the **balance sheet**, which summarizes a company’s financial position at a specific date, following the **accounting equation**: *Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity*. Assets are resources with future economic benefit; liabilities are obligations to creditors; and equity represents owners’ residual claims. Through the *Klein, Inc.* example—showing stock issuance, borrowing, equipment purchase, prepaid rent, and supplies bought on credit—students see that every transaction has a **dual effect** yet keeps the equation balanced, forming the foundation for understanding all future financial reporting.  
*(~400 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined **nested SQL queries**, showing how subqueries work within different clauses and how their logic connects to optimization concepts like *unnesting* and *monotonicity*. After reviewing key SQL operations—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and data modifications—it defined a **subquery** as a query that appears inside another and can itself be nested. Subqueries can occur in the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clause. The general rule is to avoid unnecessary nesting when equivalent results can be achieved using joins, a process called *subquery unnesting*.

In the **SELECT clause**, subqueries can return a single value or a relation, such as listing each actor with the genre of their movie or calculating average actor salaries for films rated above 9. Equivalent join-based versions perform the same computation more efficiently. However, slides showed that such equivalence can break when *outer joins* are involved—illustrated using a left outer join where movies without actors might still appear, changing results.

In the **FROM clause**, subqueries create *derived tables*, providing intermediate results that can simplify complex filters. These can also be written with *common table expressions (CTEs)*, which act as reusable subquery definitions. Such subqueries are not correlated with outer queries and can also be unnested.

In the **WHERE clause**, subqueries introduced logical quantifiers. **Existential quantifiers** (using *EXISTS* or *IN*) test whether at least one related tuple satisfies a condition, expressing “there exists.” They typically translate into join queries, as shown in examples retrieving actors who have appeared in Sci‑Fi films. **Universal quantifiers** express conditions that must hold for *all* related tuples (“for every”), requiring indirect SQL formulations. The slides demonstrated expressing “only acted in Action movies” by first identifying actors in any non‑Action film and then excluding them. Likewise, to find movies where all actors earn above \$100K, SQL uses either `<ALL` or the equivalent `NOT EXISTS` form, though support varies across systems like SQLite.

The lecture concluded with the concept of **monotone queries**—queries whose outputs never shrink when input data grow. Using nested loop semantics, a basic SELECT–FROM–WHERE query (without subqueries, aggregates, or negation) is monotone. Monotonicity governs unnesting: if a query is monotone, it can be unnested; non‑monotone queries involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates cannot. This ties logical expressiveness directly to optimization feasibility. (≈380 words)Overall winner: The first week of *Principles of Accounting* introduces financial accounting as the language of business—the system that records, measures, and communicates an organization’s financial story. Accounting captures key aspects of a firm’s performance, risks, and resources in measurable form to inform business decisions. It serves two core purposes: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records economic events in value terms, and **communication**, which conveys summarized information to external users through **financial statements**.

Financial accounting focuses on three fundamental categories of activity. **Financing activities** involve obtaining resources from investors and creditors. **Investing activities** concern acquiring or disposing of assets—like buildings, equipment, or inventory—needed to operate. **Operating activities** reflect day‑to‑day transactions related to producing and selling goods and services. The outcomes of these activities are communicated through four primary financial statements: the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows**.

Accounting information supports a wide range of users. **Investors** and **creditors** evaluate performance, repayment ability, and risk. **Suppliers** use financial data to set credit terms and assess long‑term relationships. **Management** and the **board of directors** rely on it to monitor performance and guide strategy. **Employees** and **customers** gauge financial health and reliability, while **analysts**, **regulators**, and **other stakeholders** use it for oversight and comparison.

Financial reporting follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)**—rules established by the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)** under authority delegated by the **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)**. Internationally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues **IFRS**. Public companies must have their financial statements examined by **independent auditors**, who evaluate compliance with GAAP and the presence of material misstatements. Their opinions—**unqualified**, **unqualified with emphasis**, **qualified**, and **adverse**—add credibility to reported information and guide decision‑makers.

The lecture emphasizes the **balance sheet** as a snapshot of financial position based on the equation **Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**. **Assets** are resources providing future benefit; **liabilities** represent obligations to creditors; and **equity** reflects owners’ residual claims. Examples such as issuing stock, borrowing funds, purchasing equipment, paying rent in advance, and buying supplies demonstrate that every transaction affects at least two accounts but always keeps the equation balanced. This framework establishes how accounting structures economic information to support transparent and informed decision‑making.

*(Word count: 397)*Overall winner: This lecture examined nested and subqueries in SQL, focusing on how they operate within the SELECT, FROM, and WHERE clauses and how their structure influences efficiency and expressiveness. It also introduced the related theoretical concept of query monotonicity, linking it to whether a query can be unnested during optimization.

The lecture began by reviewing core SQL capabilities—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and grouping—before introducing subqueries as queries embedded within other queries. These subqueries may return single values or whole relations and can appear in different clauses. While syntactically powerful, nested queries are often inefficient, and the slides emphasized the importance of **subquery unnesting**, or rewriting nested forms into equivalent join-based forms whenever possible.

Examples in the SELECT clause illustrated how subqueries can compute derived attributes for each row, such as retrieving a movie’s genre for each actor or calculating the number of actors per movie. Equivalent join-based versions demonstrated how flattened queries can achieve the same output more efficiently. However, the lecture noted an important caveat (Slide 10): inner joins and outer joins do not always yield identical results, meaning some subquery formulations differ semantically when movies have no matching records in the related table.

Subqueries in the FROM clause were presented as a way to build temporary result sets—also called inline views or Common Table Expressions (CTEs)—supporting stepwise query decomposition. This allows partial filtering, such as first selecting movies with rating > 8 and then limiting to those with rating < 9, while maintaining clarity.

For the WHERE clause, the lecture discussed subqueries that express logical quantifiers. **Existential quantifiers** use EXISTS or IN to check whether related tuples exist or match specified conditions (e.g., finding actors who appeared in at least one Sci‑Fi movie). These can often be rewritten as joins. In contrast, **universal quantifiers** (“for all”) are more difficult: Slide 17 explicitly stated that the SQL constructs covered so far cannot directly represent them. To approximate universal conditions, the lecture showed workarounds using NOT IN or NOT EXISTS—such as finding actors who acted only in action movies or movies where all actor salaries exceed \$100K—but emphasized their complexity.

The lecture concluded by defining **monotone** queries as those whose results never lose tuples when input data grows. Simple SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone and can be safely unnested. Non‑monotone queries—those involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregation—cannot be unnested, reinforcing the conceptual and practical limits of nested SQL.

*(Word count: 393)*Overall winner: This lecture explored **nested SQL queries**, explaining how subqueries appear within different clauses and how their logic connects to optimization through *unnesting* and *monotonicity*. After a brief recap of core SQL features—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and data modifications—it defined a **subquery** as a query inside another query. Using nested‑loop semantics, any basic SELECT–FROM–WHERE query without subqueries, aggregates, or negation is monotone. The key takeaway is that **only monotone queries can be safely unnested**, while non‑monotone queries—those involving negation, aggregates, or universal quantifiers—cannot. *(≈397 words)*Overall winner: This week’s lecture advances from understanding accounting as a judgment-based discipline to mastering how financial information is systematically recorded and connected through the double-entry framework. It begins by reviewing the financial accounting framework and emphasizing that accounting interprets complex economic activities through structured communication—the four interrelated financial statements. The balance sheet represents a company’s financial position at a specific point in time, while the income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows report performance and changes over a period. These statements are linked: net income increases stockholders’ equity, while net loss, expenses, or dividends reduce it; revenues raise equity, and all statements ultimately reconcile changes between two balance sheet dates.  

Building on this linkage, the lecture revisits the accounting equation—Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity—and expands it to show that equity comprises contributed capital and retained earnings, which evolve through revenues, expenses, and dividends across periods. This expanded equation underpins the double-entry system, ensuring that every recorded event preserves balance.  

The core portion develops the mechanics of double-entry recordkeeping. Each economic event has two sides, a “give” and a “take,” meaning every transaction affects at least two accounts. Debits record increases in assets and decreases in liabilities or equity; credits do the opposite. Debits must always equal credits—no negative numbers are used—and every transaction must include at least one debit and one credit. Students apply these principles through sequential examples: borrowing funds from a bank, purchasing equipment and inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing shares. The lecture demonstrates how these actions are documented as journal entries and then posted to T-accounts, where ending balances are computed (using Beginning Balance + increases – decreases = Ending Balance). Those ending balances appear on the balance sheet and become the starting balances for the next period, illustrating continuous linkage across reporting cycles.  

The session concludes by summarizing the process: analyze each transaction, record debits and credits in the journal, and post entries to the ledger. The rule that debits equal credits maintains the balance sheet equation (Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity) and ensures internal consistency across all financial statements, reinforcing the discipline’s structure and reliability.

It began with a recap of SQL capabilities—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and modifications—before introducing subqueries as queries embedded within other queries. *(Word count: 399)*Overall winner: This week’s lecture progresses from reviewing how financial information is communicated across statements to mastering how transactions are recorded under the double-entry accounting framework. It begins by recapping the financial accounting structure introduced in Week 1, emphasizing that accounting translates complex economic activity into organized financial statements that interconnect to portray a company’s performance and position. The balance sheet shows financial position at a specific point, while the income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows describe changes over a fiscal period. Together, they link across time: revenues and net income increase stockholders’ equity, expenses, losses, or dividends decrease it, and each statement reconciles the movement between beginning and ending balance sheet dates.  

Building on this linkage, the lecture expands the accounting equation—Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity—showing that equity consists of contributed capital and retained earnings. Retained earnings evolve with revenues, expenses, and dividends, connecting operational results to future financial position. This expanded equation forms the foundation for the double-entry system, which ensures balance by recording every economic event with equal debits and credits.

Each transaction has two sides—a give and a take—and affects at least two accounts among Assets, Liabilities, or Stockholders’ Equity. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity, while credits do the reverse. Every entry contains at least one debit and one credit, and total debits must equal total credits; no negative numbers are used. Through step-by-step examples—borrowing funds, purchasing equipment and inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing shares—students learn to record transactions first as journal entries and then post them to T-accounts. Ending balances are calculated using the formula Beginning Balance + increases – decreases = Ending Balance and carried into the next period’s opening balances.  

To aid orientation, the lecture introduces the “Super T-account” summary and the mnemonic **DEALOR** (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debit increases; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue = Credit increases). These tools clarify debit-credit effects across all account types.  

The session concludes with the structured process: analyze each transaction, record the journal entry, and post to the ledger. Maintaining Debits = Credits preserves the core equation (Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity) and ensures consistency across all financial statements.  

**Word count: 374**Overall winner: This lecture examined nested and subqueries in SQL—queries that operate within other queries—and the theoretical limits of rewriting them, focusing on unnesting and monotonicity. Building on prior SQL topics such as projections, selections, joins, and aggregates, it began by defining subqueries as embedded queries that may appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. Subqueries can return scalar values or entire relations and are sometimes unavoidable, though the “rule of thumb” is to avoid them when possible for efficiency reasons.

In the **SELECT** clause, subqueries can compute derived values, such as a movie’s genre or average actor salary. These examples illustrated *correlated subqueries*, where the inner query depends on an attribute from the outer query. The lecture compared these forms to equivalent join-based queries, showing that unnesting (rewriting a nested query as a join) often yields simpler and faster results. However, slide 10 emphasized that equivalence fails in certain cases—particularly when outer joins are involved—because join semantics may include tuples absent in the subquery result.

In the **FROM** clause, subqueries serve as intermediate tables or “refactored” relations. This can be done inline or through **WITH** clauses (common table expressions), which improve readability but can still be flattened through unnesting without changing results.

In the **WHERE** clause, subqueries support logical tests using quantifiers. **EXISTS** and **IN** implement *existential quantifiers*, returning true if any tuple satisfies the condition (e.g., finding actors in at least one Sci‑Fi movie). Conversely, *universal quantifiers* express “for all” conditions, which SQL lacks native syntax for. The lecture demonstrated workarounds using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, such as retrieving actors who acted only in action films or movies where all salaries exceed $100K. These require multi-step reasoning and can’t always be simplified into joins.

The lecture concluded by linking these query types to **monotonicity**. Monotone queries—those whose outputs cannot decrease when new tuples are added—include basic SELECT-FROM-WHERE forms without subqueries or aggregates and can generally be unnested. By contrast, **non-monotone queries**, which involve negation, aggregation, or universal quantifiers, lose monotonicity and therefore cannot be safely unnested. This distinction grounds both the practical and theoretical limits of subquery optimization in SQL.

The course clarifies that this class focuses on *financial accounting* (external reporting), not *managerial accounting* (internal cost and budgeting analysis) or *financial statement analysis* (valuation).

The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** oversees reporting for publicly traded companies and delegates standard setting to the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**. Internationally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues **International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS)**. Public companies must file periodic reports: the **10‑K** (annual audited statements), **10‑Q** (quarterly unaudited reports), and **8‑K** (current reports on major events).

Independent **auditors**, required for public firms, evaluate whether statements comply with GAAP and are free from material misstatements. Their opinions—unqualified, unqualified with emphasis, qualified, or adverse—enhance the credibility of reports for decision makers.

Emphasizing early in the term, students are encouraged to engage actively and **practice consistently**, as “practice is critical” for mastering problem‑solving and exam performance. Success depends on working through exercises in full detail and participating by asking questions to strengthen understanding.

The week concludes with the **balance sheet**, which represents a snapshot of financial position based on the **accounting equation: Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**. Through examples—issuing stock, borrowing funds, purchasing equipment, paying rent, and buying supplies—students see how each transaction has a dual effect that always maintains this balance, grounding their understanding of how accounting organizes business information for external reporting.  

*(~397 words)*Overall winner: This week’s lecture moves from reviewing how financial statements communicate information to applying the double-entry system for recording transactions. It begins with a recap of the financial accounting framework, emphasizing that accounting organizes complex economic activity through interconnected statements that portray a company’s performance and position. The balance sheet captures financial position at a point in time, while the income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows report changes over a fiscal period. These statements link across time: revenues and net income raise stockholders’ equity, while expenses, losses, or dividends reduce it. Each statement helps explain the movement between beginning and ending balance sheet dates, showing that financial reports are interdependent rather than isolated snapshots.  

Building on this linkage, the lecture expands the accounting equation from **Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity** into its detailed form: **Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + Retained Earnings (beginning of period + Revenues – Expenses – Dividends)**. This formulation illustrates how equity evolves through operations and distributions, connecting profit generation directly to future financial position. The equation’s rearranged form provides a foundation for double-entry accounting, where every transaction keeps the equation in balance by using equal debits and credits.  

The central portion of the lecture focuses on the mechanics of double-entry recordkeeping. Each economic event affects at least two accounts—reflecting a “give” and a “take”—and must obey the principle that total debits equal total credits. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity, while credits produce the opposite effect. No negative numbers are used; the balancing occurs through offsetting entries. Step-by-step examples show transactions such as borrowing from a bank, buying equipment and inventory, paying suppliers, prepaying insurance, and issuing stock. After analyzing each transaction, it is recorded as a journal entry and posted to T-accounts. Ending balances are computed using the formula **Beginning Balance + increases – decreases = Ending Balance**, and the lecture explicitly demonstrates that these ending balances become the beginning balances for the next period—reinforcing the continuity principle.  

Tools like the “Super T-account” layout and the mnemonic **DEALOR** (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debit increases; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue = Credit increases) summarize debit-credit behaviors across all account categories. The session concludes with the structured accounting process: analyze, record, and post. Maintaining **Debits = Credits** ensures the equation **Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity** remains valid and guarantees consistency across the full set of financial statements.  

**Word count: 398**Overall winner: The opening lecture of *Principles of Accounting* introduces the foundations and purpose of financial accounting—how organizations record, summarize, and communicate business activities to support informed decisions. Accounting is described as the **“language of business”**, capturing a company’s financial story of performance, opportunities, and risks. It serves two essential functions: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records economic events in value terms, and **communication**, which provides this information to external users through financial statements.  

The lecture emphasizes the diverse **users of financial information** and how they rely on accounting data. Investors judge company performance and value; creditors evaluate repayment ability; suppliers and customers assess reliability; management and boards of directors use reports to monitor operations and strategic goals; and employees, regulators, analysts, and competitors use financial statements to inform decisions.  

Financial accounting measures three types of **business activities**:  
- **Financing activities**, which include transactions with investors and creditors such as issuing stock or borrowing funds.  
- **Investing activities**, involving the acquisition or disposal of assets like equipment, land, and inventory.  
- **Operating activities**, relating to daily transactions for producing and selling goods or services.  

The results of these activities are summarized in four key **financial statements**—the *Balance Sheet*, *Income Statement*, *Statement of Stockholders’ Equity*, and *Statement of Cash Flows*. These reports communicate a company’s financial position (resources and claims), performance, and cash movements to external users.  

Financial reporting must follow **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)** to ensure consistency and comparability. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** enforces reporting for public firms and delegates standard-setting authority to the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**. Internationally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues the IFRS framework. Independent **auditors** evaluate whether statements comply with GAAP and are free of material misstatements, providing one of four opinions: unqualified, unqualified with an explanatory paragraph, qualified, or adverse—each affecting the credibility of financial disclosures.  

A central focus is the **Balance Sheet**, which presents a company’s financial position at a point in time, governed by the **accounting equation: Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**. Assets represent resources with future benefits; liabilities, obligations to creditors; and equity, owners’ residual claims. Through examples using the fictional company *Klein, Inc.*, the lecture illustrates how issuing stock, securing loans, purchasing equipment, prepaying rent, or buying supplies on credit each affect the equation while maintaining its balance.  

*(Word count: 395)*Overall winner: This lecture focused on **nested and subqueries in SQL**—queries contained within other queries—and the theoretical principles that determine when they can be rewritten or “unnested.” Building on earlier SQL concepts such as projections, selections, joins, and grouping, the lecture introduced subqueries as embedded queries that may appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. Subqueries can return a single value or an entire relation, and although they are sometimes necessary, the rule of thumb is to avoid them when possible for clarity and performance.

In the **SELECT** clause, subqueries can compute derived attributes, such as a movie’s genre or the average salary of its actors. Many of these are *correlated subqueries*, meaning the inner query references data from the outer query. The lecture compared these with equivalent join-based queries to illustrate **subquery unnesting**, which can simplify and speed up query evaluation. However, as shown in examples comparing join and outer join versions, not all subqueries are strictly equivalent once nulls or missing tuples are introduced.

In the **FROM** clause, subqueries can define intermediate results—sometimes referred to as *sub-query refactoring*. These can be written either inline or through a **WITH** (common table expression) structure. Although they can often be replaced by direct filtering and joining, equivalence depends on context, so unnesting must be applied carefully.

The **WHERE** clause introduced logical quantifiers in SQL. Constructs like **EXISTS** and **IN** represent *existential quantifiers* (“there exists at least one”), such as finding actors who have appeared in Sci‑Fi movies. More complex are *universal quantifiers* (“for all”), which SQL does not natively support. The lecture showed how these can be expressed indirectly with **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, for example when retrieving actors who acted only in action films or movies whose actors all earn above $100K. These formulations require nested logic and cannot always be transformed into simple joins.

The lecture concluded by introducing **monotonicity**—queries whose results never lose tuples when input data grows. Basic SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone. As shown in the final slides, *if a query is monotone, its nested forms can be unnested; if it is non‑monotone, they cannot*. Queries involving negation, aggregation, or universal quantifiers break monotonicity, marking a fundamental limit on subquery unnesting and optimization in SQL.

*(~394 words)*Overall winner: This lecture extended prior SQL topics into **nested queries and subqueries**, showing how they function in different clauses and how their structure relates to efficiency and logic. It began by reviewing fundamental SQL operations—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and grouping—before introducing **subqueries**, defined as queries that appear within another query. Subqueries can occur in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses, returning either individual values or full relations. They are often necessary to express conditions depending on computed tables but are generally less efficient; thus, the lecture emphasized **subquery unnesting**, where nested queries are rewritten as equivalent join or aggregation statements for better performance.

In the **SELECT clause**, examples compared a correlated subquery (e.g., retrieving each actor’s movie genre or average salary) with an equivalent join-based version. Repeated side‑by‑side examples showed how these yield identical results when properly structured but may differ when outer joins are needed. This visual demonstration underlined that unnesting is not only possible but preferred whenever the queries remain semantically equivalent.  
In the **FROM clause**, subqueries were used as intermediate or temporary relations—either directly as nested selects or defined via common table expressions (CTEs)—illustrating how such structures can simplify multistep filtering like selecting movies with ratings between specific values. Equivalent unnested queries were shown to produce the same results more directly.

Subqueries in the **WHERE clause** served to illustrate logical quantifiers. **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) used `EXISTS` or `IN` to test whether related tuples exist, while their **unnested join equivalents** confirmed the same logic with explicit relations. The lecture then turned to **universal quantifiers** (“for all”), which SQL lacks direct syntax to express. Through multi‑step examples, it showed how `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS` constructs emulate “for all” logic—such as finding actors who acted only in action movies or movies whose actors all earn above \$100K—and explicitly noted that such universal quantifier forms are **not supported in SQLite**.

The lecture concluded with the idea of **monotone versus non‑monotone queries**. Monotone queries (plain SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE without subqueries or aggregates) never lose results when data is added, making unnesting feasible. In contrast, **non‑monotone queries**—those involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregation—cannot generally be unnested. This distinction connects optimization potential with the underlying logical behavior of SQL queries.

**(Word count: 396)**Overall winner: This lecture covered **nested and subqueries in SQL**—queries contained within others—and examined when they can be simplified or *unnested*. Building on SQL fundamentals such as projections, selections, joins, grouping, and aggregation, the lecture defined a **subquery** as an embedded query that may appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. Subqueries can return either a single value or an entire relation. While sometimes unavoidable, the general rule is to avoid nesting when possible for performance and clarity.

In the **SELECT** clause, subqueries can compute derived information—for example, listing each actor with the genre of their movie or determining the average salary of actors in highly rated films. These are often *correlated subqueries*, where the inner query depends on the outer one. The lecture compared these with equivalent **join-based expressions** to demonstrate *subquery unnesting*. However, slides showed that seemingly equivalent rewrites—such as switching from a nested query to an inner join or outer join—do not always yield identical results. When data contain null values or missing matches, an outer join may preserve more rows, highlighting that unnesting can change semantics in such cases.

In the **FROM** clause, subqueries can create intermediate tables, a technique called *subquery refactoring*. Using either inline subqueries or the **WITH** clause (common table expressions), SQL can define temporary relations for further filtering. These refactored forms are often equivalent to direct selection and join operations but, as shown, can also be unnested under equivalent filtering conditions.

The **WHERE** clause introduced subqueries representing logical quantifiers. Constructs such as **EXISTS** or **IN** implement *existential quantifiers* (“there exists at least one”), as in finding actors who appeared in Sci‑Fi movies. More complex are *universal quantifiers* (“for all”), which SQL cannot express directly but can emulate using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, such as finding actors who acted only in action movies or movies where all actors earned above \$100K. These rely on negation and typically resist unnesting.

The lecture concluded with **monotonicity**, a theory describing how query outputs behave when input tables gain tuples. Basic SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone—they never lose results as data expand. Demonstrations showed that adding tuples to non‑monotone queries (those using negation, aggregation, or universal quantifiers) can remove results, proving that **only monotone queries can be safely unnested**, while non‑monotone ones cannot.

(Word count: 397)Overall winner: This lecture deepens the foundations of financial accounting by showing how economic events are translated into structured financial information through judgment, analysis, and balanced recording. It begins by emphasizing that because economic activity is complex, accounting requires careful judgment and critical thinking to interpret transactions and communicate an organization’s financial position and performance.  

The session reviews the four interrelated financial statements. The balance sheet presents a firm’s resources and obligations at specific points in time—the beginning and end of a fiscal year—while the income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows summarize value-generating and financing activities over the year. These statements are inherently connected: net income from the income statement increases stockholders’ equity, and the statement of cash flows and equity statement explain the changes between two balance sheet dates. Revenues raise retained earnings, while expenses and dividends reduce it, linking operational results to equity growth over periods.  

Building on this connection, the lecture expands the basic accounting equation into a more detailed form:  
**Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + Retained Earnings (Beginning of Period + Revenues – Expenses – Dividends).**  
This equation illustrates how income and distributions modify equity and shows that every transaction must keep total resources and claims in balance.  

The core focus then turns to the double-entry system, in which each transaction has equal debit and credit effects. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity, while credits do the opposite. Students practice translating transactions—including borrowing, equipment purchases, inventory acquisition, supplier payments, prepaid insurance, and stock issuance—into T-accounts that visualize the dual effects. All transactions must satisfy the rule that debits equal credits, ensuring the accounting equation always holds.  

After recording, ending T-account balances become the amounts reported on the current balance sheet and serve as the beginning balances for the next period, illustrating continuity across accounting cycles. The lecture concludes by summarizing the accounting process: analyze transactions, record debits and credits in the journal, post them to the ledger, and verify that total debits equal total credits. This disciplined sequence preserves accuracy and highlights how professional judgment underlies every stage of measurement and reporting.

*(≈ 385 words)*Overall winner: This lecture extended SQL fundamentals to **nested queries and subqueries**, emphasizing their use across clauses and the logical properties that determine when they can be simplified or “unnested.” It opened with a review of core SQL operations—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and data modifications—then defined a **subquery** as a query embedded within another. The lecture illustrated this by filtering movies with ratings between 8 and 9 through a nested FROM clause or through an equivalent unnested query, demonstrating how subqueries can improve readability without changing semantics.

**(Word count: 389)**Overall winner: Within-subjects designs offer a powerful alternative to between-groups designs by allowing each participant to serve as their own comparison. In a between-groups design, participants contribute data to only one condition, so individual differences—such as ability or motivation—introduce noise that can obscure effects. Within-subjects designs reduce this nuisance variability because every participant experiences all conditions, and analyses focus on differences within each person. This approach increases statistical power and efficiency, enabling researchers to detect effects with fewer participants.

Several major forms of within-subjects designs were covered. **Pretest–posttest designs** assess change across an intervention, **repeated‑measures designs** expose participants to multiple conditions within one session, and **longitudinal designs** assess repeated measurements over extended periods. These approaches are especially useful when changes occur within individuals over time, but longitudinal work can be slow and resource‑intensive.

The benefits of within-subjects designs—fewer participants and lower variability—come with important challenges. Because participants experience all conditions, they may guess an experiment’s purpose (**demand characteristics**) or exhibit **carryover effects**, where one condition influences the next. Carryover effects can reflect **fatigue** (decline in later tasks), **practice** (improvement from repetition), or external and temporal confounds such as **history**, **maturation**, and **testing** effects. For example, events between sessions or natural development might mimic an experimental effect, while taking the same test twice can alter performance through familiarity.

**Order effects** arise when the sequence of conditions itself affects outcomes. Researchers manage these by **counterbalancing**—systematically varying the order of conditions across participants. In **complete within‑subjects designs**, every participant experiences all possible orders, such as the **ABBA design** for two conditions or **block randomization** when there are three or more. However, the number of possible sequences grows factorially (e.g., 3! = 6 orders, 4! = 24, 5! = 120), making complete counterbalancing impractical for many conditions.

When full counterbalancing is infeasible, **incomplete within‑subjects designs** offer efficient alternatives. Methods like **random order with rotation** assign each participant a unique starting point so that all conditions appear equally often in each position. **Latin‑square designs** further ensure that each condition occupies every ordinal position and follows each other condition equally often, balancing order effects while limiting the number of participants required.

In cognitive science research, tasks with many short trials may instead **intermix** conditions randomly, which tends to cancel out order effects. Yet counterbalancing remains preferable when tasks are long or complex. Some manipulations—like taking a drug, undergoing surgery, or learning a specific skill—cannot be undone, setting practical limits on counterbalancing. Altogether, within-subjects designs provide an efficient, statistically sensitive framework for comparing conditions, provided researchers actively control for carryover and ordering influences.

(≈400 words)Overall winner: The Week 1 lecture of *Principles of Accounting* introduces the core purpose, structure, and foundational concepts of **financial accounting**. Accounting is presented as the **language of business**, used to tell a company’s economic story through measurable data that convey its successes, challenges, and financial position. It serves two key functions: **recordkeeping**, which identifies, measures, and records transactions in monetary terms, and **communication**, which reports that information to external decision‑makers through standardized financial statements.  

Accounting information supports a broad audience. **Investors** and **creditors** assess management performance and a company’s ability to meet obligations, while **suppliers**, **management**, and **boards of directors** use reports for evaluating operations, strategy, and financial policy. Additional users include **employees** gauging job security, **customers** judging reliability, and other stakeholders such as analysts, regulators, and competitors.  

The lecture explains that financial accounting measures and reports three types of business activities: **financing** (transactions with investors and lenders), **investing** (acquiring or disposing of resources for operations), and **operating** (day‑to‑day production, marketing, and sales). These activities are communicated through four primary **financial statements**—the **Balance Sheet**, **Income Statement**, **Statement of Stockholders’ Equity**, and **Statement of Cash Flows**—which appear in reports such as the annual **10‑K**, quarterly **10‑Q**, and event‑driven **8‑K** filings.  

All reporting follows **Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP)** to ensure comparability and consistency. The **Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC)** oversees public company reporting and delegates standard‑setting authority to the **Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB)**. Internationally, the **International Accounting Standards Board (IASB)** issues **IFRS**. Independent **auditors**, required for publicly traded firms, examine statements for adherence to GAAP and issue opinions—unqualified, qualified, or adverse—on their accuracy.  

The session concludes with the **Balance Sheet**, which presents a company’s financial position at a particular date. It must always satisfy the **accounting equation: Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity**. **Assets** are resources providing future economic benefit, **liabilities** are obligations owed to creditors, and **equity** represents owners’ residual claims. Through the *Klein Inc.* examples—raising capital from investors, borrowing from a bank, acquiring equipment, prepaying rent, and purchasing supplies on account—students see how each transaction affects at least two accounts yet keeps the equation balanced.  

This lecture establishes the conceptual and mechanical framework of financial accounting that underpins future topics such as double‑entry recording, the accounting cycle, and financial statement preparation.  

*(≈ 395 words)*Overall winner: This lecture extended SQL fundamentals to **nested queries and subqueries**, emphasizing their use across clauses and the logical properties that determine when they can be simplified or “unnested.” It opened with a review of core SQL operations—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and data modifications—then defined a **subquery** as a query embedded within another. Subqueries may appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and can yield either single values or entire relations. They are essential when a condition depends on data computed by another query, though the slides stressed the **rule of thumb**: avoid nested queries when possible, but sometimes they are necessary.  

In the **SELECT clause**, examples contrasted simple correlated subqueries—where the inner query references values from the outer query—with equivalent uncorrelated join or group‑by forms. For instance, determining each actor’s movie genre or average salary was shown both with a correlated subquery and with an equivalent join. These comparisons introduced **subquery unnesting**, highlighting that certain correlated subqueries can be rewritten for efficiency, while others require outer joins to remain logically equivalent.  

Subqueries in the **FROM clause** acted as intermediate relations or **refactored subqueries**, sometimes expressed via **common table expressions (CTEs)**. The lecture illustrated this by filtering movies with ratings between 8 and 9 through a nested FROM clause or through an equivalent unnested query, demonstrating how subqueries can improve readability without changing semantics.  

In the **WHERE clause**, subqueries revealed how SQL expresses **quantifiers**. **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) were implemented using `EXISTS` or `IN` and shown to behave equivalently to standard joins. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) were more complex, since SQL lacks a direct construct. The slides expressed them with negation—using `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS`—to capture meanings such as finding actors who acted only in action movies or movies where all actors earned over \$100K. SQLite’s limitations with certain constructs were also noted.  

Finally, the lecture examined **monotone vs. non‑monotone queries**. Through successive data table examples, it demonstrated that adding tuples to monotone queries (e.g., positive joins) never removes existing results, whereas non‑monotone queries that include negation or universal quantifiers can lose tuples. A concluding theorem formalized this: basic SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone and thus safely **unnestable**, but those with non‑monotone constructs cannot be.  

**(Word count: 389)**Overall winner: Within-subjects designs offer a powerful alternative to between-groups designs by allowing each participant to serve as their own comparison. In a between-groups design, participants contribute data to only one condition, so individual differences—such as ability or motivation—introduce noise that can obscure effects. Within-subjects designs reduce this nuisance variability because every participant experiences all conditions, and analyses focus on differences within each person. This approach increases statistical power and efficiency, enabling researchers to detect effects with fewer participants.

Several major forms of within-subjects designs were covered. **Pretest–posttest designs** assess change across an intervention, **repeated‑measures designs** expose participants to multiple conditions within one session, and **longitudinal designs** assess repeated measurements over extended periods. These approaches are especially useful when changes occur within individuals over time, but longitudinal work can be slow and resource‑intensive.

The benefits of within-subjects designs—fewer participants and lower variability—come with important challenges. Because participants experience all conditions, they may guess an experiment’s purpose (**demand characteristics**) or exhibit **carryover effects**, where one condition influences the next. Carryover effects can reflect **fatigue** (decline in later tasks), **practice** (improvement from repetition), or external and temporal confounds such as **history**, **maturation**, and **testing** effects. For example, events between sessions or natural development might mimic an experimental effect, while taking the same test twice can alter performance through familiarity.

**Order effects** arise when the sequence of conditions itself affects outcomes. Researchers manage these by **counterbalancing**—systematically varying the order of conditions across participants. In **complete within‑subjects designs**, every participant experiences all possible orders, such as the **ABBA design** for two conditions or **block randomization** when there are three or more. However, the number of possible sequences grows factorially (e.g., 3! = 6 orders, 4! = 24, 5! = 120), making complete counterbalancing impractical for many conditions.

When full counterbalancing is infeasible, **incomplete within‑subjects designs** offer efficient alternatives. Methods like **random order with rotation** assign each participant a unique starting point so that all conditions appear equally often in each position. **Latin‑square designs** further ensure that each condition occupies every ordinal position and follows each other condition equally often, balancing order effects while limiting the number of participants required.

In cognitive science research, tasks with many short trials may instead **intermix** conditions randomly, which tends to cancel out order effects. Yet counterbalancing remains preferable when tasks are long or complex. Some manipulations—like taking a drug, undergoing surgery, or learning a specific skill—cannot be undone, setting practical limits on counterbalancing. Altogether, within-subjects designs provide an efficient, statistically sensitive framework for comparing conditions, provided researchers actively control for carryover and ordering influences.

(≈400 words)Overall winner: This lecture deepens students’ understanding of core accounting mechanics by connecting the financial statements, expanding the accounting equation, and applying double-entry principles through transaction examples. It begins by reaffirming that accounting interprets complex economic activity through judgment and structured communication. The four financial statements—balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—serve as the foundation of this communication. The balance sheet captures a company’s position at a specific point, while the other three statements describe flows over a fiscal period. These reports are interlinked: the income statement explains how revenues and expenses produce net income, which adjusts retained earnings and therefore shareholders’ equity, linking to both the balance sheet and the statement of cash flows across periods.  

The accounting equation—Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity—is then expanded to show that equity consists of contributed capital and retained earnings. Retained earnings evolve throughout each period as Beginning Retained Earnings + Revenues – Expenses – Dividends, aligning with slides that depict how revenues and expenses respectively increase and decrease stockholders’ equity. Rearranging this equation demonstrates that every transaction must keep the two sides equal, a concept that underlies double-entry accounting.  

The lecture highlights that every economic event has two sides—a give and a take—and must be recorded through at least one debit and one credit of equal amount. Debits increase assets, expenses, and dividends while credits increase liabilities, common stock (equity), and revenues. This directional logic is summarized in the mnemonic **D E A L O R**: **Dividends, Expenses, Assets** (normal debit balances) versus **Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue** (normal credit balances).  

Step-by-step transaction examples illustrate these mechanics: borrowing $100,000 from a bank, purchasing equipment for $60,000 in cash, buying $15,000 of inventory on account, paying $8,000 of that liability, prepaying $600 for insurance, and issuing $100,000 of common stock. Each case shows balanced impacts among assets, liabilities, and equity through journal entries and T‑accounts. Ending balances formed from these T‑accounts carry forward as the next period’s beginning balances, maintaining the continuity of the accounting cycle.  

The lecture concludes by formalizing the accounting process: analyze each transaction, journalize it with equal debits and credits, and post to the ledger. This “debits = credits” rule ensures that the accounting equation and all financial statements remain internally consistent.

**(Approx. 395 words)**Overall winner: This lecture extended the course’s coverage of SQL by focusing on nested and subqueries—the embedding of one query inside another—and showed how their structure and semantics affect performance and expressiveness. Building on earlier SQL concepts such as projections, selections, joins, and aggregates, it examined where subqueries can appear, how they behave, and when they can be rewritten or “unnested.”  

A subquery may occur in the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clause, returning either a single value or a relation. In the **SELECT clause**, subqueries enable attribute-level computations such as finding each actor’s movie genre, counting the number of actors per film, or computing a movie’s average salary. The slides demonstrated that these patterns can often be rewritten with joins and GROUP BY clauses to improve efficiency, a process called *subquery unnesting*. However, equivalence is not always guaranteed; for example, outer joins can yield different results compared with correlated subqueries.  

Subqueries in the **FROM clause** act as intermediate or derived tables. They can be expressed as inline subqueries or as *common table expressions (CTEs)* using WITH, which improves readability and allows stepwise query construction. Both are logically equivalent to filtering directly in the WHERE clause but highlight how computation can be modularized.  

Subqueries in the **WHERE clause** introduce logical quantifiers. *Existential quantifiers*—implemented with EXISTS or IN—test whether matching tuples exist or whether a value occurs in a set. These can often be rewritten as join queries that are functionally equivalent and more efficient.  

*Universal quantifiers* (“for all”) are trickier because SQL lacks direct syntax for them. Examples such as retrieving actors who only performed in action movies or movies where all actors earn more than $100 K used NOT IN or NOT EXISTS patterns to simulate universal conditions. Some formulations—like those employing < ALL—are unsupported in SQLite, stressing practical system limitations. These queries require multi-step reasoning, such as finding violations and excluding them, reflecting their greater logical complexity.  

The lecture concluded with **monotonicity**, the property that adding tuples to input relations never removes tuples from results. Using nested-loop semantics, it proved that a SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE query *without aggregates or subqueries* is monotone. Monotone queries can be safely unnested, while non-monotone ones—those involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates—cannot. This distinction links query logic to optimization feasibility and explains why some nested forms must remain nested.

Building on prior topics such as projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and updates, the session introduced subqueries—queries embedded inside others—to express conditions or computations that depend on intermediate results. A central rule of thumb was highlighted early: **avoid nested queries when possible**, though in some cases they are unavoidable for expressing certain dependencies between tables.

Non‑monotone queries, which involve negation, universal quantification, or aggregation, may lose results when data are added and thus cannot be unnested. Understanding this distinction clarifies both the limits of query optimization and the expressive boundaries of SQL.

*(≈395 words)*Overall winner: This lecture reinforces the financial accounting framework from Week 1 and introduces the mechanics of double‑entry recordkeeping.
