These exercises illustrate how T‑accounts record increases and decreases, calculate ending balances, and roll them into beginning balances for the next period.  

Finally, the lecture formalizes the accounting process: analyze each transaction, journalize debits and credits, and post to the ledger (T‑accounts). The mnemonic **DEALOR**—Dividends, Expenses, Assets = normal debits; Liabilities, Owners’ equity, Revenue = normal credits—summarizes account behavior. Consistently applying **Debits = Credits** preserves the integrity of the accounting equation and links all financial statements within the framework.  (≈398 words)Overall winner: This lecture focused on **nested SQL queries**, explaining how subqueries operate within larger queries and how they relate to query rewriting and logical reasoning. Building on earlier SQL concepts—projections, selections, joins, grouping, and updates—the session introduced subqueries as a means to express conditions or computations that depend on intermediate results. A key guideline emphasized early was to **avoid nested queries when possible**, though certain dependencies make them necessary.

Subqueries can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. In the SELECT clause, they may return a single value—such as a movie’s genre for each actor or the average salary of actors in a film. These correlated subqueries can often be replaced with joins or grouped aggregates through **subquery unnesting**, which improves efficiency. However, as shown when comparing inner and outer joins, equivalent results require careful handling—specifically, using a LEFT OUTER JOIN when preserving unmatched records.

In the FROM clause, subqueries can define intermediate tables for cleaner logic, as illustrated by filtering movies with ratings between 8 and 9 using either an inline view or a **Common Table Expression (CTE)**. These are *not correlated* and can be flattened without changing results.

Most conceptually, subqueries within the **WHERE** clause connected SQL to logical quantifiers. **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) were captured through `EXISTS` or `IN`, allowing checks for whether related tuples exist—for example, identifying actors who appeared in any Sci‑Fi movie. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) required more involved transformations combining negation and `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS`. Slides 18–20 demonstrated this stepwise: first identifying actors linked to any non‑Action movie, then retrieving those not in that set—yielding actors who acted *only* in Action films. The same logic extended to queries such as finding movies where all actors’ salaries exceed $100 K.

The lecture concluded with the concept of **monotone versus non‑monotone queries**. Using examples that traced result changes as data were added (Slides 30–32), it showed that monotone queries—simple SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE forms without subqueries or aggregates—never lose outputs as data grow and can therefore be safely unnested. In contrast, queries involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregation are non‑monotone, meaning results may shrink when inputs expand. Recognizing this distinction explains both the limits of unnesting and the logical boundaries of SQL’s expressive power.  

*(≈392 words)*Overall winner: This lecture focuses on within-subjects designs as an alternative to between-groups approaches for comparing experimental conditions. In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data in only one condition, introducing individual differences that add noise and can obscure effects. A within-subjects design, by contrast, has each participant experience all conditions, enabling researchers to compare individuals to themselves. This reduces participant-related variability and increases statistical power, allowing for fewer participants while maintaining the same number of observations per condition.

Several forms of within-subjects designs were discussed. A *pretest–posttest design* involves measuring participants before and after an intervention. A *repeated-measures design* includes two or more conditions presented within one session, such as varying stimulus intensity across trials. A *longitudinal design* assesses participants across extended time points to track developmental change. The major benefits of these designs are greater efficiency and reduced nuisance variability, producing more powerful statistical tests.

However, within-subjects designs present potential drawbacks. Participants might guess the study’s purpose (demand characteristics) or be influenced by *carryover effects*, in which earlier conditions affect later performance. Carryover can appear as *fatigue* (decline over time) or *practice* (improvement through familiarity). Other time-related threats include *history effects* (external events occurring during the study), *maturation effects* (natural developmental or aging changes), and *testing effects* (familiarity with test content upon retesting).

*Order effects* represent a major concern—when the sequence of conditions changes outcomes. To manage these, researchers use *counterbalancing*, deliberately varying or rotating condition orders. In *complete within-subjects designs*, every possible order is used. For instance, an *ABBA design* (for two conditions) balances practice effects if learning occurs gradually, although it does not control for very rapid learning. For three or more conditions, *block randomization* presents all conditions once per block and randomizes block sequences. When all orders are impractical, *incomplete designs* are used, such as *random order with rotation* or the *Latin-square design*, which ensures each condition occurs equally often in each position and follows every other condition.

In cognitive science research with many short trials, conditions are often *intermixed*—randomly presented—to cancel order effects directly. Counterbalancing remains preferable when intermixing would confuse participants or when tasks are lengthy. Finally, counterbalancing has clear limits: some manipulations cannot be reversed, such as taking a drug, undergoing surgery, or learning something permanently.  

**(Approx. 395 words)**Overall winner: This lecture explored **nested and subqueries in SQL** and their connection to logical reasoning about when such queries can be rewritten—or *unnested*. It built on prior SQL fundamentals (projections, selections, joins, grouping, and aggregation) before focusing on the role and structure of subqueries. A **subquery** is a query embedded within another and can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clause. It may return either a single value or a relation. The guiding rule of thumb stated that nested queries should be avoided *when possible*—not mainly for performance reasons, but because equivalent and simpler logic can often be expressed using joins or aggregation. Still, some problems necessitate nesting to capture certain logical dependencies.

Subqueries in the **SELECT** clause introduced **correlated subqueries**, where the inner query references attributes from the outer query. Examples included retrieving each actor’s movie genre, the average actor salary per highly rated movie, or counting actors per film. These demonstrations showed how **subquery unnesting** transforms correlated or nested formulations into equivalent join or aggregation queries, emphasizing that equivalence may differ if outer joins are needed.

Subqueries in the **FROM** clause function as **derived tables** that produce intermediate results, sometimes rewritten as **common table expressions (CTEs)** for clarity and modularity. This process, described as *subquery refactoring*, structures computations into logical steps even when unnesting yields the same outcome.

In the **WHERE** clause, subqueries express logical conditions using **quantifiers**. **Existential quantifiers** were illustrated through `EXISTS` and `IN`, which check whether at least one tuple satisfies a predicate—e.g., finding actors who appeared in Sci‑Fi movies. These could often be flattened into equivalent join-based forms. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) were more challenging: there is no direct operator in standard SQL, but similar meaning can be achieved through `NOT IN`, `NOT EXISTS`, or `< ALL`. The slides highlighted this with the example of retrieving movies where *all actors’ salaries exceed $100K*, noting that direct use of `< ALL` is *not supported in SQLite*.

The lecture concluded with the concepts of **monotone and non‑monotone queries**. A query is *monotone* if adding input tuples never removes output tuples. Slides formally justified this using nested-loop semantics, where inserting a new tuple cannot eliminate results. Monotone queries can be safely unnested, while non-monotone ones—those involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates—cannot.  
*(≈397 words)*Overall winner: This lecture revisits the financial accounting framework from Week 1 and develops the mechanics of **double‑entry recordkeeping**. It begins by noting that economic activity is complex, requiring judgment and critical thinking. The four primary financial statements—the **balance sheet**, **income statement**, **statement of stockholders’ equity**, and **statement of cash flows**—work together to communicate how a company’s resources, obligations, and results evolve. The balance sheet reports assets, liabilities, and equity at a point in time, while the income, equity, and cash‑flow statements describe performance across a fiscal period. The lecture highlights their interconnection: net income from the income statement increases stockholders’ equity, the equity statement reflects this change, and the cash‑flow statement explains variations in cash between consecutive balance‑sheet dates.  

The **accounting equation** expands from its basic form (Assets = Liabilities + Stockholders’ Equity) to identify components of equity—**contributed capital** and **retained earnings**—and to show temporal flow:  
Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + Retained Earnings (beginning + Revenues − Expenses − Dividends).  
This formulation connects revenues and expenses to net income, which then alters retained earnings and total equity.  

**Double‑entry accounting** ensures each economic event affects at least two accounts so that the equation remains balanced. A **debit** increases assets and decreases liabilities or equity, while a **credit** does the opposite. Every transaction must contain at least one debit and one credit, no negative numbers are used, and total debits must equal total credits. Through numerical examples—borrowing $100 000 from a bank, purchasing $60 000 of equipment for cash, buying $15 000 of inventory on account, paying $8 000 to a supplier, prepaying $600 for insurance, and issuing 10 000 shares for $100 000—students trace how these entries flow through T‑accounts. Each T‑account follows the relationship **BB + increases − decreases = EB**, illustrating how ending balances carry forward as beginning balances in the next period.  

The lecture then introduces **journalization**, demonstrating how transactions are first analyzed, then recorded as journal entries (debits listed first, credits indented), and finally **posted to the ledger** as T‑accounts to compute balances. The mnemonic **DEALOR** (Dividends, Expenses, and Assets → debits; Liabilities, Owners’ equity, and Revenue → credits) summarizes account behavior. Applying “**Debits = Credits**” throughout preserves the accounting equation’s integrity and ties all financial statements within a coherent reporting framework.  (≈395 words)Overall winner: This lecture explained within-subjects designs as a contrast to between-groups designs. In a between-groups design, each participant experiences only one condition, so differences among participants add nuisance variability (“noise”) that can mask real effects. In a within-subjects design, each participant provides data for all conditions and is compared to themselves across conditions. This removes much of the participant-related noise and allows researchers to obtain the same number of data points per condition with fewer participants. The design is thus more efficient and statistically powerful.

Several types of within-subjects designs were introduced. A *pretest–posttest design* measures participants before and after an intervention, often to gauge change caused by that manipulation. A *repeated-measures design* involves two or more conditions within one session, such as different stimulus levels. A *longitudinal design* measures participants at multiple time points to track change across longer timescales. All share the benefit of needing fewer participants and lowering nuisance variability.

Potential drawbacks include *demand characteristics*, when participants deduce the purpose of the experiment, and *carryover effects*, when early experiences influence later performance. Carryover can take the form of *fatigue* (poorer later performance) or *practice* (improved performance through familiarity). Additional time-based threats include:  
• *History effects*—external events occurring during the experiment, such as midterms or major events, that coincide with the study manipulation.  
• *Maturation effects*—naturally occurring changes (e.g., child development or sensory decline) that may mimic treatment effects.  
• *Testing effects*—performance changes from repeated exposure to the same test items.

*Order effects* occur when the sequence of conditions itself affects results. To manage these, researchers use *counterbalancing*—systematically varying order or rotation. In *complete within-subjects designs*, every possible order is used. The *ABBA design* (two conditions) controls for gradual practice effects, while *block randomization* (three or more conditions) randomizes blocks containing all conditions once each. When full counterbalancing is impractical, *incomplete designs* like *random order with rotation* or the *Latin-square design* ensure each condition appears equally often in each position and follows each other condition. Although counterbalancing can become complex quickly, it is worth the effort to reduce confounds. In cognitive science, researchers sometimes intermix trials to average out order effects directly, reserving counterbalancing for tasks where intermixing would be confusing or when each trial is lengthy. Nonetheless, not all manipulations can be reversed—such as taking a drug or learning a skill—so counterbalancing has definite limits.

*(≈392 words)*Overall winner: This lecture focused on **nested SQL queries**, explaining how subqueries operate within larger queries and how they connect to logical quantifiers and query rewriting. Building on prior SQL tools—projections, selections, joins, grouping, and modifications—it introduced subqueries as a way to express conditions or computations that depend on intermediate results. A key **rule of thumb** emphasized early was to **avoid nested queries when possible — but sometimes it’s impossible**, since some conditions intrinsically require them.  

Subqueries can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. In the SELECT clause, a subquery can yield a single value, such as the movie genre for each actor or the average salary of actors in a high‑rated film. These are typically *correlated subqueries*, dependent on the outer query. Through **subquery unnesting**, such constructs can often be rewritten as joins or grouped aggregates for efficiency. However, care is required when unnesting—particularly when unmatched tuples must be maintained—so **LEFT OUTER JOINS** may be necessary to preserve equivalence.  

In the FROM clause, subqueries act as intermediate relations for clearer logic. The example of filtering movies with ratings between 8 and 9 illustrated **subquery refactoring**, either by an inline view or by a **Common Table Expression (CTE)**. These are *not correlated* and can be flattened safely, since the inner subquery’s output is self‑contained.  

Subqueries in the **WHERE** clause linked SQL evaluation to logical **quantifiers**. **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) appear through conditions like `EXISTS` or `IN`, allowing checks for related tuples—for instance, finding actors who appeared in some Sci‑Fi film. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) are expressed by combining negation and set exclusion (e.g., `NOT IN` or `NOT EXISTS`). Slides 18–20 demonstrated this using a two‑step method: (1) identify actors who participated in any non‑Action movie, then (2) select those not in that set—yielding actors who acted *only* in Action movies. Similar logic applies to finding movies where all actors’ salaries exceed $100 K.  

The lecture concluded with **monotone and non‑monotone queries**. A formal theorem stated: *If Q is a SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE query with no subqueries and no aggregates, then it is monotone.* Such queries never lose results when new data are added and thus can be safely unnested. Conversely, queries involving **negation**, **universal quantifiers**, or **aggregates** are **non‑monotone**, meaning their results may shrink as data grow. Recognizing this distinction clarifies when unnesting is possible and delineates SQL’s logical boundaries.

*(Word count: 385)*Overall winner: This lecture deepens understanding of how financial statements connect and introduces the mechanics of double-entry bookkeeping. Net income increases equity, and revenues and expenses directly raise or reduce retained earnings.

Equity is separated into contributed capital and retained earnings, which adjust through revenues, expenses, and dividends. The extended form—assets = liabilities + contributed capital + beginning retained earnings + revenues – expenses – dividends—shows how performance and distributions alter financial position across periods.

Debits represent increases in assets and decreases in liabilities or equity; credits do the opposite. Each transaction requires at least one debit and one credit, and total debits must equal total credits. Concrete examples illustrate these principles: borrowing $100,000 from a bank increases both cash (asset) and notes payable (liability); buying equipment for $60,000 cash shifts assets within the balance sheet; purchasing $15,000 of inventory on account adds both assets and liabilities; paying an $8,000 supplier obligation reduces each; prepaying $600 for insurance exchanges one asset for another; and issuing 10,000 shares for $100,000 raises both cash and common stock.  

After recording entries in T-accounts, students compute ending balances, which become the starting balances for the next period. The lecture concludes by outlining the recording process—analyze each transaction, record debit and credit entries, and post them to the ledger. The governing principle “debits equal credits” upholds the balance sheet equation and ensures integrity throughout the accounting cycle.  

(Word count: 398)Overall winner: This lecture explored **within-subjects designs**, contrasting them with **between-groups designs**. In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data to only one condition, so individual differences across participants add nuisance variability that can obscure real effects. A within-subjects design instead tests each participant in all conditions and compares each person to themselves across those conditions. This approach helps remove participant-related noise, makes statistical tests more powerful, and requires far fewer participants for the same number of data points per condition.  

The lecture outlined several forms of within-subjects designs. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants both before and after an intervention. **Repeated-measures designs** include two or more conditions—for instance, varying levels of a stimulus such as motion coherence. **Longitudinal designs** measure participants across extended time spans to examine change over time. Each type offers efficiency and reduced variability relative to between-groups approaches.  

While effective, within-subjects designs can face specific challenges. **Demand characteristics** may occur when participants experience all conditions and infer the purpose of the study. **Carryover effects** arise when exposure to one condition (A) alters performance in the next (B)—for example through **fatigue** or **practice**. Broader time-linked threats include **history effects** (external events like exams or world events), **maturation effects** (changes due to aging or time rather than the intervention), and **testing effects** (where retaking the same test influences later performance).  

**Order effects** occur when the sequence of conditions affects results. To manage this, researchers use **counterbalancing**, varying order across participants. In **complete within-subjects designs**, every possible condition order is presented. The **ABBA design** (for two conditions) balances practice effects when learning changes gradually, while **block randomization** (for three or more conditions) arranges all condition orders randomly within repeated blocks. When full counterbalancing is impractical, **incomplete designs** are used. In the **random order with rotation** method, each participant experiences all conditions but only one rotated order; the number of participants must be a multiple of the number of conditions (e.g., 5, 10, or 15 for five conditions). The **Latin-square design** ensures each condition appears equally often in every position and follows every other condition equally often.  

In fields like cognitive science, where tasks involve many noisy trials, researchers may instead **intermix** easy and hard trials to cancel order effects, reserving counterbalancing when conditions are few or long. However, some manipulations—such as taking a drug or undergoing training—cannot be undone, limiting the use of counterbalancing.

For subqueries in the **FROM** clause, the lecture demonstrated how a subquery can define a **derived table** or temporary view. Using the **WITH clause** introduced **common table expressions (CTEs)**, enabling *subquery refactoring*—naming an intermediate result to clarify multi‑step logic. This differs from unnesting, which removes nesting entirely; both were shown to produce equivalent results in certain cases.

In the **WHERE** clause, subqueries represent **logical quantifiers**. **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) appear through `EXISTS` or `IN`, testing whether any tuple satisfies a condition—for example, identifying actors who acted in at least one Sci‑Fi film. These mirror join semantics. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) require more complex reasoning: the lecture presented a **two‑step process**—(1) select actors who participated in a non‑Action film, and (2) retrieve others not in that set—to find actors who acted **only in Action movies**. The same logic extended to queries such as finding movies where every actor’s salary exceeded $100K, expressible via `<ALL` or equivalently by `NOT EXISTS`.

The session concluded with the notion of **monotone versus non‑monotone queries**. A query is *monotone* if adding tuples to input tables cannot remove outputs. Slides proved that plain SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone under nested‑loop semantics. The lecture related this property to **unnesting** theoretically: **if a query is monotone, unnesting is possible**, whereas **non‑monotone queries**—those involving **negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates**—cannot be unnested in principle.  
*(≈394 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined nested queries and subqueries in SQL, focusing on their structure, use cases, and their relation to logical quantifiers and monotonicity. Building on prior SQL concepts such as projection, selection, joins, grouping, and aggregation, it defined a **subquery** as a query that is part of another query. These subqueries can appear within the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and may return either a single value or an entire relation. The instructor emphasized a key rule of thumb: **avoid nested queries when possible**, since they are often less efficient and harder to interpret—but acknowledged that in some situations, subqueries cannot be avoided.

Examples of **subqueries in the SELECT clause** showed how inner queries can derive information such as each actor’s movie genre, the number of actors per movie, or average salaries of actors in highly rated films. The slides introduced the concept of **subquery unnesting**, illustrating that equivalent results can frequently be produced using joins and aggregates. However, Slide 10 highlighted that equivalence does not always hold—particularly when outer joins are involved—so careful comparison is required when rewriting nested queries.

**Subqueries in the FROM clause** were presented as a way to create temporary or derived tables for intermediate filtering steps. By assigning names to subquery outputs (through aliases or common table expressions), the queries become more organized and readable. The lecture demonstrated how these forms can often be unnested back into simpler joins without changing the result.

**Subqueries in the WHERE clause** introduced the logical notion of quantifiers. Using **EXISTS** and **IN**, SQL expresses *existential quantification*—conditions that are true if some related record exists. Equivalent join-based rewritings were shown to achieve the same result. Conversely, **universal quantifiers** (“for all”) were harder to express directly in SQL. Examples showed how to simulate them with **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, such as finding actors who acted only in action movies or films where all actors earned above $100K. These required multi-step logic and emphasized that universal conditions increase query complexity and cannot always be unnested.

The lecture concluded by introducing **monotone** and **non-monotone queries**. A query is monotone if adding tuples to its input tables cannot remove results—this property holds for simple SELECT-FROM-WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates. In contrast, queries involving **negation, aggregation, or universal quantifiers** are **non-monotone**, meaning they cannot generally be unnested. This distinction forms the theoretical basis for understanding when query transformations are valid.

(Word count: 396)Overall winner: This lecture extends students’ understanding of how financial statements connect and introduces the mechanics of the double-entry bookkeeping system. It begins by emphasizing that accounting, as a representation of complex economic activity, requires both judgment and critical thinking. The four core financial statements—the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—serve as key communication tools. The balance sheet provides a snapshot of a company’s financial position at a point in time, while the other three describe activity over a period. These statements interlink: the income statement explains the profit or loss for the year, which alters retained earnings on the statement of stockholders’ equity; this, together with the cash flow statement, explains movements between two balance sheet dates. Revenues and expenses raise or reduce stockholders’ equity, so that net income increases equity and a net loss decreases it.

The lecture then expands the accounting equation beyond its basic form, assets = liabilities + shareholders’ equity. Equity is separated into contributed capital and retained earnings, the latter updated each period by revenues, expenses, and dividends. The extended form—assets = liabilities + contributed capital + beginning retained earnings + revenues – expenses – dividends—highlights how performance and distributions modify the firm’s financial position from one period to the next.

Building on this foundation, the class develops the double-entry framework: every economic event has two sides—a give and a take—and each transaction must affect at least two accounts so that total debits always equal total credits. A debit increases assets and decreases liabilities or equity, while a credit does the opposite. Practical examples illustrate this rule: borrowing increases both cash and notes payable; purchasing equipment or inventory shifts assets or adds liabilities; paying suppliers or insurance reduces either; and issuing shares raises both cash and equity. After posting transactions to T-accounts, students compute ending balances and observe that these become beginning balances in the next period, demonstrating the continuity of the accounting cycle.

Finally, the lecture formalizes recording steps—analyze the transaction, record journal entries with debits listed first, and post amounts to the ledger. The “DEALOR” mnemonic (Dividends, Expenses, Assets = Debit; Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue = Credit) summarizes T‑account behavior. The core principle “debits equal credits” preserves the accounting equation and ensures every financial statement remains internally consistent.  

*(Word count: 399)*Overall winner: This lecture deepens the study of Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **coloniality of power**, illuminating how colonial domination endures as a global matrix that organizes knowledge, labor, and identity in Latin America. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist whose work evolved from dependency theory and political economy to decolonial thought—argues that independence did not end colonialism’s logic; instead, its hierarchies were absorbed into the structures of global modernity.  

Within the **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD)** framework, modernity and coloniality are shown to be **co‑constituted**. Western modernity’s story of progress emerged through the conquest and exploitation of the Americas, not separately from it. “Coloniality” thus refers to the ongoing hierarchies of race and knowledge that sustain this world order, while “decoloniality” seeks plural modernities grounded in non‑Western languages, epistemologies, and cosmologies.  

The **coloniality of power** rests on two intertwined historical processes: the racial classification of peoples into conquerors (“West”) and conquered (“Rest”), and the creation of a global labor system linking racial difference to economic control. Systems such as slavery, encomienda, and mita naturalized a **systemic racial division of labor** that persists under capitalism. As slides on **Eurocentrism** explain, these material hierarchies were reinforced by epistemic ones—linear theories of history (“underdeveloped–developed,” “Third world–First world”) and **dualist evolutionism** that framed Europe as humanity’s vanguard. Stuart Hall’s notion of the “West and the Rest” helps reveal how such narratives, embedded in Enlightenment thought, produced a **Eurocentric mode of knowledge** that justified global inequality as progress.  

The lecture links these dynamics to **nation‑building and identity** in post‑independence Latin America. Elites embraced *Indianesque nationalism*, celebrating an idealized Indigenous past while marginalizing living Indigenous peoples. Later the ideology of **mestizaje** portrayed racial mixing as national unity yet reinforced aspirations toward whiteness and Eurocentric modernity—continuing the linkage between race, labor, and status noted by Quijano. Visual and cultural examples such as Modesto Broccos’s *A Redenção de Cã (The Redemption of Ham)* illustrate how ideas of whitening, family, and nation reproduced colonial hierarchies.  

Quijano’s **call for decoloniality** closes the lecture: to free ourselves from the “Eurocentric mirror” that distorts who we are and to recognize diverse, situated ways of knowing as equally valid bases for modern life. Decolonial practice means dismantling both the economic and epistemological continuities of empire.

*(Word count: 398)*Overall winner: This lecture deepens students’ understanding of how financial statements connect and introduces, in detail, the mechanics of the double‑entry system. It begins by stressing that accounting represents complex economic activity and requires judgment and critical thinking. Financial statements—balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—are the main communication tools describing a company’s position and performance. Debits increase assets and decrease liabilities or equity; credits do the opposite. No negative numbers are used—each transaction includes at least one debit and one credit, and the totals must balance. The “DEALOR” rule—Dividends, Expenses, Assets (debit); Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue (credit)—summarizes how T‑accounts behave. Throughout, the core principle “debits = credits” preserves the accounting equation and ensures internal consistency across all financial statements.

(≈395 words)Overall winner: This lecture covered nested and subqueries in SQL, focusing on their forms, logical roles, and their relationship to query optimization and monotonicity. Building on prior SQL features—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and data modification—the lecture introduced how queries can themselves appear inside other queries. For **subqueries in SELECT**, examples showed computing derived attributes such as movie genre, number of actors, or average actor salaries. The same results can frequently be obtained using joins and Group By operations—an optimization known as *subquery unnesting*. However, slide 10 highlighted that these rewritings are not always truly equivalent: subtle differences can arise when outer joins or missing values affect the outcome. This emphasized that recognizing when two SQL statements yield identical semantics requires careful attention to join types and null handling.

**Subqueries in FROM** can define intermediate results, acting like temporary tables or “refactored” steps within a query. This can be done inline or through a common table expression (CTE) to improve readability and modularization. The logic can often still be flattened into a single FROM clause using equivalent join conditions.

**Subqueries in WHERE** evaluate logical conditions over related data and introduced the **existential quantifier**. The lecture showed the use of `EXISTS` and `IN` to test for the presence of matching tuples (“there exists”) and contrasted these with `NOT IN` for negation. Such expressions can also be rewritten as joins when testing for existence of relationships, illustrating how join queries inherently capture existential logic.

Finally, the lecture moved from existential to **universal quantifiers** (“for all”), which require reasoning about every possible related tuple. Since SQL lacks a direct construct for universal quantification, examples demonstrated multi-step formulations: first identify actors associated with any non-action movie, then retrieve the remaining actors who acted only in action films. Similarly, to find movies whose actors all earn above a threshold, a NOT EXISTS pattern was used. These examples showed that universal queries involve negation and are harder to optimize. The session concluded with the **monotonicity principle**: monotone queries—those that never lose results when adding data—can be unnested, while queries containing negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates are non‑monotone and resist such rewriting. (Word count ≈ 402)Overall winner: This lecture examined **nested SQL queries**—queries embedded within other queries—and linked their practical use to the theoretical concept of **monotonicity** in query evaluation. Building on earlier SQL topics like projections, selections, joins, grouping, and aggregates, it defined a **subquery** as a query appearing in a **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clause, returning either a value or a relation. The guiding rule is to avoid nesting when an equivalent join can express the same logic more efficiently.

In the **SELECT clause**, examples showed correlated subqueries, such as retrieving each actor’s movie genre, counting the actors in a movie, or computing average salaries for high‑rated films. Each was compared to an equivalent **join‑based form** through **subquery unnesting**. Slides emphasized that these rewrites can differ when outer joins are needed: a **LEFT OUTER JOIN** preserves movies without actors, whereas its nested equivalent may omit them, highlighting semantic differences in query results.

Subqueries in the **FROM clause** were presented as **derived tables** or **subquery refactoring**—for instance, selecting movies with ratings between 8 and 9, naming that result “myTable,” and then querying it. This modular approach makes query logic clearer while producing the same output, and equivalent unnested forms were shown.

In the **WHERE clause**, subqueries introduced **logical quantifiers**. Using **EXISTS** or **IN** expresses existential conditions (“there exists”) and often can be flattened into joins. By contrast, **universal quantifiers** (“for all”) require more complex logic because SQL cannot directly express them. The slides illustrated this with two forms for testing whether all actor salaries exceed $100K: an **unsupported** comparison using `$100K < ALL (...)`, and a **supported alternate** using `NOT EXISTS` to check that no actor earns $100K or less. Other stepwise examples—such as finding actors who appear only in action movies—demonstrated this “negated existence” reasoning pattern.

The lecture concluded with **monotone versus non‑monotone queries**. A query is monotone if adding data never removes existing results; simple SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates satisfy this property. Non‑monotone queries involve **negation**, **universal quantifiers**, or **aggregates**, meaning their answers can shrink as data grows. The key takeaway: **only monotone queries can be safely unnested**, connecting the formal theory of query semantics to practical SQL rewriting.  

(≈382 words)Overall winner: This lecture strengthened understanding of how financial statements, the accounting equation, and double-entry mechanics integrate within the Principles of Accounting framework. It began by emphasizing that accounting translates complex economic activity into information through judgment and critical thinking. A quick Week 1 refresher reviewed the four interrelated financial statements—balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—as a communication system reflecting both a company’s position at specific dates and its performance over a fiscal period. The statements connect through retained earnings: revenues and expenses generate net income, which increases or decreases stockholders’ equity and links one balance sheet to the next.

The session expanded the accounting equation from its basic form,  
**Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity**,  
to an analytical version that highlights period changes:  
**Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + (Beginning Retained Earnings + Revenues – Expenses – Dividends)**.  
This formulation shows how income, losses, and distributions drive equity and maintain balance across financial statements.

The lecture then detailed double-entry accounting as the system ensuring that every transaction affects at least two accounts and that total debits always equal total credits. Debits increase assets, expenses, and dividends, while credits increase liabilities, contributed capital, and revenues. The T‑account was introduced to visualize these dual effects, with no negative numbers used—only debits and credits that must balance. Stepwise examples illustrated this symmetry:  
• Borrowing $100,000 from a bank → Debit Cash $100,000; Credit Notes Payable $100,000.  
• Purchasing equipment for $60,000 cash → Debit Equipment $60,000; Credit Cash $60,000.  
• Buying inventory for $15,000 on account → Debit Inventory $15,000; Credit Trade Payable $15,000.  
• Paying a supplier $8,000 reduces both Cash and Trade Payable by that amount.  
• Prepaying a $600 insurance policy → Debit Prepaid Insurance $600; Credit Cash $600.  
• Issuing 10,000 shares for $100,000 → Debit Cash $100,000; Credit Common Stock $100,000.  
Ending balances confirmed across T‑accounts—e.g., Cash $131,400 and Trade Payable $7,000—demonstrated that debits equaled credits and that closing balances become the next period’s beginning amounts, ensuring continuity.

Finally, the accounting process was summarized in three steps: (1) analyze each transaction, (2) record the debits and credits as journal entries, and (3) post to ledger accounts to determine ending balances. The DEALOR cue—Dividends, Expenses, Assets (Debit); Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue (Credit)—was reinforced to recall normal account sides. Together, these elements maintain the core equality **Assets = Liabilities + Equity**, the foundation of financial reporting integrity.  

(≈398 words)Overall winner: This lecture examined **nested SQL queries (subqueries)**—queries embedded within other queries—and their logical roles, performance implications, and connection to **monotonicity**. Building on prior SQL operations (projection, selection, joins, aggregation, GROUP BY, HAVING, and data updates), the session showed how subqueries can appear within the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. Because nested queries may depend on the output of outer queries, they are sometimes necessary, but the rule of thumb is to **avoid nesting when possible** by rewriting or “unnesting” them for efficiency.

For **subqueries in SELECT**, examples included deriving each movie’s genre, counting actors, and computing average salaries. These results can typically be produced equivalently with joins and GROUP BY clauses—a process called *subquery unnesting*. Slide 10 stressed that such rewritings are not always identical: a nested form with filtering can correspond semantically to a **LEFT OUTER JOIN**, while an unnested version more closely resembles an **INNER JOIN**, leading to different outcomes when missing values appear.

**Subqueries in FROM** define intermediate tables, either inline or via common table expressions (CTEs), to improve query readability and structure. Their results can usually be flattened into a single FROM clause without changing output—an example of *subquery refactoring*.

For **subqueries in WHERE**, the lecture introduced **existential quantifiers**, implemented through `EXISTS` or `IN`, which test whether a related record exists. These express “there exists” logic and can often be replaced by equivalent joins, showing that basic join queries already capture existential reasoning. Using `NOT IN` introduces **negation**, which will later matter for monotonicity.

The lecture then moved to **universal quantifiers** (“for all”), expressed indirectly through a two‑step pattern—first identifying actors connected to any non‑Action movie, then retrieving those not in that set to find actors who only performed in Action films. Similar logic retrieved movies whose every actor earned above $100 K using `NOT EXISTS`. Because these involve **negation and “for all” logic**, they are harder to optimize and cannot always be unnested.

The session concluded with **monotone vs. non‑monotone queries**. Subqueries can be unnested only when the containing query is monotone—i.e., adding tuples to input relations never removes results. A SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE query without subqueries or aggregates is monotone; queries containing **negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates** are **non‑monotone** and resist unnesting. (Word count ≈ 382)Overall winner: This lecture examines Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **coloniality of power** and its continuing influence on modern forms of knowledge, labor, and identity in Latin America. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist who moved from dependency theory and political economy toward decolonial theory—argued that colonialism produced not just territorial domination but a persistent global hierarchy of race, labor, and knowledge. His work is central to the **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD)** framework, which interrogates how ideas of modern progress and Western superiority were built alongside systems of colonial subjugation.

The lecture first established how **modernity and coloniality were co‑constituted**. Modernity’s “great march of progress” depended on silencing the colonial histories that enabled it. Through the “coloniality of power,” racial distinctions divided conquerors and conquered, establishing a lasting social order linking whiteness to authority and productivity. This structure took shape through new systems of labor control—slavery, the encomienda, and the mita—that tied racial categories to global capitalism. Quijano showed that **Eurocentrism**, the belief in Western cultural and epistemic preeminence, naturalized these hierarchies over time, embedding them in linear narratives of development that positioned Europe as the world’s protagonist.

Building on Stuart Hall’s “West and the Rest,” the lecture connected these theories to the ways racialized labor and Eurocentric knowledge systems sustain inequality. Examples such as Alfonso Cuarón’s *Roma* illustrated how colonial legacies of class, race, and gender persist in contemporary society. Discussion also addressed the tension between **homogeneity and heterogeneity** in social change: decolonial thinking emphasizes diverse historical trajectories and local agency rather than one linear path of progress.

In the second half, the lecture turned to the **formation of national identity** after independence. Drawing on historian Rebecca Earle, it outlined how early “Indianesque nationalism” celebrated a romanticized Indigenous past as national symbolism while marginalizing living Indigenous peoples. Later **mestizaje** ideologies presented racial mixing as unity yet frequently reaffirmed whiteness and excluded Afro‑descendant and Indigenous populations from the national ideal. These examples demonstrated how Eurocentric frameworks continued to define Latin American modernity even after formal colonialism ended.

The lecture concluded with Quijano’s call for **decoloniality**—to break from the Eurocentric mirror that distorts self‑understanding and to recognize multiple, situated ways of knowing, being, and imagining the future beyond colonial logics.  
*(~397 words)*Overall winner: This lecture deepened understanding of how accounting translates complex economic activity into clear, communicative financial information. Building on Week 1, it emphasized that financial statements serve as a company’s formal communication to users about performance and position, requiring both judgment and critical thinking. The four statements—the balance sheet, income statement, statement of stockholders’ equity, and statement of cash flows—were reviewed as interlinked periodic reports. Each provides a different view: the balance sheet shows resources and claims at specific points in time, while the other three explain changes across the fiscal period. Together, they connect through retained earnings: revenues and expenses generate net income, which raises or reduces stockholders’ equity and links one balance sheet to the next.

The discussion then unpacked the accounting equation from its basic form,  
**Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders’ Equity**,  
to an expanded version showing the flow of earnings and distributions:  
**Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + (Beginning Retained Earnings + Revenues – Expenses – Dividends)**.  
This framework explains how business activities change equity while keeping the equation balanced and the statements connected.

A major focus was double-entry recordkeeping—the mechanism that guarantees balance. Every transaction has two sides, “a give and a take,” affecting at least one of the classes (A, L, or SE). Debits record increases to assets, expenses, and dividends; credits record increases to liabilities, contributed capital, and revenues. All entries use positive numbers, and for every transaction total debits must equal total credits.  
Illustrated examples showed this duality: borrowing funds increases both cash and notes payable; purchasing equipment with cash swaps one asset for another; buying inventory on account adds both inventory and a payable; paying suppliers or prepaying insurance reduces cash while altering the corresponding asset or liability; and issuing common stock raises both cash and contributed capital. These examples demonstrated that each event preserves **A = L + SE**.

The lecture concluded by sequencing the accounting process: (1) analyze the transaction, (2) record it as a journal entry with debits listed first and credits indented, and (3) post to T‑accounts to determine ending balances that feed the next period’s beginning amounts. The mnemonic **DEALOR**—Dividends, Expenses, Assets (debit); Liabilities, Owners’ Equity, Revenue (credit)—was used to recall normal account sides. Altogether, the system positions accounting as a disciplined, communicative framework that converts economic events into reliable, balanced reports.  

(≈387 words)Overall winner: This lecture focused on **nested SQL queries (subqueries)**—queries embedded inside another query—and examined their syntax, logical meaning, rewriting techniques, and the connection to **monotonicity**. By contrast, queries using **negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates** are **non‑monotone**, meaning their nested form cannot be equivalently flattened.

to include contributed capital and the flow within retained earnings:  
**Assets = Liabilities + Contributed Capital + (Beginning Retained Earnings + Revenues – Expenses – Dividends)**.

(≈398 words)Overall winner: This lecture examined nested SQL queries, emphasizing their structure, use cases, and theoretical properties such as query monotonicity. Building on previously learned SQL components—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and data modification—the lecture introduced subqueries as queries embedded within another query. These can appear in the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses, returning either single values or full relations. While subqueries allow conditions that depend on computed tables or intermediate results, the general rule of thumb is to avoid nesting when possible, because simpler, unnested rewrites are typically more efficient.

Subqueries in the SELECT clause were illustrated through examples computing the genre of each actor’s movie, the number of actors per movie, and the average salary for highly rated films. These examples showed that many nested constructs can be rewritten as equivalent joins with grouping or aggregation (“subquery unnesting”). However, slide comparisons demonstrated that equivalence depends on the query structure—adding a LEFT OUTER JOIN, for instance, can produce different results, emphasizing when unnesting is valid. Subqueries in the FROM clause were shown to act as derived relations or temporary tables. Using inline subqueries or common table expressions (CTEs) can improve query organization without changing the answer, and often such queries can also be unnested into single SELECT–FROM–WHERE statements.

Subqueries in the WHERE clause introduced logical quantifiers. Using EXISTS or IN expresses existential quantification (“there exists”), which tests whether matching tuples exist in another relation. These can often be replaced with joins. In contrast, universal quantifiers (“for all”) are harder to capture directly in SQL and require careful constructions using NOT IN or NOT EXISTS. Examples included retrieving actors who acted only in action movies and finding movies where all actors earn above $100K, demonstrating multi-step logic to exclude disqualifying cases. Universal quantifier queries are inherently more complex and frequently cannot be unnested.

The lecture concluded by connecting these ideas to query monotonicity. A query is monotone if adding tuples to input tables never removes output tuples. A theorem stated that SELECT–FROM–WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are provably monotone. Monotone queries can be safely unnested, while non-monotone queries—those involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates—cannot. This distinction determines both optimization potential and the practical limits of rewriting nested queries in SQL.

**Point 1 – Coloniality of Power.** The conquest of the Americas generated two foundational processes: (1) distinguishing conquerors from the conquered through racial categories, and (2) constructing new systems of labor control for extracting resources. Slavery, the encomienda, and mita systems tied race to work and established global dependency, forming what Quijano called a new model of power. This racialized organization of labor naturalized inequalities and positioned whiteness as the standard of civilization and humanity.  

**Point 2 – Eurocentrism.** European dominance in world markets and knowledge was historically produced, not given. From the beginning of colonization, Europeans linked coerced or unpaid labor to supposedly “inferior” races, embedding racial hierarchy into capitalism. **Eurocentrism** operates as both perspective and epistemology: it presents Europe as the universal reference point while rendering other histories “pre‑capital” or “underdeveloped.” Linear progress narratives—First World/Third World, developed/underdeveloped—emerge from this view. Connections to Stuart Hall’s “West and the Rest” helped show how images and representations sustain Eurocentric assumptions, further examined through examples such as Alfonso Cuarón’s *Roma*.  

**Point 3 – Eurocentrism and Historical Experience in Latin America.** After independence, criollo elites built national imaginaries that romanticized an Indigenous past while excluding living Indigenous peoples (“Indianesque nationalism”). Later **mestizaje** ideologies recast mixture as unity but continued centering whiteness, revealing continuity between colonial hierarchies and national identity‑making.  

Finally, the lecture contrasted Europe’s linear view of progress with Aymara concepts of *pachakuti*, which emphasize cyclical transformation and heterogeneous pathways of change. Quijano’s closing call for **decoloniality** urged freeing ourselves from the **Eurocentric mirror**—to unlearn distorted self‑images and engage non‑Western ways of knowing and being.  
*(~369 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examines Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **“coloniality of power”** within the broader framework of **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD)**. Quijano, a Peruvian sociologist whose work spans dependency theory, cultural analysis, and epistemology, argues that modernity and coloniality are **co‑constituted** rather than separate historical stages. The MCD framework identifies three interlinked dimensions: **modernity** as a Eurocentric narrative of progress centered on the “West”; **coloniality** as the enduring matrix of domination that privileges Western forms of knowledge, labor, and identity; and **decoloniality** as an effort to recover and center subjugated worldviews, epistemologies, and ways of being.

At the core of Quijano’s theory are **two historical processes** that shaped the “coloniality of power”: the invention of racial distinctions separating conquerors (“West”) from the conquered (“Rest”), and the creation of new structures for labor control in the Americas. Systems such as **slavery, the encomienda, and the mita** organized colonial economies and naturalized a **racialized global division of labor**, linking race to social status and access to power. This coupling of race and labor became foundational to global capitalism, sustaining a social order justified through ideas of European superiority. **Eurocentrism**, as both an ideology and a mode of producing knowledge, reinforced the notion that Western civilization represented the universal path to progress, obscuring other ways of knowing.

The lecture connects this epistemological critique to cultural and political dynamics in Latin America. Films such as *Roma* exemplify how colonial hierarchies persist in everyday life, shaping perceptions of class, gender, and race. Building on this, the lecture explores how **Eurocentric ideologies infused post‑independence nation‑building**. Historian **Rebecca Earle’s “Indianesque nationalism”** shows how early republics celebrated Indigenous heritage through symbolism while excluding Indigenous populations from full citizenship. Later **mestizaje ideologies** promoted racial mixing as national unity but often privileged whiteness, marginalizing Indigenous and Afro‑descendant peoples.

Quijano’s call for **decoloniality** urges societies to “free ourselves from the Eurocentric mirror” that distorts identity and to embrace **heterogeneous, historically situated knowledges** that challenge linear models of history and progress. Decolonial practice, in this sense, is both critical and creative—seeking to dismantle the colonial matrix of power and envision plural modernities rooted in diverse cultural and epistemic experiences.  

*(399 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined nested SQL queries, emphasizing their structure, use cases, and theoretical properties such as query monotonicity. Building on previously learned SQL components—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, and data modification—the lecture introduced subqueries as queries embedded within another query. These can appear in the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses, returning either single values or full relations. While subqueries allow conditions that depend on computed tables or intermediate results, the general rule of thumb is to avoid nesting when possible, because simpler, unnested rewrites are typically more efficient.

Subqueries in the SELECT clause were illustrated through examples computing the genre of each actor’s movie, the number of actors per movie, and the average salary for highly rated films. These examples showed that many nested constructs can be rewritten as equivalent joins with grouping or aggregation (“subquery unnesting”). However, slide comparisons demonstrated that equivalence depends on the query structure—adding a LEFT OUTER JOIN, for instance, can produce different results, emphasizing when unnesting is valid. Subqueries in the FROM clause were shown to act as derived relations or temporary tables. Using inline subqueries or common table expressions (CTEs) can improve query organization without changing the answer, and often such queries can also be unnested into single SELECT–FROM–WHERE statements.

Subqueries in the WHERE clause introduced logical quantifiers. Using EXISTS or IN expresses existential quantification (“there exists”), which tests whether matching tuples exist in another relation. These can often be replaced with joins. In contrast, universal quantifiers (“for all”) are harder to capture directly in SQL and require careful constructions using NOT IN or NOT EXISTS. Examples included retrieving actors who acted only in action movies and finding movies where all actors earn above $100K, demonstrating multi-step logic to exclude disqualifying cases. Universal quantifier queries are inherently more complex and frequently cannot be unnested.

The lecture concluded by connecting these ideas to query monotonicity. A query is monotone if adding tuples to input tables never removes output tuples. A theorem stated that SELECT–FROM–WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are provably monotone. Monotone queries can be safely unnested, while non-monotone queries—those involving negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates—cannot. This distinction determines both optimization potential and the practical limits of rewriting nested queries in SQL.  

*(Word count: 400)*Overall winner: This lecture deepened discussion of Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **Coloniality of Power**, tracing how colonial domination reshaped global systems of race, labor, and knowledge that still organize life in Latin America. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist whose work spans dependency theory, political economy, and decolonial epistemology—showed that the colonial order did not end with independence. Instead, its logic endured as a worldwide structure of racialized capitalism and Eurocentric thought. Within the **Modernity / Coloniality / Decoloniality (MCD)** framework, the lecture emphasized that modernity and coloniality were **co‑constituted**: Western “progress” and white supremacy grew together as parts of the same historical process, not as separate or parallel modernities.  

**Point 1 – Coloniality of Power.** The conquest of the Americas created two intertwined formations: (1) new racial categories that separated conquerors and conquered, and (2) systems of labor control—slavery, encomienda, and mita —that extracted wealth while tying race to economic role. These relations produced a **new model of global power** combining economic exploitation with epistemic domination. Racial difference became a mental category of modernity, naturalizing whiteness as a universal standard of civilization and humanity.  

**Point 2 – Eurocentrism.** European dominance in economy and knowledge was not inevitable but historically forged through racialized exploitation. Early colonizers linked unpaid labor to “inferior” races, embedding hierarchy within capitalism itself. **Eurocentrism** functions as both worldview and method of knowledge‑making that privileges Europe as the universal reference point while casting others as “pre‑capital,” “developing,” or “Third World.” This perspective formed the ideological foundation for linear narratives of progress. Connections to Stuart Hall’s “West and the Rest” highlighted how cultural images and discourses reproduce those assumptions—illustrated in analyses of films like *Roma*, which expose contemporary layers of colonial power.  

**Point 3 – Eurocentrism and Historical Experience in Latin America.** After independence, elites constructed national imaginaries that romanticized a distant Indigenous past yet excluded living Indigenous peoples. Rebecca Earle termed this “**Indianesque nationalism**”: symbolic pride used by non‑Indigenous creoles to claim deep roots while maintaining colonial hierarchies. Subsequent **mestizaje** ideologies reframed mixture as unity but continued to center whiteness, revealing continuity between colonial and national projects of identity.  

The lecture concluded by contrasting Europe’s linear vision of history with Aymara ideas of *pachakuti*, underscoring diverse, cyclical understandings of change. *(~390 words)*Overall winner: This lecture develops Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the **coloniality of power** within the larger **Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD)** framework. A Peruvian sociologist whose work spans dependency theory, cultural identity, and epistemology, Quijano argues that **modernity and coloniality were co‑constituted**, not sequential. The MCD project identifies three dimensions: **modernity**, the Eurocentric narrative portraying Western progress as universal; **coloniality**, the long‑lasting structure of domination that privileges Western knowledge and rationality; and **decoloniality**, the effort to recentre non‑Western languages, cultures, and ways of being.

**Point 1 – Coloniality of Power:**  
Quijano traces two entwined historical processes that defined the formation of “America”: (1) the construction of differences between “conquerors” (the West) and the “conquered” (the Rest); and (2) the establishment of new forms of labor control tied to the Americas’ resources. Systems such as slavery, the encomienda, and the mita organized production for global markets and institutionalized a **racialized division of labor**. Race became a mental category of modernity, naturalizing hierarchies that structured political economy and global capitalism.

**Point 2 – Eurocentrism:**  
European domination of the world market was not inevitable but arose through colonial exploitation. **Eurocentrism** became both ideology and knowledge system, linking whiteness to superiority and defining the West as the exclusive agent of progress. This perspective produced dualisms—developed/underdeveloped, civilized/primitive—that framed world history as a linear path toward European modernity. Quijano’s analysis complements Stuart Hall’s “West and the Rest” discourse, revealing how such representations continue to shape global hierarchies and ideas of success.

**Point 3 – National Identity and Historical Experience:**  
In post‑independence Latin America, Eurocentric thought persisted in projects of nation building. As historian **Rebecca Earle** shows, “Indianesque nationalism” romanticized an Indigenous past while excluding living Indigenous peoples from citizenship. Later **mestizaje** ideologies promoted racial mixing and unity yet often reaffirmed whiteness and social inequality. Media such as *Roma* illustrate how these colonial hierarchies endure in everyday life.

Quijano’s **call for decoloniality** (Slide 19) urges a step beyond the “Eurocentric mirror” that distorts self‑understanding. Decoloniality means freeing knowledge and identity from colonial frameworks and acknowledging the **heterogeneity and discontinuity** of historical processes. In doing so, Latin American thought can recover multiple modernities rooted in diverse epistemologies and lived experiences.

A Peruvian sociologist, Quijano traced how colonialism established enduring hierarchies that shaped modern global power. Building on his work in dependency theory and cultural analysis, he proposed that coloniality—not merely colonialism—continues through the epistemological and social structures that privilege Western ways of knowing and being. Within the MCD project, *modernity* refers to dominant Western narratives of progress, *coloniality* exposes the hidden mechanisms of racial, economic, and epistemic domination, and *decoloniality* calls for centering non-Western languages, cosmologies, and knowledge systems.

At the core of the “coloniality of power” are two intertwined historical processes: (1) the invention of racial categories to differentiate conquerors (the “West”) from the conquered (the “Rest”), and (2) the creation of new systems of labor control—slavery, the *encomienda*, and *m’ita*—that tied racial identity to economic function. These dynamics forged a global capitalist model organized through racialized labor divisions, enabling Europeans to naturalize their dominance as universal and progressive. Slides further link this system to *Eurocentrism*, a worldview that privileges European perspectives and measures all societies by Western concepts of development and success. (≈398 words)Overall winner: This lecture examines Aníbal Quijano’s concept of the *coloniality of power* as a framework for understanding how colonial-era hierarchies continue to shape modernity, capitalism, and national identity in Latin America. Quijano—a Peruvian sociologist whose intellectual trajectory moved from dependency theory and political economy to cultural and decolonial theory—argued that colonialism did not simply end with independence but rather evolved into enduring structures of domination over knowledge, labor, and identity.

Within the broader Modernity/Coloniality/Decoloniality (MCD) project, Quijano and other scholars such as Arturo Escobar, Walter Mignolo, and Catherine Walsh critique Western modernity’s self-presentation as universal “progress.” They instead emphasize how modernity and coloniality were co-constituted. The lecture outlines two foundational processes forming the coloniality of power: the creation of racialized distinctions between “West” and “Rest,” and the establishment of a new global labor system in the Americas through slavery, the encomienda, and the mita. These systems embedded racial hierarchies into capitalism, naturalizing the association of whiteness with power and viewing non-European identities as inferior. Through Eurocentrism, such hierarchies were rationalized as inevitable stages of development, reinforcing narratives of European superiority.

Quijano linked this colonial legacy to multiple arenas: race and labor exploitation, gender and sexuality (to be expanded in later discussions), national identity, and the continued dependency of Latin America within global capitalism. Decoloniality, in contrast, calls for centering non-Western languages, cosmologies, and epistemologies—recovering suppressed perspectives to unlearn the Eurocentric mirror that distorts both history and self-knowledge. The lecture also highlights how heterogeneity and discontinuity challenge linear historical models, opening intellectual space for plural modernities.

Applications of these ideas appear in contemporary and historical examples. *Roma* (2018) exemplifies how structures of race, class, and labor persist through colonial logics within everyday life and cultural production. Historian Rebecca Earle’s notion of “Indianesque nationalism” further illustrates how early post-independence elites romanticized Indigenous heritage as cultural symbolism while excluding living Indigenous peoples from national belonging. Subsequent *mestizaje* ideologies—ostensibly celebrating racial mixture—continued to privilege whiteness and obscure Afro-descendant and Indigenous realities. Together, these cases display the persistence of Eurocentric ideals within nation-building. The lecture concludes with Quijano’s decolonial call: to dismantle the matrix of colonial power and imagine more plural, equitable ways of knowing and being.

Modernity and coloniality were *co‑constituted*—colonial exploitation made modern capitalism possible—even as this shared origin was later silenced in linear narratives of Western “progress.” These dynamics established patterns of dependency and inequality that persist across the Americas.

**Point 2 – Eurocentrism**  
Coloniality extends to knowledge and culture through the dominance of Eurocentrism, which constructs Europe as the universal measure of civilization. Slides critique the dualisms it produces—developed/underdeveloped, First/Third World, modern/traditional—and its portrayal of history as linear advancement toward European ideals. Drawing connections to Stuart Hall’s “West and the Rest,” the lecture shows how Enlightenment thought and cultural representations sustained myths of Western superiority. The example of Alfonso Cuarón’s *Roma* served to analyze how racialized and gendered hierarchies from colonial contexts remain embedded in contemporary imaginaries. The lecture also questioned Eurocentric models of *democracy* and *development*, asking who defines measures of national “success” and whose experiences those standards erase.

**Point 3 – Eurocentrism and Latin American National Identity**  
Following independence, elites adopted Eurocentric visions in building “imagined communities.” Through “Indianesque nationalism” and later *mestizaje*, they celebrated romanticized Indigenous ancestry while excluding living Indigenous and Afro‑descendant peoples. Rebecca Earle’s work illustrated this contradiction and invited discussion of how Quijano would interpret such symbolic inclusion as a continuation of coloniality.

Slides concluded by contrasting homogenous, linear histories with heterogeneous and discontinuous understandings of change—reflected in Aymara notions of *pachakuti*—and by invoking Quijano’s call for *decoloniality*: freeing ourselves from the Eurocentric mirror to recover plural modernities grounded in diverse epistemologies and cosmologies.  

(400 words)Overall winner: This lecture traced how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, following Darwin’s postulates that organisms struggle for existence, vary in traits affecting survival and reproduction, and pass these heritable variations to offspring. Although Darwin lacked a mechanism for maintaining variation, evidence from artificial selection in plants and animals—such as the rapid transformation of dog and pigeon breeds—demonstrated that significant change can occur within a few generations. Quantitative examples like the rapid evolution of a placental structure in minnows and the gradual 1% incremental evolution of the eye (completed in about a million years) illustrated both the pace and pathways of evolutionary change.

Mendel’s experiments with pea plants between 1856 and 1863 revealed particulate inheritance: traits are controlled by discrete hereditary “particles” that remain intact across generations. Rediscovered around 1900, his work connected genetics to cellular structures. Walther Flemming described chromatin and mitosis, and Sutton and Boveri later identified chromosomes as the carriers of genetic material—the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Humans possess 46 chromosomes arranged in 23 homologous pairs, providing the structural basis for heredity across species.

The lecture distinguished mitosis, which produces identical diploid somatic cells, from meiosis, which generates haploid gametes. Meiosis halves the chromosome number so that fertilization restores species consistency in chromosome count, while mutation and recombination introduce genetic variation. Mendel’s results clarified the genotype–phenotype relationship and how dominant and recessive alleles interact. Crosses of heterozygotes and Punnett square predictions illustrated predictable inheritance. Linkage and crossing-over showed that genes on different chromosomes assort independently, whereas physically close loci may not, with recombination rates quantifying the frequency of exchange.

The identification of DNA as the molecular basis of heredity unified these discoveries. Watson and Crick, using Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray images, described the double helix of deoxyribonucleic acid: a sugar-phosphate backbone with complementary base pairs (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, while mitochondrial DNA—shorter, maternally inherited, and nonrecombining—provides evidence for evolutionary relationships, including “Mitochondrial Eve,” estimated to have lived about 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa.  

**(398 words)**Overall winner: This lecture traced how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, guided by Darwin’s postulates: organisms struggle for existence, vary in traits affecting survival and reproduction, and transmit these heritable variations to their offspring. Although Darwin lacked a clear mechanism for maintaining variation, examples from artificial selection—such as the rapid diversification of dogs and pigeons—demonstrated that substantial changes can emerge in only a few generations. Quantitative cases, including the rapid evolution of a placenta-like structure in minnows and the approximately one‑million‑year formation of the eye through 1% incremental changes, illustrated different rates of evolutionary change.  

Mendel’s pea plant experiments (1856–1863) revealed particulate inheritance: hereditary traits are controlled by discrete “particles” inherited from each parent, preserving variation rather than blending it. His findings, rediscovered around 1900, were linked to cellular structures when Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, and when Sutton and Boveri demonstrated that chromosomes carry hereditary material—the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Humans have 46 chromosomes in 23 homologous pairs, providing the structural basis for heredity. Chromosomes replicate before cell division; without this step, successive divisions would halve the genetic material—a key mechanism maintaining fidelity across generations.  

The lecture distinguished mitosis, which produces identical diploid somatic cells, from meiosis, which produces haploid gametes. Meiosis halves the chromosome number so that fertilization restores species‑specific counts while also introducing variation through mutation and recombination. Mendel’s results defined genes, alleles, genotype, and phenotype, and explained how dominant and recessive alleles determine expressed traits. Crosses of heterozygotes, shown through Punnett squares, predict trait distributions. Linkage and crossing‑over demonstrated that genes on different chromosomes assort independently, but closely located genes may not; recombination frequency measures how often crossing‑over occurs.  

The molecular basis of inheritance was clarified when Watson and Crick, drawing on Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray data, described DNA’s double‑helix structure—a sugar‑phosphate backbone paired with specific bases (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, while mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA), maternally inherited and nonrecombining, is shorter and highly conserved. Analyses of mtDNA identified “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of humans (about 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa). DNA comparisons also function as a molecular clock, permitting estimation of species divergence times based on genetic differences at neutral sites.
