*(≈391 words)*Overall winner: This lecture traced how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change, linking Darwin’s observations with modern genetics and molecular biology. Evolution was defined as a change in allele frequencies within a population over time, shaped by three postulates: organisms compete for limited resources, individuals vary in traits linked to survival and reproduction, and these variations are heritable. While Darwin recognized that variation underpins natural selection, he could not explain how it was maintained or transmitted across generations. Artificial selection—humans deliberately breeding plants and animals such as dogs and pigeons—illustrated how targeted selection can rapidly reshape populations, demonstrating evolution in action.

Gregor Mendel’s mid-19th-century experiments with pea plants established that inheritance is particulate: discrete “particles,” now known as genes, are passed from each parent, preserving variation rather than blending it. His findings, rediscovered around 1900, laid the groundwork for connecting genetic material to physical cell structures. Walther Flemming discovered chromatin and described mitosis, while Sutton and Boveri proposed the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance, identifying chromosomes as carriers of hereditary material.

Understanding cell division clarified how genetic information is copied and distributed. Mitosis produces identical diploid somatic cells, ensuring genomic stability, whereas meiosis forms haploid gametes, halving chromosome number. Variation arises both through mutation and recombination during meiosis, as homologous chromosomes exchange sections in crossing over. Mendel’s definitions of genes, alleles, genotype, and phenotype helped explain predictable inheritance patterns using dominant and recessive traits, visualized through crosses and Punnett squares. However, genes physically close on the same chromosome may not assort independently, since linkage can limit segregation, while recombination increases genetic diversity.

The discovery of DNA’s structure completed the link from inheritance to molecular mechanisms. Watson and Crick, using Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray data, showed that DNA is a double helix with a sugar‑phosphate backbone and complementary base pairing (A‑T, C‑G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, and some—such as mitochondrial DNA—are inherited exclusively from mothers. Mitochondrial DNA, shorter and more conserved than nuclear DNA, allows scientists to trace lineages and estimate evolutionary timelines, including identifying “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans from roughly 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa.

*(≈391 words)*Overall winner: This lecture traced how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change, linking Darwin’s observations with Mendelian genetics and modern molecular biology. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, shaped by three postulates: organisms struggle for existence, individuals vary in traits affecting survival and reproduction, and these variations are heritable. While Darwin recognized variation as the raw material for selection, he could not explain how it was maintained or transmitted. Artificial selection—humans selectively breeding plants and animals such as dogs and pigeons—demonstrated that substantial changes can occur in just a few generations. Quantitative examples, such as minnows evolving placenta-like structures and the gradual evolution of the eye through 1,800 small steps over about one million years, highlighted that evolutionary rates vary but are measurable over both short and long timescales.  

Mendel’s mid-19th-century pea plant experiments showed that inheritance is particulate: discrete “particles,” now called genes, are passed from each parent and preserve variation. Rediscovery of his work around 1900 spurred investigations into the physical basis of heredity. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and described mitosis, and Sutton and Boveri’s Chromosome Theory of Inheritance established that chromosomes carry genetic material. Understanding cell division clarified these mechanisms: mitosis produces identical diploid somatic cells, while meiosis forms haploid gametes, maintaining a consistent chromosome number across generations. Variation arises through mutation and recombination during meiosis, as homologous chromosomes exchange segments in crossing-over. Mendel’s definitions of genes, alleles, genotype, and phenotype explained predictable inheritance patterns, while linkage revealed that genes located close together may not assort independently, though recombination increases genetic diversity.  

The discovery of DNA’s structure connected inheritance to molecular processes. In 1953, Watson and Crick, building on Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray images, described the double-helix molecule composed of a sugar‑phosphate backbone and complementary base pairs (A‑T, C‑G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, and humans possess about 20,000–25,000 of them. Mitochondrial DNA, inherited only from mothers and lacking recombination, is shorter and more conserved than nuclear DNA, making it valuable for tracing ancestry and estimating evolutionary timing. Analyses of mtDNA led to the identification of “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans, who lived roughly 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa. DNA comparison also enables molecular clocks that estimate when species diverged and underlies modern ancestry analyses such as genetic testing services.  

(Word count: 399)Overall winner: This lecture examined nested queries in SQL—queries embedded within other queries—and how their logical structure affects both meaning and optimization. Building on prior knowledge of SQL operations such as projection, selection, joins, and aggregation, it introduced **subqueries** as a means to express conditions or intermediate computations that must be derived from the data itself. Subqueries can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and may return either a single scalar value or a full relation.  

A key theme was when nesting is useful versus when it should be avoided. The slides emphasize a practical rule of thumb: while it is usually better to “unnest” a subquery into an equivalent join for clarity and efficiency, **some situations make this impossible**. Examples in the SELECT clause showed how correlated subqueries compute related attributes—such as linking each actor with a movie’s genre or averaging salaries for highly rated films—and how equivalent results often can, but not always should, be expressed through joins and aggregations. These examples highlighted that rewriting can change semantics when outer joins or filtering conditions are involved.

Subqueries in the **FROM** clause act as temporary tables or views, either written inline or defined through **common table expressions (CTEs)**. This approach, known as subquery refactoring, improves readability while preserving execution logic. When appearing in the **WHERE** clause, subqueries introduce logical expressions corresponding to **quantifiers**. Using **EXISTS** and **IN**, SQL expresses *existential quantification* (“there exists” or “for some”), enabling checks for the presence or membership of tuples. These are said to be straightforward because their logic aligns directly with join predicates.

By contrast, **universal quantifiers** (“for all” or “every”) are more difficult, since SQL lacks an explicit construct for them. The lecture demonstrated how such logic must be simulated using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS** patterns, such as retrieving actors who acted only in Action movies or movies where all salaries exceed $100K. These examples required multi-step reasoning—first identifying counterexamples and then excluding them—revealing why universal quantification tends to be more complex and less easily rewritten.

The lecture concluded by linking these logical ideas to **query monotonicity**. A query is *monotone* if adding tuples to its input cannot remove tuples from its output. Simple SELECT–FROM–WHERE statements without subqueries or aggregates are monotone and can therefore be safely unnested. Queries involving **negation, aggregation, or universal quantification** are **non‑monotone**, meaning nesting cannot be eliminated without altering results. This distinction explains when subquery unnesting is feasible and when it fundamentally is not.

(Word count: 394)Overall winner: This lecture covered **nested SQL queries**—queries written inside other queries—and how their structure affects query meaning and optimization. Building on earlier SQL operations (projection, selection, joins, and aggregation), it introduced **subqueries** as expressions that compute intermediate results required by an outer query. Subqueries can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and may return either a single value or a relation.  

A guiding principle is to **avoid nested queries when possible**, since equivalent results are often achievable with joins or grouping. However, some cases make *unnesting* impossible. Examples in the **SELECT** clause illustrated both correlated subqueries (e.g., matching an actor to movie genre or computing the average salary for movies rated above 9) and their join-based equivalents. These comparisons showed when nested approaches and join rewrites yield the same logic and when subtle differences—especially with outer joins or filtering—affect semantics.

In the **FROM** clause, a subquery supplies a derived relation that can simplify complex logic. The lecture introduced **subquery refactoring** and the **common table expression (CTE)** syntax `WITH myTable AS (...)`, which defines a temporary view used in subsequent queries. This technique improves readability without changing query behavior.

Subqueries in the **WHERE** clause introduce **logical quantifiers**. Constructs such as **EXISTS** and **IN** represent *existential quantification* (“there exists” or “for some”), enabling checks for tuple membership. These are conceptually straightforward since they mirror join behavior. More difficult are **universal quantifiers** (“for all” or “every”), for which SQL provides no direct operator. The lecture demonstrated how to simulate these using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, such as finding actors who acted only in Action movies or movies where all actors earn over \$100K. These required multi‑step reasoning—first identifying exceptions, then excluding them—illustrating the complexity of “for all” logic.  

A related example on “actors in at most two Action movies” highlighted **bounded-count conditions**, which depend on aggregates rather than universal quantifiers, distinguishing aggregate limits from true “for all” forms.

The lecture concluded with the notion of **query monotonicity**. A query is *monotone* if adding tuples to inputs never removes outputs. Basic SELECT–FROM–WHERE queries without aggregates or subqueries are monotone and can be safely unnested. Queries involving **negation, aggregation, or universal quantifiers** are **non‑monotone**, meaning they cannot be rewritten without changing results. This distinction links logical structure directly to when subquery unnesting is feasible.  
*(≈394 words)*Overall winner: This lecture traced how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, based on Darwin’s postulates that populations face a struggle for existence, individuals vary in traits affecting survival and reproduction, and these variations are heritable. Although Darwin recognized variation as essential, he was uncertain about its origin, inheritance, and maintenance. Artificial selection in domesticated plants and animals—such as in dogs and pigeons—illustrated how selective breeding can rapidly produce distinct traits within only a few generations, showing that evolution can occur on observable timescales.

Empirical studies further demonstrate the tempo of evolutionary change: for example, minnows evolving placenta-like structures in short time spans, and complex organs such as eyes arising stepwise through many small modifications—about 1,800 incremental steps over roughly one million years. These examples underscore that both rapid and gradual evolutionary processes operate in nature.

The lecture then turned to genetics and inheritance. Mendel’s pea plant experiments showed that heredity follows particulate (not blending) principles—traits are governed by discrete units later called genes, and variation persists because alleles are transmitted independently from each parent. Rediscovery of Mendel’s work in the early 1900s connected these principles to cell structures. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, while Sutton and Boveri demonstrated that chromosomes carry hereditary material, forming the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Understanding chromosomes proved central to explaining how inheritance drives evolution.

Cell division occurs by mitosis, producing identical diploid somatic cells, and by meiosis, which creates haploid gametes. During meiosis, recombination and independent assortment introduce variation; genes close together on chromosomes may show linkage, with recombination rate indicating the likelihood of crossing over. Mendel’s subsequent findings distinguished genotype from phenotype and clarified dominance and recessivity in predictable inheritance patterns.

Finally, the molecular basis of heredity was revealed when Watson and Crick, using Franklin’s x-ray data, described DNA’s double-helix structure: a sugar-phosphate backbone with complementary bases (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional segments of DNA, and mitochondrial DNA—maternally inherited and conserved—has enabled dating of ancestry, such as identifying “Mitochondrial Eve” about 100,000–200,000 years ago in Africa. Molecular clocks using neutral DNA sites help infer divergence times, though these estimates depend on evolutionary rate assumptions and carry inherent uncertainty.  

(≈398 words)Overall winner: This lecture examined how natural selection and genetic inheritance explain evolutionary change by tracing ideas from Darwin to molecular genetics. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies in a population over time, supported by Darwin’s postulates: individuals compete for survival, exhibit heritable variation in traits affecting reproduction, and these traits are passed to offspring. Although Darwin recognized variation as essential, he lacked a mechanism to explain its maintenance. Artificial selection in domesticated species—such as dogs and pigeons—demonstrated that selective breeding can produce large changes within a few generations, illustrating that evolution can occur rapidly when selection pressures are strong.

Examples of evolutionary rate variation included minnows evolving placenta-like structures in short time periods and complex traits, such as the eye, forming gradually through many small steps. These show that evolution can proceed both quickly and incrementally.

Modern genetics resolved Darwin’s problem of heredity. Mendel’s pea plant experiments showed that traits are passed as discrete “particles,” now called genes, maintaining variation through distinct alleles inherited from each parent. Rediscovery of his work led to the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, and Sutton and Boveri demonstrated that chromosomes carry hereditary material. In humans, there are 46 chromosomes arranged in 23 homologous pairs—22 autosomal and one pair of sex chromosomes—which can be visualized through a karyotype. This understanding linked inheritance at the cellular level to evolutionary processes.

Cell division occurs through mitosis for somatic cells and meiosis for gametes. Meiosis halves chromosome number to preserve species totals through sexual reproduction and introduces variation through recombination and independent assortment. Genes on the same chromosome can be linked, but crossing over during meiosis can produce new allele combinations; the recombination rate measures how often this occurs.

At the molecular level, Watson and Crick, using Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray images, described DNA’s double-helix structure of sugar-phosphate backbones and paired bases (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional DNA sequences; humans have about 20,000–25,000. Mitochondrial DNA, inherited maternally without recombination, was the first human DNA sequenced and has been used to identify “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent common matrilineal ancestor from East Africa about 100,000–200,000 years ago. Comparing DNA sequences also allows estimation of species divergence through a molecular clock based on neutral genetic changes.

(≈394 words)Overall winner: This lecture explored **nested SQL queries**—queries written inside other queries—and how their placement and structure affect meaning and optimization. Building on earlier SQL features (projection, selection, joins, and aggregation), it defined **subqueries** as inner queries that supply values or relations needed by an outer query. Subqueries can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and may return a single value or a full table.  

A key guideline is to **avoid nesting when possible**, since equivalent results are often obtainable through joins or grouping. Slides illustrated this “subquery unnesting” for queries such as finding each actor’s movie genre or computing the average actor salary for highly rated films. These examples compared correlated subqueries with their join-based counterparts and showed that small changes—like using an outer join—can alter semantics and make queries non‑equivalent.  

In the **FROM** clause, subqueries serve as **derived tables**, allowing complex conditions to be isolated and reused. The lecture demonstrated “subquery refactoring” using the **WITH** clause (common table expression) to improve readability without changing results; these can often also be unnested.  

Subqueries in the **WHERE** clause introduced **logical quantifiers**. Constructs such as **EXISTS** and **IN** capture *existential quantification* (“there exists” or “for some”), which align closely with join operations and are relatively straightforward. The lecture then covered **universal quantifiers** (“for all” or “every”), which SQL lacks direct syntax for. The slides broke this logic into a clear two‑step method:  
1. Identify actors who have participated in at least one non‑Action movie.  
2. Retrieve those **not in** that result—actors who acted *only* in Action movies.  
Additional examples used **NOT EXISTS** or comparison forms like `$100K < ALL(...)` to check conditions true for every tuple (e.g., all actors in a movie earning above \$100K). These formulations illustrated that universal quantifiers often require **negation** and thus differ fundamentally from existential ones. A related case study—finding actors appearing in at most two Action movies—showed how aggregate conditions use **counting** rather than universal quantification.  

The lecture concluded with **query monotonicity**: a query is *monotone* if adding tuples to input relations never removes output tuples. A basic SELECT–FROM–WHERE query without subqueries or aggregation is provably monotone under nested‑loop semantics. The final theorem linked logic to optimization:  
- If a query is monotone, it **can be unnested**.  
- If it includes **universal quantifiers, negation, or aggregates**, it becomes **non‑monotone** and **cannot be unnested** without changing meaning.

(Word count: 394)Overall winner: This lecture connected natural selection with the genetic mechanisms that explain inheritance and evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, following Darwin’s postulates that organisms struggle for existence, vary in traits influencing survival and reproduction, and transmit this variation to offspring. However, Darwin himself faced difficulties explaining how variation arises and persists—questions later answered through genetics. Humans possess 46 chromosomes (23 homologous pairs: 22 autosomal and one sex pair), although other species differ greatly in number. (Word count: 396)Overall winner: This lecture examined **nested SQL queries**, focusing on how their placement, meaning, and performance implications shape query design. Building on earlier SQL concepts—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and updates—it defined a **subquery** as an inner query whose output feeds an outer query. Subqueries can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clause and return either a single value or an entire relation.  

A key principle (Slide 3) is to **avoid nested queries when possible**, primarily for **performance reasons**: correlated subqueries can be computationally expensive because they execute once per outer‑query row. When possible, they should be replaced by equivalent **join‑based** solutions, a process shown repeatedly as **subquery unnesting**. For example, finding each actor’s movie genre or the average actor salary for movies with rating > 9 can be written either with correlated subqueries or with joins and grouping operations. This approach not only improves clarity but also supports incremental construction of complex queries, showing its functional role beyond readability.  

In the **WHERE** clause, the lecture linked subqueries to **logical quantifiers**. **EXISTS** and **IN** correspond to *existential quantification* (“there exists”), which is conceptually equivalent to testing for matching tuples via joins and is relatively easy to unnest. **Universal quantification** (“for all”) is more complex: SQL expresses it indirectly through forms such as **< ALL(...)** or **NOT EXISTS**. The slides implemented this logic stepwise—first finding actors with any non‑Action role, then excluding them to list those who acted only in Action films. Other examples measured counts (“actors in at most two Action movies”) or checked conditions across all tuples (“all actors in a movie earn > $100K”).  

The lecture concluded with **query monotonicity**, describing how adding input tuples affects results. A basic **SELECT–FROM–WHERE** query without subqueries or aggregates is **monotone**, meaning it never loses output when data increase. **Monotone queries can be safely unnested**, while those with **universal quantifiers, negation, or aggregates** are **non‑monotone** and cannot be flattened without changing semantics.

Cell division occurs through mitosis, which produces identical diploid body cells, and meiosis, which generates haploid gametes. Meiosis introduces genetic variability through mutation, recombination, and independent assortment. Mendel’s principles distinguished genotype (genetic composition) from phenotype (observable traits) and showed how dominant and recessive alleles interact. Linkage and recombination further explain that while traits on different chromosomes assort independently, genes located close together may be inherited jointly; crossing over creates new allele combinations and contributes to variation.  

The discovery of DNA’s double‑helix structure by Watson and Crick, derived from Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray data, unveiled the molecular basis of heredity. DNA consists of a sugar‑phosphate backbone and paired bases (A–T, C–G) that enable accurate replication. Genes are functional DNA sequences, and mitochondrial DNA—smaller, maternally inherited, and lacking recombination—helps trace evolutionary lineages, identifying “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans about 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa.  

*(Word count: 399)*Overall winner: The lecture examined how race and culture are socially constructed systems that shape identity, law, and education in the United States. It began with reflection questions inviting students to consider how schools label and categorize individuals, how such labels shape self-perceptions of intelligence, motivation, and capability, and whether they capture or distort one’s full identity. This led into a discussion of the pervasive **silence, fear, and denial** that surround conversations about race and cultural difference in schools and society, and the need for **courage** to confront how social views and personal upbringing influence our assumptions.  

Drawing on Sonia Nieto, the lecture explained that society sorts people through visible and invisible traits—race, ethnicity, gender, religion, and class—using these classifications to rationalize unequal policies and practices. These categories exaggerate differences and produce stereotypes that favor some groups while disadvantaging others. Race, in particular, is not biologically determined but a social construct formed through historical, political, and economic processes that have made racial categories appear “common sense.” Projects such as *RACE: Are We So Different?* use scientific evidence to dispel enduring racial myths.  

Law played a powerful role in constructing race. Ian Haney López described **law as coercion**, physically shaping who could belong through immigration restrictions, marriage laws, and naturalization policies that tied citizenship to whiteness and “moral character.” He also described **law as ideology**, showing how legal systems make racial divisions seem natural and legitimate. Cases involving Armenians, Japanese, and Mexicans illustrated how courts defined “whiteness” inconsistently to maintain privilege. The **U.S. Census** also institutionalized racial categories, forcing people to identify within limited options that changed over time—highlighting how the state codifies identity. Even after bans on interracial marriage were overturned, racial ideology persisted in “race-neutral” laws and practices such as criminal sentencing and school discipline.  

The second half turned to **culture**. Nieto defined culture as the evolving values, relationships, and worldviews created and transformed by groups. Culture is learned, not genetic; dynamic, not fixed; and hybrid, not singular. It is shaped by many mediating factors—family, peers, class, language, gender, community, and media—and cannot be reduced to ethnicity or traditions alone. Schools embody their own culture through routines, tracking, resources, and assumptions that ability is fixed and hierarchical. These beliefs shape definitions of “smart” and “bad” students, influencing inclusion, participation, and achievement. Understanding race and culture as socially constructed reveals how education helps reproduce or challenge inequality.  

(Word count: 398)Overall winner: This lecture traced the development of ideas linking heredity and evolution, from Darwin’s postulates of natural selection to Mendel’s particulate inheritance and the discovery of DNA’s molecular structure.  

Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time. Natural selection operates when organisms struggle for existence, individuals vary in heritable traits affecting survival and reproduction, and those traits are passed to their offspring. Darwin recognized the importance of variation but could not explain how it was maintained or inherited. Artificial selection—humans selectively breeding plants and animals such as dogs and pigeons—demonstrated that substantial change can occur within a few generations. Quantitative examples, such as minnows evolving a placenta‑like structure and the eye developing through about 1,800 small, 1 % optical improvements over roughly one million years, illustrated measurable and incremental evolutionary change.  

Mendel’s pea plant experiments (1856–1863) established the particulate nature of inheritance: each parent contributes discrete “particles,” now known as genes, which do not blend but remain intact across generations. Rediscovery of his work in the early 1900s linked genetics to cell biology. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, and later, Walter Sutton and Theodor Boveri proposed that chromosomes carry the hereditary material—the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance. Humans possess 46 chromosomes—22 pairs of somatic chromosomes and one pair of sex chromosomes—though chromosome counts vary by species.  

Cell division occurs by mitosis, producing identical diploid body cells, and by meiosis, forming haploid gametes. During meiosis, variation arises through mutation, recombination, and independent assortment. Mendel’s distinctions between genotype (genetic composition) and phenotype (observable expression) and the roles of dominant and recessive alleles explain predictable inheritance patterns. Linkage and recombination further show that genes on different chromosomes assort independently, but closely located genes may be inherited together; crossing over during meiosis can create new allele combinations, generating additional variation.  

The discovery of DNA’s double‑helix structure by James Watson and Francis Crick in 1953, based on Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray data, revealed the molecular basis of heredity. DNA consists of a sugar‑phosphate backbone and paired bases (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional sequences of DNA, while mitochondrial DNA—shorter, maternally inherited, and not subject to recombination—provides evidence for a common matrilineal ancestor, “Mitochondrial Eve,” who lived about 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa.  

*(Word count: 394)*Overall winner: The lecture explored how race and culture are social constructions that shape identity, law, and education in the United States. It began with reflection questions about how schools label students and how those labels affect self-perception, motivation, and beliefs about intelligence. This led into a discussion of the fear and silence surrounding issues of race and cultural difference—emphasizing that courage and honest reflection are needed to confront how upbringing and societal messages shape our assumptions.  

Drawing on Sonia Nieto, the lecture explained that society classifies people by visible and invisible traits such as race, ethnicity, gender, and class, using these categories to justify privilege for some groups and disadvantage for others. Race, though widely assumed to be biological, is socially constructed through historical, political, and economic processes that have made it seem “common sense.” Projects like *RACE: Are We So Different?* use scientific evidence to challenge these myths.  

Law was shown to be a central force in constructing race. Ian Haney López described **law as coercion**, shaping who could belong through immigration, naturalization, and marriage laws that tied citizenship and rights to whiteness and “moral character.” Specific cases revealed the arbitrariness of these definitions: Armenians successfully argued for whiteness and citizenship in 1909 because they were not perceived as a threat, while a 1922 Japanese petition was denied, exposing the inconsistency of race-by-skin-color reasoning. Mexican legal status also shifted as U.S. expansion and prejudice redefined them as distinct and inferior. The **U.S. Census** institutionalized race further by forcing identification into categories that changed over time, often excluding or misclassifying groups such as those of Middle Eastern or North African descent. Laws later banning interracial marriage persisted until 1967, illustrating how the state maintained racial boundaries. López also described **law as ideology**, showing how race-neutral legal and media narratives reinforce racial hierarchy even without explicit racial language.  

The lecture then turned to **culture**, with Nieto defining it as dynamic, learned, and hybrid—a product of shared values, relationships, and worldviews created and changed through social interaction. Culture is shaped by multiple mediating factors such as family, peers, gender, class, language, and community. Schools have their own distinct cultures—established through routines, tracking, discipline systems, and unequal access to programs—which rest on beliefs that ability is fixed and can be measured. These assumptions influence how schools define who is “smart” or “bad,” shaping opportunity, participation, and achievement. Understanding race and culture as fluid and socially produced exposes how educational systems can either reproduce or challenge inequality.

(Word count: 398)Overall winner: This lecture explained how natural selection and genetic inheritance together account for evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, based on Darwin’s postulates that organisms compete for existence, vary in traits linked to survival and reproduction, and pass these variations to their offspring. Although Darwin could not explain how variation was preserved or generated, the emergence of genetics later solved these puzzles. Artificial selection in dogs, pigeons, and crops showed that targeted breeding can produce rapid and observable change, while natural processes work more slowly through accumulated small differences—such as an eye evolving through about 1,800 incremental steps over roughly one million years.

Gregor Mendel’s pea plant experiments (1856–1863) demonstrated that traits are inherited through discrete “particles,” now understood as genes, rather than blending together. This particulate inheritance allows variation to persist across generations. His results distinguished genotype—the allele combinations an organism carries—from phenotype—the visible trait—and showed that dominant alleles mask recessive ones in hybrids. When heterozygotes reproduce, meiosis separates alleles into gametes, and recombination or crossing-over can create new allele combinations, thereby increasing genetic diversity. Independent assortment explains why genes on different chromosomes segregate independently, while linkage reveals that closely located genes may not.

In the early 1900s, research connected Mendel’s factors to physical structures in cells. Walther Flemming discovered chromosomes and mitosis, and Sutton and Boveri proposed the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance, demonstrating that hereditary material resides in chromosomes. Mitosis produces identical diploid cells, whereas meiosis forms haploid gametes, halving the chromosome number to maintain species consistency after fertilization. Understanding these processes revealed how heritable variation arises, the foundation for evolutionary change.

Later, the discovery of DNA provided the molecular basis of heredity. In 1953, Watson and Crick, building on Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray images, described DNA’s double‑helix structure of sugar‑phosphate backbones and complementary base pairs (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, and some exist in mitochondria as mtDNA—shorter, maternally inherited, and lacking recombination. Comparisons of mtDNA have helped trace evolutionary relationships, including identifying “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent common matrilineal ancestor of modern humans living roughly 100,000–200,000 years ago in Africa.

(Word count: 398)Overall winner: This lecture traced how natural selection and genetic inheritance together explain evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as the change in allele frequencies within a population over time, based on Darwin’s postulates that organisms face a struggle for existence, vary in traits related to survival and reproduction, and transmit those variations to offspring. Darwin, however, could not explain how variation persisted or new traits arose, challenges later resolved through genetics. Rates of change can differ—some species, like minnows, evolve new features such as placenta-like structures rapidly, while complex organs like the eye evolve slowly through many small functional steps, estimated at about 1,800 incremental changes over roughly one million years.

This particulate inheritance preserves genetic variation across generations. Mendel’s laws distinguished genotype (allele combinations) from phenotype (observable traits), showing that dominant alleles mask recessive ones. Subsequent research connected these hereditary factors to cellular structures. The discovery of DNA provided the molecular foundation for inheritance. Watson and Crick, drawing on Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray data, described DNA’s double‑helix structure of sugar‑phosphate backbones and complementary base pairs (A–T, C–G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, while smaller mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) is inherited only from mothers and lacks recombination. mtDNA’s consistency and slower change rate help estimate divergence times, as in identifying “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent shared matrilineal ancestor, inferred to have lived roughly 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa using molecular‑clock approaches.

(Word count: 398)Overall winner: This lecture explained how natural selection and genetic inheritance together shape evolutionary change. Evolution is defined as a change in allele frequencies within populations over time, grounded in Darwin’s postulates that there is a struggle for existence, variation in traits affecting survival and reproduction, and heritable transmission of those traits. Darwin, however, lacked a clear mechanism for how variation was preserved or how new traits appeared—questions later clarified through genetics rather than resolved at the time.

Artificial selection in domesticated animals and plants—such as dogs and pigeons—provides a concrete example of how targeted breeding can produce rapid changes across few generations, illustrating the same principles that natural selection operates on more gradually. The pace of change can vary: some species, like minnows, show rapid evolution (e.g., a placenta‑like structure), while complex features such as eyes can evolve through many small, functional steps (the slide example estimated around 1,800 incremental changes over about one million years).

Mendel’s pea plant experiments (1856–1863) demonstrated that traits are inherited through discrete “particles” rather than blending, maintaining variation within populations. These hereditary units—genes—occur in alternative forms called alleles. An organism’s genotype refers to its allele combination, while phenotype describes the expressed trait. Dominant alleles mask recessive ones, and during meiosis alleles segregate so each gamete carries one from each pair. Recombination or crossing‑over creates new allele combinations, while independent assortment ensures genes on different chromosomes inherit separately, except when closely linked loci reduce independence.

Cytological studies connected these hereditary “particles” to cellular structures. Walther Flemming identified chromatin and mitosis, and later Sutton and Boveri established the Chromosome Theory of Inheritance, recognizing chromosomes as the carriers of genetic material. Mitosis produces identical diploid cells, whereas meiosis forms haploid gametes, maintaining chromosome numbers and enabling genetic variation through recombination and mutation.

The discovery of DNA revealed the molecular basis of inheritance. Watson and Crick, using Rosalind Franklin’s x‑ray evidence, described DNA’s double‑helix structure of sugar‑phosphate backbones and base pairs (A with T, C with G). Genes are functional DNA sequences, while mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) occurs separately in mitochondria and is passed only through the maternal line without recombination. Because mtDNA evolves slowly and predictably, it helps estimate divergence times using molecular clocks—supporting concepts such as “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans, inferred to have lived roughly 100,000–200,000 years ago in East Africa.

*(~386 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined how natural selection and genetic inheritance jointly explain evolutionary change. Evolution was defined as changes in allele frequencies within populations over time, grounded in Darwin’s postulates: organisms face a struggle for existence, show heritable variation in traits related to survival and reproduction, and differential survival leads to evolutionary change. Darwin could not explain how variation persisted or new traits arose—questions later answered through Mendelian genetics.

Genes are functional segments of DNA, and mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA)—inherited only from mothers—provides a tool for tracing ancestry. Its stability allows estimation of divergence times using molecular clocks that compare neutral DNA regions. Analyses of mtDNA support the concept of a “Mitochondrial Eve,” the most recent matrilineal ancestor of all humans, living roughly 100–200 thousand years ago in East Africa.

(Approximately 398 words)Overall winner: This lecture examined nested queries in SQL—queries that include other queries as components—and their relationship to query optimization and monotonicity. Building on earlier coverage of SQL operations such as selection, projection, joins, grouping, and aggregation, it introduced subqueries as constructs that can appear within the SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses. Subqueries can return single values, sets of values, or entire relations. A central practical guideline emphasized is to **avoid nested queries when possible**, since join-based or flattened rewritings (“unnesting”) are often more efficient; however, the lecture noted that sometimes nested forms are unavoidable for expressing certain conditions.

For **subqueries in SELECT**, examples included retrieving each actor’s movie genre, the number of actors per movie, and average salaries for highly rated films. The slides demonstrated that correlated subqueries, where the inner query depends on the outer one, can often be replaced by equivalent join and GROUP BY forms—a process called subquery unnesting. Yet, differing join types (e.g., inner vs. outer) can affect equivalence when null or missing values occur.

**Subqueries in the FROM** clause were introduced as subquery refactoring, treating a derived relation as a temporary table or as a named common table expression (CTE). This approach improves readability and modularity without changing semantics.  

For **subqueries in the WHERE** clause, the lecture connected logic concepts to SQL through quantifiers. *Existential quantifiers* (“there exists”) were represented using `EXISTS` or `IN`, returning true if the subquery produces one or more matching tuples. These can generally be rewritten as join queries and are conceptually straightforward. In contrast, *universal quantifiers* (“for all”) are harder: SQL lacks direct syntax for them, but their meaning can be simulated using negation constructs like `NOT IN`, `NOT EXISTS`, or comparisons with `ALL`. Examples included finding actors who acted **only in action movies** or movies where **all actors’ salaries exceed $100K**, illustrating multi-step reasoning and the complexity of such queries.

The lecture concluded by linking quantification to **monotone vs. non-monotone** query behavior. A monotone query—one whose output never loses tuples when more data are added—can be safely unnested. Simple SELECT–FROM–WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone. In contrast, queries involving negation, universal quantification, or aggregation are **non‑monotone**, as shown by slide examples using `NOT IN`. Because their outputs can shrink when new data are inserted, they cannot be unnested reliably, defining a key boundary for SQL query optimization.  

*(≈395 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined nested SQL queries—subqueries embedded within other queries—and how their structure affects query formulation, equivalence, and optimization. After reviewing core SQL features such as projection, selection, joins, grouping, and aggregation, the lecture introduced subqueries as queries that can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses and can return either a single value or an entire relation. A guiding principle was emphasized early: **avoid nested queries when possible**, as they often reduce efficiency, though some tasks require them because certain conditions depend on intermediate results that must be computed first.

In subqueries within the **SELECT** clause, examples illustrated computing related attributes such as movie genres, the number of actors per movie, or the average salary of actors in highly rated films. These correlated subqueries are often functionally equivalent to join-based or aggregate forms, but the lecture showed that their equivalence depends on join type. For instance, queries using inner joins may differ from those using **LEFT OUTER JOIN**, highlighting that equality does not always hold across all unnesting transformations.

Subqueries in the **FROM** clause were presented as a way to create intermediate tables, either directly as inline views or using **WITH** clauses for common table expressions (CTEs). This approach, sometimes called *sub-query refactoring*, improves organization while preserving logical meaning and can often be rewritten as non-nested queries through unnesting.

Subqueries in the **WHERE** clause introduced logical quantifiers. Conditions using **EXISTS** or **IN** represent **existential quantifiers**, checking for the presence or membership of related tuples—conceptually equivalent to join queries that test existence. In contrast, **universal quantifiers** (“for all”) are more complex and not directly supported in SQL. To express them, the lecture demonstrated constructions using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, such as finding actors who acted only in action movies or movies where all actors earn above \$100K. These examples showed how universal conditions require multi-step logic or negation-based rewrites.

The lecture concluded with the relationship between query structure and monotonicity. A **monotone query**—one whose results never shrink as data grows—can typically be safely unnested. **Non-monotone queries**, which involve negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates, cannot. Understanding this distinction explains why some nested queries can be optimized into simpler join forms, while others must remain nested due to their logical dependencies.  

**(400 words)**Overall winner: This lecture explored **nested SQL queries**, focusing on where subqueries appear (in SELECT, FROM, or WHERE clauses) and how their structure relates to optimization and query **monotonicity**. Building on prior material about SQL projections, selections, joins, and aggregates, it emphasized that subqueries—queries embedded within others—let us express conditions that must first compute a relation. However, the **rule of thumb** is to avoid nesting when possible because unnested (joined) versions are often more efficient, though some logic requires true nesting.

For **subqueries in SELECT**, examples included retrieving each actor’s movie genre, counting actors per movie, and computing average salaries in films rated > 9. The slides compared these correlated subqueries—where the inner query references outer variables—with equivalent **join + GROUP BY** statements, illustrating **subquery unnesting**. Slide 10 specifically highlighted that inner and left outer joins may yield different results when rows are missing, so equivalence must be verified carefully. This distinction showed that efficiency gains must not compromise correctness.

**Subqueries in FROM** demonstrated **subquery refactoring**, where a derived relation is treated as a temporary table, or expressed through a **common table expression (CTE)** using `WITH`. This improves modularity and readability without altering meaning and can often be flattened into an equivalent single‑query form.

For **subqueries in WHERE**, the lecture mapped SQL operators to logical **quantifiers**.  
– **Existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) were expressed with `EXISTS` or `IN`, evaluating to TRUE if the subquery returns any record. These can often be converted to joins and are conceptually straightforward.  
– **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) required multi‑step reasoning because SQL lacks a direct keyword. Their intent was simulated using `NOT IN`, `NOT EXISTS`, or comparison with `ALL`. Examples included finding actors who acted **only in action movies** and movies whose **all actors earned > $100K**, illustrating equivalence through negation and the difficulty of expressing “for every” logic.

The lecture concluded by formalizing the link between quantification and **monotonicity**. A **monotone** query never loses output tuples when input data increase; such queries can be **unnested safely**. Simple **SELECT–FROM–WHERE** queries **without subqueries or aggregates** are guaranteed monotone—a theorem proved via nested‑loop semantics. Conversely, queries using **negation, universal quantifiers, or aggregates** are **non‑monotone**, meaning their outputs may shrink when new tuples are added and thus **cannot be reliably unnested**, establishing the theoretical boundary for SQL query optimization.

Such expressions can often be replaced by equivalent joins. **Universal quantifiers** (“for all”) are more complex and lack direct SQL support. Examples included retrieving actors who worked only in Action films or movies where all actors earned more than $100K. These required multi‑step logic using **NOT IN** or **NOT EXISTS**, since the **ALL** operator is unsupported in SQLite.

The lecture concluded with **monotone and non‑monotone queries**. A query Q is monotone if *adding tuples to input tables never removes tuples from the output* (Slide 27). Simple SELECT‑FROM‑WHERE queries without subqueries or aggregates are monotone—as shown through examples returning actors by genre—and can be unnested. Queries involving **negation**, **universal quantifiers**, or **aggregation** are **non‑monotone** (Slides 30–32), meaning unnesting could change their results. This monotonicity distinction defines when optimizing nested queries through unnesting is logically valid.  

≈398 wordsOverall winner: This lecture examined **nested and subqueries in SQL**, their various forms, logical interpretations, and implications for optimization and query monotonicity. Building on prior topics such as projections, selections, joins, and aggregates, it introduced how queries can contain other queries and how nesting affects both expressiveness and efficiency.

A **subquery** is a query embedded within another and can appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. A **nested query** often refers to a subquery correlated with its parent, meaning it depends on outer query values. Although SQL allows such constructs, the lecture’s rule of thumb (Slide 3) stressed: *avoid nested queries when possible—but sometimes it is impossible.* Subqueries can return a single value or an entire relation. When equivalent results can be obtained through simple joins or grouping, **subquery unnesting** (rewriting them as joins) improves clarity and performance.

Examples in **SELECT clause** subqueries included finding each actor’s movie genre, counting how many actors appear per movie, or computing the average salary for movies rated above nine. Paired queries demonstrated that such subqueries are frequently equivalent to join‑based formulations, though outer joins may alter equivalence. In the **FROM clause**, subqueries can act as temporary tables or **common table expressions (CTEs)**, supporting cleaner logic refactoring, as shown in filtering movies by rating ranges.

The **WHERE clause** cases introduced **quantifiers**. Using `EXISTS` or `IN` implements **existential quantifiers** (“there exists”) to check for the presence or membership of related tuples and can generally be replaced with join formulations. In contrast, **universal quantifiers** (“for all”) are trickier because SQL lacks direct syntax. The lecture illustrated how to handle them indirectly—for example, selecting actors who only acted in action movies or movies whose actors all earn above $100 K—by combining `NOT IN`, `NOT EXISTS`, or multi‑step exclusion logic. Certain universal‑form queries (e.g., `$100 K < ALL (...)`) were noted as **not supported in SQLite**, underscoring limitations of real systems.

Finally, the lecture connected these ideas to **query monotonicity**. A **monotone query** (without negation, aggregates, or universal conditions) never loses results when data is added and can therefore be unnested safely. **Non‑monotone queries**, which include negation or universal quantification, cannot be unnested this way. This conceptual link between logic, optimization, and expressiveness concluded the discussion of nested SQL queries.

*(~392 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examined **nested and subqueries in SQL**, their use across different clauses, associated logical meanings, and optimization concepts such as **subquery unnesting** and **query monotonicity**. Building on prior SQL operators—projections, selections, joins, aggregates, grouping, and modification statements—it showed how one query can be embedded within another to express complex conditions.

A **subquery** is a query inside another query and may appear in the **SELECT**, **FROM**, or **WHERE** clauses. **Nested queries** are often correlated, meaning the inner query depends on the outer one. As noted on Slide 3: avoid nested queries when possible, though some tasks require them. Subqueries can return single values or entire relations; when an equivalent join or grouping form exists, **unnesting** improves efficiency.

In the **SELECT clause**, examples included finding each actor’s movie genre, counting actors per movie, and averaging salaries for highly rated films. These examples demonstrated that a nested subquery can often be rewritten as a join. However, Slide 10 emphasized that equivalence may fail if outer joins are used, since left outer joins can include tuples with NULL values not captured by the nested form.

Subqueries in the **FROM clause** act as temporary tables or **common table expressions (CTEs)** to refactor queries—for instance, filtering movies with ratings between 8 and 9. Although semantically similar to direct filtering, this approach improves organization and readability.

Subqueries in the **WHERE clause** illustrated logical **quantifiers**. Using `EXISTS` or `IN` implements **existential quantifiers** (“there exists”), representing at least one matching tuple; these queries are often flattenable into joins. In contrast, **universal quantifiers** (“for all”) are more complex because SQL lacks direct syntax. Slides 18–20 presented a two‑step strategy:  
1) find actors who acted in at least one *non‑action* movie,  
2) then retrieve those **not** in that set—yielding actors who acted **only** in action movies.  
The lecture extended this reasoning to other uses, such as verifying that all actors in a movie earn more than $100 K. Since SQLite does not support `$100K < ALL (...)`, Slide 26 showed an equivalent `NOT EXISTS` rewrite to express universal constraints.

Finally, the lecture connected these ideas to **query monotonicity**. A **monotone query** (no negation, aggregation, or universal form) never loses results when new data are added and can therefore be safely unnested. **Non‑monotone queries**, which include negation, aggregates, or universal quantifiers, cannot be reliably unnested. This connection between logical form and optimization closed the discussion of nested SQL queries.  

*(~395 words)*Overall winner: This lecture examines within-subjects designs as an alternative to between-groups approaches. In between-groups designs, each participant contributes data from just one condition, so differences between people add noise that can obscure real effects. Within-subjects designs address this problem by having each participant experience all conditions and comparing each person to themselves across those conditions. Because individual variability is largely removed, within-subjects designs yield a more powerful test and require fewer participants to achieve the same statistical confidence.

Several forms of within-subjects designs were outlined. **Pretest–posttest designs** measure participants before and after an intervention; **repeated-measures designs** expose participants to two or more levels of an independent variable in one session; and **longitudinal designs** measure change across extended time periods. These setups are efficient but also introduce challenges such as **demand characteristics**, in which participants infer the experimental purpose, and **carryover effects**, where one condition influences performance in later ones. Examples include fatigue (declining performance), practice (improving performance through learning), and broader contextual effects such as **history** (external events), **maturation** (natural development or aging), and **testing effects** (improvement due to prior exposure to the same test).

**Order effects**—where outcomes depend on the sequence of conditions—are a particular concern. They can make one condition appear better simply because it was experienced first or second. To handle this, researchers use **counterbalancing**, adjusting the order of conditions across participants. In *complete counterbalancing*, each participant experiences all possible condition orders, as in an **ABBA design** (for two conditions) or **block randomization** (for three or more). When every possible order is impractical, *incomplete counterbalancing* methods such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs** ensure that each condition appears equally often in each serial position and follows each other condition the same number of times.

The lecture illustrated these issues through research examples such as the “**spaced text in dyslexia**” study, which asked whether reading improves with extra letter spacing—a case that also raises questions about complete versus incomplete within-subjects designs. In cognitive science, where experiments often contain many short trials, conditions are sometimes **intermixed**, which basically cancels out order effects, except when the task would become confusing or overly long. Finally, counterbalancing has limits: some manipulations can’t be reversed—participants can’t “un-take” a drug, “unlearn” a skill, or “un-see” a stimulus. Overall, within-subjects designs provide an efficient, powerful way to detect effects while demanding careful control of order and carryover influences.

*(≈398 words)*Overall winner: Within-subjects designs provide an alternative to between-groups research by allowing each participant to serve as their own comparison. In a between-groups design, participants contribute data in only one condition, and individual differences between participants increase variability (“noise”) that can obscure real effects. Within-subjects designs test the same people in all conditions, comparing each participant to themselves. This removes much of the participant-related noise, increases statistical power, and allows researchers to achieve the same number of data points per condition with fewer participants.

Several forms of within-subjects designs were outlined. A **pretest–posttest design** involves two conditions—measurement before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions, such as varying difficulty levels within the same task. A **longitudinal design** tracks performance over extended time periods, offering rich developmental information but requiring considerable time to complete. The main advantages of within-subjects designs are efficiency (fewer participants required) and sensitivity (reduced nuisance variability).

Nonetheless, several methodological concerns arise. **Demand characteristics** occur when participants experience all conditions and infer the experiment’s purpose. **Carryover effects** occur when exposure to one condition influences later performance—through **fatigue effects** (declining performance) or **practice effects** (improvement from repetition). Beyond carryover, the slides identify distinct “other concerns”:  
- **History effects**, when outside events during the study (exams, major news, holidays) influence results.  
- **Maturation effects**, when natural changes like growth or aging—not the manipulation—cause differences.  
- **Testing effects**, when taking the same test multiple times changes performance due to familiarity or expectations.

Because order influences can confound results, researchers employ **counterbalancing**. Complete counterbalancing requires each participant to experience all possible condition orders—for example, **ABBA** for two conditions or **block randomization** for three or more. When full counterbalancing becomes impractical, **incomplete methods** such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs** ensure each condition appears in each position across participants. Notably, random order with rotation requires a total number of participants that is a multiple of the number of conditions.  

In cognitive science studies with many short trials, experimenters may instead randomly intermix conditions to balance order effects automatically. However, counterbalancing cannot overcome irreversible manipulations such as drug administration, surgery, or unique experiences. Overall, within-subjects designs efficiently increase statistical power but demand careful attention to order and time-related influences.

*(≈398 words)*Overall winner: Within-subjects designs provide an alternative to between-groups research by testing the same participants in every condition. In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data to only one condition, and individual differences between participants add nuisance variability (“noise”) that can obscure true effects. Within-subjects designs eliminate much of this participant-related noise by comparing each person to themselves across conditions. This approach increases statistical power and efficiency: to obtain 20 data points per condition, researchers need only 20 participants instead of 40 (or 60, or 80) because each individual supplies data for all conditions.

Three common types of within-subjects designs were described. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** involves two or more conditions, such as varying task difficulty levels. A **longitudinal design** tracks the same individuals across extended time periods, providing valuable developmental insights but requiring more time to complete. Overall, these designs allow researchers to use fewer participants while achieving more powerful statistical tests.

However, within-subjects research carries several potential drawbacks. **Demand characteristics** arise when participants deduce the purpose of the study after experiencing every condition. **Carryover effects** occur when performance on one condition influences another—through **fatigue effects** (declining performance over time) or **practice effects** (improvement from repetition). Additional time-related concerns include **history effects** (outside events confounding results), **maturation effects** (natural change rather than manipulation driving differences), and **testing effects** (familiarity with the test altering later responses). A pretest–posttest control group can help estimate change unrelated to the intervention.

To address order effects, researchers use **counterbalancing**. In **complete counterbalancing**, each participant experiences every possible order—such as an **ABBA** sequence for two conditions, which controls for gradual practice effects but not for rapid, one-trial improvements. With three or more conditions, **block randomization** presents all conditions once per block in every possible order, though this becomes impractical with many conditions. **Incomplete counterbalancing**—through **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs**—ensures that each condition appears equally often in each position across participants; participant numbers must match multiples of the number of conditions.

Cognitive science experiments often involve many short trials, where researchers may instead randomly intermix conditions to cancel order effects automatically. Counterbalancing is preferable when items are few or lengthy, or when intermixing would confuse participants. Yet, some manipulations—like drug administration or surgery—cannot be counterbalanced because they are irreversible.  

**(Word count: 395)**Overall winner: Within-subjects designs provide an efficient alternative to between-groups research by testing the same participants in all conditions. In a between-groups design, each person contributes data to only one condition, and individual differences add nuisance variability (“noise”) that can mask true effects. In contrast, within-subjects designs compare each participant to themselves across conditions, reducing participant-related noise and increasing statistical power. Because each person serves as their own control, researchers can obtain the same number of data points with far fewer participants.

Several types of within-subjects designs were covered. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions, such as varying task difficulty levels. A **longitudinal design** measures the same individuals across multiple time points, providing rich developmental data but requiring greater time investment. Overall, these designs allow stronger inferences using smaller samples and more precise comparisons.

Despite these benefits, within-subjects designs face potential validity threats. **Demand characteristics** can arise when participants discern the study’s purpose after experiencing all conditions. **Carryover effects** occur when one condition influences another—through **fatigue effects** (worse performance over time) or **practice effects** (improvement with repetition). Time-related challenges include **history effects** (external events coinciding with the study), **maturation effects** (natural change rather than the intervention), and **testing effects** (repeated test exposure altering responses). Including a pretest–posttest control group can help separate true intervention effects from these sources of change.

**Order effects**—performance differences caused by the sequence of conditions—present a core design issue. Researchers can address them either by switching to a **between-subjects design** or by using **counterbalancing** to vary order. In **complete counterbalancing**, each participant experiences every possible order. For two conditions, an **ABBA** sequence controls gradual practice effects; for three or more, **block randomization** presents all conditions once per block in every possible order. When full counterbalancing is infeasible, **incomplete designs** such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs** ensure each condition appears equally often in each position across participants.

In cognitive science studies with many short trials, conditions may be randomly intermixed—often eliminating order effects. However, counterbalancing is preferred when intermixing would confuse participants or when trials are few or lengthy. Some manipulations, like taking a drug or unlearning a technique, cannot be counterbalanced at all.

**(Word count: 394)**Overall winner: This lecture focuses on **within-subjects designs** as a methodological counterpart to **between-groups designs**. In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data to only one condition, so differences among individuals introduce nuisance variability that can obscure the true effect. In a within-subjects design, every participant experiences all experimental conditions and is compared to themselves across conditions. This approach removes at least some participant-related noise, increases statistical power, and requires fewer participants to achieve the same number of data points per condition.

Several main types of within-subjects designs were covered. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions within one session (for example, different levels of task difficulty). A **longitudinal design** measures the same individuals at multiple points over time—highly informative but often slow to complete. These designs reduce nuisance variability, making them efficient and powerful, but they come with specific challenges.

Potential **threats to validity** include **demand characteristics** (participants guessing the study’s purpose), **carryover effects**, and several time-related threats. Carryover effects occur when earlier conditions influence later ones—either through **fatigue** (performance declines) or **practice** (performance improves). **History effects** arise when external events occur during the study and become confounded with the manipulation (e.g., exams or major campus events). **Maturation effects** reflect natural biological or developmental changes over time, and **testing effects** occur when repeated exposure to the same test alters performance. These issues can distort comparisons if uncontrolled.

**Order effects**—changes in outcomes based solely on sequence—are a central concern. Researchers can redesign the study as a between-subjects design or apply **counterbalancing** to distribute order effects evenly. In **complete counterbalancing**, each participant experiences all possible orders. Examples include the **ABBA design** for two conditions, which controls for gradual practice effects, and **block randomization** for three or more conditions, suitable only when tasks are very short. When complete counterbalancing is impractical, **incomplete designs** such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs** ensure each condition occurs equally often in each position and follows every other condition equally often.

In **cognitive science**, experiments with many quick trials often randomize conditions to cancel order effects, though counterbalancing is preferred when items are few or complex. Finally, counterbalancing cannot undo irreversible manipulations—like drug administration, surgery, or permanent learning—setting clear **limits** on its use.  

**(Word count: 396)**Overall winner: Within-subjects designs provide an efficient way to reduce nuisance variability by testing the same participants in all conditions. In a between-groups design, each person contributes data to only one condition, and individual differences introduce variability that can obscure true effects. In contrast, within-subjects designs compare each participant to themselves across conditions, removing participant-related noise and increasing statistical power. Because each person serves as their own control, researchers can obtain the same number of data points with fewer participants.

Several common forms were discussed. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions, such as varying a perceptual difficulty level. A **longitudinal design** follows the same individuals over time, offering valuable insight but requiring patience and extended data collection. These approaches enhance precision and efficiency rather than necessarily producing “stronger” causal inferences—they must still address validity threats.

Examples illustrated the design logic. In the dyslexia **letter-spacing study**, children read texts with normal versus wide spacing. Researchers had to decide whether each child would see both conditions (a within-subjects approach) or whether different groups would see different versions (between-subjects). The study also raised questions about **complete** versus **incomplete** within-subjects structures—in which everyone gets all conditions but not all possible orders.

Potential drawbacks include **demand characteristics**, where participants may guess the purpose after multiple conditions, and **carryover effects**, where one condition influences later ones through **fatigue** or **practice**. Time-based threats include **history effects** (outside events during the study), **maturation effects** (natural developmental change), and **testing effects** (being influenced by having taken the test before). Including a control pretest–posttest group can help isolate these influences.

**Order effects**—changes in performance due solely to sequence—pose a central challenge. Researchers can counter them by running a between-subjects design or by **counterbalancing** the order of conditions. In **complete counterbalancing**, every possible order is used: the **ABBA** design controls gradual practice effects, and **block randomization** organizes multiple conditions in all possible sequences. When that is impractical, **incomplete designs** such as **random rotation** or a **Latin-square design** ensure each condition appears in each position equally often. In cognitive tasks with many short trials, intermixing condition types can cancel order effects, but counterbalancing is favored when tasks are long or confusing to alternate. Some manipulations—like taking a drug or unlearning a skill—cannot be counterbalanced at all.

In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data to only one condition, so differences among individuals introduce nuisance variability that can obscure the true effect. In a within-subjects design, every participant experiences all experimental conditions and is compared to themselves across conditions. This approach removes at least some participant-related noise, increases statistical power, and requires fewer participants to achieve the same number of data points per condition.

Several main types of within-subjects designs were covered. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions within one session (for example, different levels of task difficulty). A **longitudinal design** measures the same individuals at multiple points over time—highly informative but often slow to complete. These designs reduce nuisance variability, making them efficient and powerful, but they come with specific challenges.

Potential **threats to validity** include **demand characteristics** (participants guessing the study’s purpose), **carryover effects**, and several time-related threats. Carryover effects occur when earlier conditions influence later ones—either through **fatigue** (performance declines) or **practice** (performance improves). **History effects** arise when external events occur during the study and become confounded with the manipulation (e.g., exams or major campus events). **Maturation effects** reflect natural biological or developmental changes over time, and **testing effects** occur when repeated exposure to the same test alters performance. These issues can distort comparisons if uncontrolled.

**Order effects**—changes in outcomes based solely on sequence—are a central concern. Researchers can redesign the study as a between-subjects design or apply **counterbalancing** to distribute order effects evenly. In **complete counterbalancing**, each participant experiences all possible orders. Examples include the **ABBA design** for two conditions, which controls for gradual practice effects, and **block randomization** for three or more conditions, suitable only when tasks are very short. When complete counterbalancing is impractical, **incomplete designs** such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs** ensure each condition occurs equally often in each position and follows every other condition equally often.

In **cognitive science**, experiments with many quick trials often randomize conditions to cancel order effects, though counterbalancing is preferred when items are few or complex. Finally, counterbalancing cannot undo irreversible manipulations—like drug administration, surgery, or permanent learning—setting clear **limits** on its use.  

**(Word count: 396)**Overall winner: Within-subjects designs provide an efficient way to reduce nuisance variability by testing the same participants in all conditions. In a between-groups design, each person contributes data to only one condition, and individual differences introduce variability that can obscure true effects. In contrast, within-subjects designs compare each participant to themselves across conditions, removing participant-related noise and increasing statistical power. Because each person serves as their own control, researchers can obtain the same number of data points with fewer participants.

Several common forms were discussed. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions, such as varying a perceptual difficulty level. A **longitudinal design** follows the same individuals over time, offering valuable insight but requiring patience and extended data collection. These approaches enhance precision and efficiency rather than necessarily producing “stronger” causal inferences—they must still address validity threats.

Examples illustrated the design logic. In the dyslexia **letter-spacing study**, children read texts with normal versus wide spacing. Researchers had to decide whether each child would see both conditions (a within-subjects approach) or whether different groups would see different versions (between-subjects). The study also raised questions about **complete** versus **incomplete** within-subjects structures—in which everyone gets all conditions but not all possible orders.

Potential drawbacks include **demand characteristics**, where participants may guess the purpose after multiple conditions, and **carryover effects**, where one condition influences later ones through **fatigue** or **practice**. Time-based threats include **history effects** (outside events during the study), **maturation effects** (natural developmental change), and **testing effects** (being influenced by having taken the test before). Including a control pretest–posttest group can help isolate these influences.

**Order effects**—changes in performance due solely to sequence—pose a central challenge. Researchers can counter them by running a between-subjects design or by **counterbalancing** the order of conditions. In **complete counterbalancing**, every possible order is used: the **ABBA** design controls gradual practice effects, and **block randomization** organizes multiple conditions in all possible sequences. When that is impractical, **incomplete designs** such as **random rotation** or a **Latin-square design** ensure each condition appears in each position equally often. In cognitive tasks with many short trials, intermixing condition types can cancel order effects, but counterbalancing is favored when tasks are long or confusing to alternate. Some manipulations—like taking a drug or unlearning a skill—cannot be counterbalanced at all.
