In between-groups designs, each participant contributes data under only one condition, making individual differences a major source of variability. In contrast, within-subjects designs test each participant in all conditions, comparing individuals to themselves. This approach reduces participant-related noise and increases statistical power while requiring fewer participants.

Pretest-posttest designs measure participants before and after an intervention; repeated-measures designs involve multiple conditions within the same session; and longitudinal designs track change across extended time periods. Key benefits include reduced nuisance variability and improved efficiency. However, these advantages come with potential challenges. Participants may deduce the study’s purpose (demand characteristics) or experience carryover effects, where performance in one condition influences subsequent performance. Carryover effects include fatigue (declining performance), practice (improvement over time), history (external events), maturation (developmental or aging changes), and testing effects (familiarity with the test).

Overall, within-subjects designs provide efficient, powerful means to detect effects while demanding careful control of order and carryover influences.Overall winner: This lecture introduces within-subjects designs as a methodological counterpart to between-groups designs. In between-groups designs, each participant contributes data in only one condition, which makes it difficult to separate true treatment effects from individual differences. Within-subjects designs, by contrast, test each participant in all conditions, allowing researchers to compare each person to themselves. This design reduces participant-related noise, increases statistical power, and requires fewer participants to achieve the same number of data points per condition.

Several specific types of within-subjects designs were discussed. A **pretest–posttest design** includes two conditions: a pretest, an intervention, and a posttest. A **repeated-measures design** involves two or more conditions within a single session, such as testing performance at multiple stimulus levels. A **longitudinal design** tracks participants’ responses over extended time periods and can reveal developmental or long-term change, though it requires significant time investment. The main benefits of these approaches include efficiency (fewer participants needed) and reduced nuisance variability, yielding more reliable detection of effects.

However, within-subjects designs also present challenges. **Demand characteristics** can occur when participants experience all conditions and deduce the study’s purpose. **Carryover effects** happen when performance in one condition influences later conditions—through **fatigue** (decline from tiredness) or **practice** (improvement from learning). In addition to carryover issues, several **other concerns** can threaten validity: the **history effect** (external events coinciding with parts of the study), **maturation effect** (changes due to aging or passage of time rather than the manipulation), and **testing effect** (improvement or bias from repeated testing).

A further issue is **order effects**, when condition sequence alters outcomes. To control for these, researchers use **counterbalancing**, varying orders systematically or randomly across participants. **Complete counterbalancing** methods like ABBA sequences and block randomization ensure every possible order is experienced but become impractical with many conditions. **Incomplete counterbalancing**—such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs**—ensures each condition appears equally often in each position and follows others evenly, without requiring all possible sequences.

In cognitive science research, when tasks involve many short trials, investigators often intermix conditions randomly to minimize order effects unless doing so would confuse participants. Nonetheless, counterbalancing has limits; it cannot undo irreversible manipulations such as drug administration, surgery, or unique experiences. Overall, within-subjects designs offer statistically powerful, efficient tools for comparing conditions, provided researchers account for order and carryover influences through thoughtful experimental design.  
*(~395 words)*Overall winner: The lecture focuses on **within-subjects designs** as a methodological alternative to between-groups designs. In between-groups designs, each participant takes part in only one condition, so individual differences across participants add nuisance variability. Within-subjects designs address this by having each participant experience all conditions and be compared to themselves across those conditions. This approach removes participant-related noise, allowing researchers to detect effects more easily and often with fewer participants.

Several specific within-subjects approaches are highlighted. The **pretest–posttest design** involves two measurements—before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** includes two or more conditions tested within the same group, such as varying stimulus difficulty levels. A **longitudinal design** tracks the same participants over extended time spans, offering rich developmental insights but taking considerably longer to complete. Collectively, these designs reduce nuisance variability and provide more powerful tests, though they present unique complications.

Potential problems include **demand characteristics**, where participants who receive all conditions might guess the experiment’s purpose, and **carryover effects**, where one condition influences later performance due to fatigue, practice, or learning. The lecture distinguishes **history**, **maturation**, and **testing effects** as additional threats to validity: history effects arise from external events during data collection; maturation effects reflect changes due to time or biological development; and testing effects result from familiarity with the same assessment over repeated administrations.

**Order effects**—when the sequence of conditions alters performance—pose another challenge in within-subjects research. To control for these, researchers use **counterbalancing**, systematically varying or randomizing condition orders. Counterbalancing can take several forms. **Complete designs** include all possible orders, such as **ABBA** sequences or **block randomization**, ensuring every condition precedes and follows every other equally. When the number of conditions makes full counterbalancing impractical, **incomplete designs** like **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square arrangements** achieve balanced representation with fewer participants. These strategies can become “pretty complex pretty quickly—but they’re worth it” to achieve rigorous control.

In cognitive science experiments with many rapid trials, researchers may also **intermix conditions** randomly, which helps cancel order effects when the task allows. However, counterbalancing has limits: it cannot reverse irreversible manipulations, such as drug administration or surgery. Overall, within-subjects designs provide a powerful and efficient framework for studying effects while requiring thoughtful handling of order, carryover, and validity threats.  

**(402 words)**Overall winner: This lecture examined **within-subjects designs** as a methodological contrast to **between-groups designs**. In a between-groups design, each participant provides data in only one condition, so individual differences add noise and require larger samples. A within-subjects design instead tests every participant in all conditions, allowing each person’s performance to be compared against their own. This removes much participant-related variability, yields a more powerful statistical test, and achieves the same number of observations with fewer participants.

Several specific forms of within-subjects designs were covered. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants both before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** collects data across two or more conditions within a single session (for example, testing varying levels of random dot motion coherence). A **longitudinal design** follows the same individuals over long periods to capture developmental or temporal change. Example studies highlighted included one on children’s comprehension of speech in their own accent and another testing whether **extra letter spacing** improves reading for children with dyslexia—both illustrating when participants might experience multiple conditions versus separate groups.

The advantages of within-subjects approaches include efficiency and reduced nuisance variability. However, they also raise important validity concerns. **Demand characteristics** arise when participants experience every condition and infer the study’s purpose. **Carryover effects** occur when one condition influences later conditions—through **fatigue** (decreased performance) or **practice** (improvement through learning). Additional threats include the **history effect** (external events such as exams or major campus activities influencing results), **maturation effect** (changes from aging or passage of time rather than manipulation), and **testing effect** (performance altered by repeated exposure to the same test).

**Order effects**—when condition sequence itself changes outcomes—pose a major challenge. Researchers address these through **counterbalancing**, systematically varying condition order across participants. **Complete counterbalancing** methods such as ABBA sequences and **block randomization** give every possible order but can become impractical with many conditions. **Incomplete counterbalancing**, including **random order with rotation** and **Latin-square designs**, ensures each condition appears equally across positions and follows others evenly without testing all sequences. In cognitive science tasks with many trials, conditions may instead be randomly intermixed to cancel order effects. Yet counterbalancing has **limits of irreversibility**—some experiences, like taking a drug, undergoing surgery, or learning a technique, cannot be undone.  

*(~385 words)*Overall winner: The lecture focuses on **within-subjects designs** as a methodological alternative to between-groups designs. In between-groups designs, each participant contributes data to only one condition, and individual differences across participants add nuisance variability. Within-subjects designs address this by having each participant experience all conditions and be compared to themselves across those conditions. This approach removes *at least some* participant-related noise, enabling more powerful tests and typically requiring fewer participants to achieve the same number of data points per condition.

The lecture distinguishes among several **types of within-subjects designs**. In a **pretest–posttest design**, participants complete measures before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** involves two or more conditions—such as different stimulus difficulty levels—and a **longitudinal design** measures the same participants across extended periods, trading efficiency for valuable developmental insight. These designs effectively reduce nuisance variability but also introduce unique challenges.

Potential drawbacks include **demand characteristics**, where participants who experience all conditions may infer the study’s purpose, and **carryover effects**, where an earlier condition influences performance in later ones through fatigue, practice, or learning. Additional threats include **history effects** (outside events affecting results), **maturation effects** (changes over time or biological development), and **testing effects** (familiarity with repeated measures altering performance).

**Order effects**—differences produced by condition sequence—are a central concern. Researchers manage these through **counterbalancing**, varying or rotating condition orders to distribute these effects evenly. **Complete within-subjects designs** expose participants to all possible orders, using methods such as the **ABBA design** (for two conditions) or **block randomization** (for three or more), though these can become complex with many conditions. **Incomplete within-subjects designs**—such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs**—ensure that each condition appears equally across positions and follows every other condition equally often while using fewer participants.

In cognitive science experiments with many trials, researchers sometimes **intermix conditions** instead of counterbalancing, which can cancel order effects efficiently. However, counterbalancing remains preferable when intermixing would confuse participants or when tasks involve few or lengthy items. Finally, some manipulations cannot be counterbalanced—participants cannot “un-take” a drug or “unlearn” a skill. Overall, within-subjects designs offer a powerful and efficient means of research when paired with thoughtful control of order and carryover effects.  

**(379 words)**Overall winner: Within-subjects designs were introduced as an alternative to between-groups designs. In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data in only one condition, meaning differences among participants add unwanted variability (“noise”) that can obscure real effects. Within-subjects designs instead have each participant experience all conditions. Because each person is compared to themselves across conditions, participant-related variability is reduced, statistical power increases, and the same number of observations can be obtained with fewer participants.

Several forms of within-subjects designs were highlighted. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** exposes participants to two or more conditions (for instance, different motion coherences). A **longitudinal design** follows the same individuals over time; though informative, it requires long-term data collection. These designs share the core advantages of efficiency and lower nuisance variability, producing more sensitive tests.

The lecture emphasized that these benefits come with possible drawbacks. **Demand characteristics** can occur when participants infer the study’s purpose after receiving all conditions. **Carryover effects** also threaten validity when earlier conditions influence later performance, as with **fatigue** (decline from boredom or tiredness) or **practice** (improvement from learning the task). Beyond carryover, the lecture described additional and distinct concerns:  
- **History effects**—external events (e.g., exams, major news, campus events) that coincide with the experiment and confound results.  
- **Maturation effects**—natural developmental or aging changes (e.g., vocabulary growth, hearing loss) that might falsely appear as treatment effects.  
- **Testing effects**—improvement or strategic change from taking the same test multiple times.

**Order effects** can distort outcome patterns depending on the sequence of conditions—for example, performance improving or reversing simply because one condition always comes first. To address this, researchers use **counterbalancing**, varying the condition order to distribute order effects evenly. Complete counterbalancing methods include the **ABBA design** and **block randomization**, while **incomplete counterbalancing**—such as **random order with rotation** or **Latin-square designs**—maintains balance with fewer participants.

In cognitive science applications, when tasks involve many trials, researchers often **randomly intermixed conditions** to cancel order effects, resorting to counterbalancing when intermixing might confuse participants or tasks are lengthy. Finally, the lecture underscored **limits of counterbalancing**: some manipulations (e.g., surgery, drug administration, once-seen stimuli) cannot be undone. Overall, within-subjects designs offer a powerful, efficient approach when researchers carefully manage order, carryover, and time-related confounds.  
*(Approx. 398 words)*Overall winner: Within-subjects designs were introduced as an alternative to between-groups designs. In a between-groups design, each participant contributes data in only one condition, so individual differences add variability (“noise”) that can obscure real effects. Within-subjects designs instead have each participant experience all conditions, allowing direct comparison of each person to themselves. This approach reduces participant-related variability, increases statistical power, and yields the same number of observations with fewer participants.

Several forms of within-subjects designs were discussed. A **pretest–posttest design** measures participants before and after an intervention. A **repeated-measures design** exposes participants to multiple conditions (e.g., varying motion coherence levels). A **longitudinal design** measures the same individuals across extended time points—highly informative but time-intensive. All share the advantages of efficiency and sensitivity through reduced nuisance variability.

The lecture emphasized potential drawbacks that accompany these advantages. **Demand characteristics** can emerge when participants exposed to all conditions guess the study’s purpose. **Carryover effects**, in which earlier conditions influence later ones, pose additional threats: **fatigue effects** (worse performance from tiredness or boredom) and **practice effects** (improvement due to learning the task). Other confounding influences include:  
- **History effects**—external events (e.g., exams, campus celebrations, world events) that coincide with the study and confound results.  
- **Maturation effects**—changes due to natural development or aging (e.g., vocabulary gains, hearing decline) rather than the manipulation.  
- **Testing effects**—participants improving or adjusting because they have taken the same test before.

**Order effects** occur when performance differences depend on the sequence of conditions rather than the manipulation itself. To address these, researchers use **counterbalancing**, distributing condition orders across participants. Complete counterbalancing methods include the **ABBA design** (for two conditions) and **block randomization** (for three or more). **Incomplete counterbalancing**—such as **random order with rotation** or a **Latin-square design**—achieves balanced exposure with fewer participants. The lecture also illustrated a developmental example comparing **4‑year‑olds and 7‑year‑olds** understanding speech in their own versus other accents, highlighting how design choice affects interpretation.

In cognitive science tasks with many trials, researchers often **randomly intermix conditions** to minimize order effects, though counterbalancing is preferable when intermixing would confuse participants or items are lengthy. Finally, some interventions (e.g., surgery, drug administration, or once-seen stimuli) cannot be reversed, defining the **limits of counterbalancing**. Overall, within-subjects designs offer a statistically powerful, efficient framework when researchers carefully manage order, carryover, and time-related confounds.
